While a national currency could become unstable by losing its purchasing power domestically due to high inflation rates, it could – at the same time – have much higher purchasing power in another country with relatively higher inflation rates. In the reality of free global trade, inflation refers to the rate of exchange of a currency in purchasing power, and a currency is not necessarily inflationary or deflationary just because the supply of a currency “goes up” or “goes down." Unfortunately these concepts are often oversimplified in the crypto space.  
       
       
         In Web3, a new type of non-state actor in the form of a Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAO) has emerged – which can be considered a Web3-based “ 
         
           network state 
         
         .” 
         
           
             
               15 
             
           
         
          These new types of non-state actors have their own internal currencies as well as an entire ecosystem of economic services that build on these network states. This new type of non-state actor is an addition to the global interplay of currency, exchange rate and inflation, and generally adds to existing complexities, both in foreign exchange markets in particular and the financial landscape in general.  
       
       
         
         
         Fiat-Collateralized & Commodity-Collateralized Stable Tokens 
       
       
         A simple way to achieve a token with a stable value is to back the token with a reserve of assets of the real economy that have relatively stable prices. Fiat currencies or certain types of commodities are examples for such assets: “ 
         
           Tether 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           TrueUSD 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           USDC 
         
         ” were early examples of stable tokens that were backed by the U.S. Dollar; “ 
         
           Digix Gold 
         
         ” was an early example of a token that was backed by gold. But on global asset markets, absolute price stability in relation to other currencies can never exist. In order to hedge against potential exchange rate volatilities or inflation risk, the concept of backing a stable token with a basket of commodities, basket of fiat currency, or an index such as the CPI emerged over time.  
       
       
         Fiat-collateralized stable tokens are relatively easy to set up, but they have a centralization problem. This is also why some refer to them as “centralized” stable tokens. An institution – usually operated by a private company – needs to manage the physical assets that act as collateral of the stable token, usually in a bank account or secure vault. Trust is outsourced to a single entity, which guarantees that the amount of stable tokens issued corresponds to the amount of actual assets held in a secure vault as collateral, making the stable token system subject to so-called “ 
         
           counterparty risk 
         
         .” 
         
           
             
               16 
             
           
         
          Such a risk could be mitigated if a system of hardware and software oracles are programmed to feed the token contracts with data from  
         
           Internet-of-Things 
         
          (IoT) devices, such as video cameras that monitor the collateral held in the vaults. This would be the only way to create publicly verifiable and real-time proofs about the state of the collaterals managed by a trusted institution. While this is technically possible, it would require that all IoT devices come equipped with a hardware wallet, in order to provide the necessary security and privacy architecture on a native Web3 level. At the time of writing this book, most fiat-collateralized stable tokens still predominantly rely on trusting a centralized service provider and a handful of auditors who guarantee that the collateral assets are where they are supposed to be. Auditors, however, don’t check the amount of collateral held in real time, but only periodically. The auditing process is often opaque and can involve higher fees and delays. 
       
       
         
           Tether (USDT) 
         
          is one of the more established and popular stable tokens. It was founded in 2014 and was originally called Realcoin. USDT has a 1:1 peg against the U.S. Dollar (USD), which means that for every USDT in circulation, one USD (or an equivalent asset) is added to a centrally managed secure storage as collateral. At the time of writing this book, the majority of Tether reserves are diversified across cash, cash equivalent, and other short-term deposits and commercial paper, corporate bonds, precious metals, secured loans, and other investments. Apart from being an early mover in the space, one of Tether’s success factors has been that USDT can be issued on various blockchain networks which makes the stable token interoperable with many DeFi protocols. However, Tether is managed by a private company, and for a long time failed to undergo a full auditing process in order to prove that they actually held in reserve the full collateral. Many doubted that Tether was fully collateralized, and US authorities investigated the case. In 2020 Tether changed their terms, admitting that some of their reserves might have been used for loans to other entities, such as token exchanges. Their general counsel admitted that only 74% of USDT was backed by cash and cash equivalents. This was quite critical, as USDT was one of the top traded tokens and a significant amount of BTC trading resulted from Tether. Many were worried that if allegations were true, fake Tethers could be used to buy BTC, driving up the market price and resulting in market manipulation, and potential short-term price collapse of BTC. In 2021, Tether was fined 41 million USD for misrepresenting the amount of reserve assets held. To provide more transparency on their collateral assets, Tether started to publish reports more frequently – first on a quarterly and now on a monthly basis. These reports, however, are conducted in the form of third party attestation by specialized auditing companies.  
       
       
         
           Circle (USDC) 
         
          was founded in 2013 and is another example of a stable token that is pegged to the U.S. Dollar. USDC was originally issued on the Ethereum network and could only be used on applications building on the Ethereum network or other EVM-compatible blockchain networks. In 2022, they introduced multi-blockchain capabilities and their own interoperability tool called “Cross-Chain Transfer Protocol” in order to be able to compete with Tether’s multi blockchain capabilities. Just as USDT, USDC has a 1:1 peg against USD and is backed by USD-denominated assets that are held in segregated accounts with U.S.-regulated financial institutions that partner with Circle. USDC is a collaboration between Coinbase (a U.S.-based centralized token exchange) and Circle (a U.S.-based licensed money transmitter backed by Goldman Sachs) managed by the Centre Consortium. It is perceived by some to be a safer stable token than USDT, since the issuer – Centre – has more transparent reserves that are fully-backed, and it is perceived to comply with audits and governmental regulation. Circle eventually also issued a Euro-backed stable token called EUROC with the same reserve model as USDC. EUROC adopts the same full-reserve model as USDC, with each token backed by Euros held in Euro-denominated bank accounts, ensuring their ability to be redeemed.  
       
       
         
           TrueUSD (TUSD) 
         
          is another USD-pegged token. For a while it appeared to be more trustworthy than USDT, as they regularly published independent audits to verify their funding claims. But USDC eventually caught up with them on the trustworthiness front and gained market share. 
       
       
         
           Binance USD (BUSD) 
         
          is also a 1:1 USD-backed stable token that is issued by the token exchange Binance in partnership with Paxos (blockchain infrastructure). BUSD seems to have been approved by the New York State Department of Financial Services (NYDFS) and issues monthly audit reports. As one of the biggest token exchanges worldwide, Binance is one of the most important market makers in crypto and therefore has a competitive advantage as it can distribute BUSD on their own exchange and integrate it with other DeFi services building on their exchange. 
       
       
         While USD tokens are pegged to the value of the U.S. Dollar, they are not necessarily only backed by cash reserves. The array of reserves backing the value of an asset collateralized stable token can also contain other securities such as short dated government bonds. The same is true for stable tokens pegged to other fiat currencies such as the Euro (such as  
         
           EURS 
         
         ,  
         
           EUROC 
         
         ) or stable tokens that are pegged to other currencies, such as the Singapore Dollar in the case of  
         
           StraitsX Singapore Dollar (XSGD) 
         
         . 
       
       
         When it comes to total market share, USD-denominated stable tokens make up more than 98% of the total volume of all stable tokens in circulation, emulating traditional financial markets, where the U.S. Dollar serves as the world's reserve and denomination currency. With the popularity of DeFi protocols, the demand for USD-denominated stable tokens has also increased and there seem to be strong network effects in place to favor USD-based stable tokens. Decentralized exchanges incentivize stable token holders to provide market liquidity through high interest rates on stable tokens deposited in so-called “liquidity pools.” DeFi derivatives also rely on USD-denominated stable tokens as their settlement currency. But there are also other reasons why there are less stable tokens pegged to other currencies. In the European Union, stable tokens are regulated under the  
         
           MiCA framework 
         
         , 
         
           
             
               17 
             
           
         
          which classifies stable tokens as e-money and requires any issuer to have an e-money license. Circle has been able to circumvent these regulatory constraints by issuing EUROC via a U.S.-based bank that has no legal entity residing within Europe. However, it seems unlikely that their workaround will be sustainable in the long run. Furthermore, EUR- denominated assets suffered from negative interest rates in recent years, which made it unattractive to use EUR as a collateral asset. A change of macroeconomic environment, such as positive interest rates, would make EUR-denominated stable tokens profitable and therefore much more feasible.  
       
       
         The regulatory constraints on setting up Euro-backed stable tokens, and the first-mover advantage of USDT and USDC, have led to considerable network effects which have made USD-denominated stable tokens more popular. Stable tokens were initially a crucial tool for isolating oneself from the price volatility of traditional cryptocurrencies. Once USD-denominated stable tokens provided a reliable medium of exchange in Web3, other denominations became less attractive. In DeFi trading, pairs of currencies are essential when it comes to understanding market liquidity for specific asset classes. Splitting asset denominations into USD, EUR, and other currencies means that one also needs to split the liquidity for those trading pairs. As will be discussed in the chapter on token exchanges and tokenized credit and lending, liquidity is a main issue especially in novel DeFi products with little market adoption. Bootstrapping liquidity is already a challenge – even when using liquidity pools – which is why Binance, for example, has combined all USD stable tokens on their platform into their own USD-based stable token, BUSD. While this raises centralization concerns, it does make sense from a liquidity perspective of financial markets. 
       
       
         
           Digix Gold Token (DGX) 
         
          was a stable token that was pegged to the gold price and held gold as collateral to maintain the peg. It went live in 2018. However, as of September 2022, DGX started to close down all business activities. The project is still worth mentioning, since they were the first gold-backed token that scaled, and also for the fact that DGX partially automated the verification process of their collateral assets, which differed significantly from the traditional audit process of most other asset-backed tokens. The project founders developed a partially on-chain auditing process, where the custodians and auditors time-stamped the reports on Ethereum's ledger to provide publicly verifiable evidence that the gold was really held in the vaults managed by a custodian company in Singapore. The scans of the paperwork were uploaded to  
         
           IPFS 
         
         , a decentralized file storage network, and the hashes of these scans were recorded on the Ethereum network, creating an on-chain audit trail ( 
         
           Proof-of-Provenance protocol 
         
         ). DGX token holders had to pay a management fee to cover the costs of securely storing the gold in the vaults. Digix was also one of the first DeFi protocols organized as a DAO (Decentralized Autonomous Organization). Managerial questions around the governance of the DGX token were decentralized and performed by DigixDAO. The DigixDAO token (DGD) granted voting rights for the governance of the Digix system.  
       
       
         Many other gold-backed tokens have been issued over time, which are managed by different companies, consortia, and sometimes DAOs. Selected examples are: “ 
         
           Anthem Gold 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           AurusGOLD 
         
         ” (AWG), “ 
         
           Meta Gold 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           PAX Gold 
         
         ” (PAXG), “ 
         
           Perth Mint Gold Token 
         
         ” (PMGT), “ 
         
           Tether Gold 
         
         .” “ 
         
           Cash Telex 
         
         ” is an example of a stable token pegged to a basket of precious metals and other assets such as gold, silver, diamonds, and real estate. While all the tokens mentioned are similar in their pegging dynamics, they vary in market depth, liquidity, and trustworthiness of the institutions safeguarding the collaterals. 
       
       
         
           Globcoin 
         
          (GLX) was an early private stable token initiative launched in 2016, backed by currencies of the fifteen largest economies in addition to gold. Even though the project is not operational anymore, it is worth mentioning, as it attempted to create a tokenized version of a currency basket which the founders had developed – the GRCI (Global Reserve Currency Index). This was at a time when most projects were still trying to define stability by pegging a stable token to one single national currency and also collateralizing it with that one currency, making the stable token subject to exchange rate fluctuations. Globcoin’s reserve diversification covered 85% of the global economy, acting as a "global coin." The aim of the founders was to create a fully-backed asset token with reserves that were weighted according to indices such as  
         
           purchasing power parity 
         
         -adjusted 
         
           
             
               18 
             
           
         
           
         
           GDP 
         
         
           
             
               19 
             
           
         
          data of the fifteen largest economies. 
       
       
         
           Diem (formerly Libra 
         
         ) was intended to be an asset-collateralized stable token for cross-border online payments. It was announced in 2019 by a consortium of companies and NGOs, spearheaded by Facebook (now Meta). The consortium intended to operate the stable tokens on their own federated distributed ledger network. The tokens were designed to be issued and burned on a regular basis, to respond to demand shifts for its reserve and keep the exchange rate stable. The consortium was planning to back the stable token with a basket of currencies and other assets, but quickly ran into opposition from policy-makers in the US and worldwide. Initially the Libra Association, headquartered in Switzerland, had around 30 founding members, all established institutions ranging from traditional payment networks (Mastercard, Visa, Paypal) to Internet companies (Uber, Lyft, eBay) to blockchain companies (Xapo), Venture Capital companies (Thrive Capital, Andreessen Horowitz), and NGOs, such as Women’s World Banking and Mercy Corps. The founding members were supposed to hold a second set of tokens that granted voting rights on governance changes of the protocol of their federated blockchain network. The announcement was a black swan event that brought global attention to Web3 applications – with a Web2 giant such as Facebook and established financial institutions wanting to move into Web3. While the announcements of the stable token created a big media hype in 2019, it was not welcomed by most regulators worldwide and spurred considerable regulatory pushback. It seems that a private company such as Facebook – with more than 2 billion users – was more of a threat for global governments than Bitcoin. In October 2019, PayPal, Visa, MasterCard, and Stripe dropped their support for the project. Even Facebook announced that they would drop the development of the project if it failed to “make sufficient headway” with regulators, who feared that the Libra token could be used for (i) illegal activities, (ii) become a shadow bank, (iii) privatize money, and also (iv) undermine user privacy. Facebook first changed and rebranded their activities to Diem, but operations eventually fully stopped in early 2022 and all assets were sold. In spite of the fact that the Libra project never materialized, the playground for supernerds, crypto-anarchists, and speculators took a big step toward tokenizing larger parts of the monetary system (more on this in the section on CBDCs later on in this chapter). 
       
       
         
           Celo 
         
         : Many members of the Libra federation, such as Anchorage, Bison Trails, Coinbase Ventures, Andreessen Horowitz, and Mercy Corps joined the “Celo Alliance” in 2020. With 50 founding members, Celo is similar to the erstwhile Libra consortium, with a token called “Celo Dollar.” The Celo protocol has various token types. (i) The Celo token (CELO) is the protocol token of the underlying blockchain network (Celo network) which serves as a medium of payment for network transaction fees and includes governance rights; (ii) Celo also has three platform-native stablecoins: Celo Dollars (cUSD), Celo Euros (cEUR), and Celo Real (cREAL) which are settled via the Celo blockchain network, that is built on the Ethereum codebase and EVM compatible. To simplify the process of sending funds, transaction fees in the Celo protocol can be paid with any fungible token that was issued on the Ethereum network. Transaction fees paid out of non-Celo currencies incur a slightly higher fee. 
       
         
       
         
         
         Crypto-Collateralized Stable Tokens 
       
       
         One way to mitigate the centralization problem of stable tokens that are backed by real-word assets is to collateralize a stable token with a native crypto token such as ETH, which is why these stable tokens are sometimes also referred to as “crypto-collateralized” or “decentralized.” In such a setup, the stable token is collateralized with crypto-native assets such as ETH instead of a fiat currency or other real-world asset. The advantage of Web3-native collateral assets is that they can be managed entirely on-chain, and are audited by a publicly verifiable infrastructure – a blockchain network – instead of a third party who would need to hold physical collateral assets in secure vaults. The public and verifiable nature of native crypto tokens makes these stable token systems more trustworthy –at least from a Web3 perspective – as it mitigates the centralization problem and the risk of under-collateralization. BitUSD was the project that introduced this stable token model back in 2013. It inspired many other projects, such as “ 
         
           MakerDAO 
         
         ” (DAI), which has become the most dominant crypto-collateralized stable token on the market. At the time of writing this book, other tokens of its kind – such as “ 
         
           Liquity 
         
         ” (LUSD), “ 
         
           Reflexer 
         
         ” (RAI), “Decentralized USD” ( 
         
           USDD 
         
         ) or “Dark Dollar” ( 
         
           DUSD 
         
         ) – don’t even come close to being a serious competitor to DAI in terms of trust, market cap, usage, and reputation. 
       
       
         
           DAI 
         
          is a stable token operated by MakerDAO which was conceptualized in 2014, and first launched in 2017 after a long development phase. The stable token is pegged 1:1 to the U.S. Dollar. The collaterals used to back the stability mechanism are predominantly crypto-native assets. DAI was initially only backed by a reserve of ETH, which was held as collateral in a smart contract. In 2019, DAI started to use a basket of crypto-native tokens as collateral, to mitigate the volatility risks associated with a single asset such as ETH. MakerDAO later also started to include other stable tokens such as USDC as well as real-world assets into their mix of collaterals. Anyone can deposit ETH or other accepted crypto collaterals in a smart contract and take out a loan of DAI tokens in exchange, for which periodic interest (aka stability fee) has to be paid. This fee is used by MakerDAO – DAI’s governing entity – to maintain the operation of the DAI stable token and to pay for macroeconomic policy interventions to maintain the peg of DAI. The smart contract locks the deposited collateral tokens and generates DAI tokens, which results in a debt position for DAI token holders once the DAI stable tokens are withdrawn from the smart contract. This is why the smart contract was originally referred to as a “collateralized debt position” (CDP). Since the transition to multi-collateral DAI in 2019, the smart contracts are called “vaults.” Once a DAI lender pays back their DAI debt, the collateral tokens are automatically released by the vault, and the DAI tokens are in turn destroyed (i.e., burnt). This way, the stable token is always collateralized with real values, and not created out of thin air. However, the great challenge of crypto-collateralized stable tokens is that they are prone to the price volatility of native crypto assets. This volatility makes it harder to maintain the 1:1 peg of a crypto-collateralized stable token to a fiat currency, because the reserve assets (ETH and other crypto-native assets) have a different volatility than the asset the stability mechanisms is pegged to (in this case, USD). DAI therefore started out with a 150% collateral-to-debt ratio, to make sure that the system had enough excess collateral to mitigate the volatility risk. This collateral-to-debt ratio is also referred to as “liquidation ratio” or “minimum collateral.” The MakerDAO protocol has assigned a different collateral-to-debt ratio to each collateral asset type, depending on the volatility risk of the underlying collateral asset. Let's assume the case of ETH as a collateral: If the value of the collateral asset drops too quickly, DAI could become under-collateralized. When the individual vault drops below the required collateral-to-debt ratio, the smart contract can auto-liquidate the collateral tokens to offset the risk of non-payment. To make sure that the required surplus of collateral exists at all times, the protocol has macroeconomic policies encoded into smart contracts: they incentivize so-called “Keepers” with arbitrage opportunities to maintain a constant watch over vaults and prevent them from becoming under-collateralized. Without this provision, a significant price crash of the collateral token below the peg could make DAI token holders lose all their funds. 
         
             
         
         DAI has more complex mechanisms in place to protect against price fluctuations. The monetary policy of DAI is collectively governed by all token holders of the MakerDAO (MKR). This means that all monetary decisions must pass a vote before they get implemented. While such a democratic process mitigates many third party risks, it is also slow and faces participation problems, since many token holders lack the mental bandwidth to participate in every decision. In spite of many challenges that DAI has faced since its launch, DAI is still a landmark use case for crypto-collateralized stable tokens and has developed complementary DeFi products over time. The DAI Savings Rate (DSR) is an example of such an additional service, which makes it possible to earn periodic interest by locking DAI tokens into a savings contract. The interest rate and other macroeconomic parameters that influence demand for DAI, are decided by vote from all MKR token holders. MakerDAO has constantly evolved, branched out to different asset types, and developed new collateralization mechanisms, collateral assets, as well as governance mechanisms. In fact, at the time of writing the book, USDC, which is an asset-backed stable token operated by a centralized institution, represents over 50% of the asset basket collateralizing DAI. Critics argue this undermines the original decentralization efforts of DAI. USDC as a collateral type was introduced in the wake of what is now referred to as “ 
         
           Black Thursday 
         
         ,” where due to a rapid plunge of stock prices and crypto prices triggered by the COVID pandemic, a 90% decrease of ETH price destabilized the collateral backing of DAI. In 2022, MakerDAO holders furthermore voted to approve the creation of a real-world asset vault dedicated to Société Générale – one of the biggest French banks and a leading investment bank. Société Générale will be able to borrow up to 30 million DAI by depositing so-called OFH tokens as collateral. These OFH tokens are issued by Société Générale and are supposedly supported by corporate obligations as well as a pool of French home loans. This is quite remarkable, as it means that in spite of the early stage and risk-prone nature of stable tokens, classic financial institutions are interacting with and taking loans from DAOs. More recently, MKR token holders have voted to deposit a part of their collateral tokens with cryptocurrency exchange Coinbase, where the collateral tokens can yield interest, which means that a part of the collateral tokens of DAI are invested in short-term bonds. A more in-depth analysis of MakerDAO and DAI can be found in another book of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.” 
       
       
         
           Reflexer (RAI) 
         
          is a crypto-collateralized stable token similar to DAI, but it does not have a 1:1 peg to the USD (hard peg). Instead, the price of RAI in USD is designed to have low volatility (some kind of soft peg). Depending on supply and demand, it fluctuates around a target range, which is referred to as the “redemption price,” allowing for regulated volatility. The protocol is designed to balance the tradeoffs between maintaining a relatively hard peg at any costs and offering investors relatively high levels of stability compared to other crypto asset classes. At the time of writing, the stable token is operated on the Ethereum network and only ETH can be used as collateral. By only accepting ETH, the protocol avoids the issue of centralization of underlying collateral assets such as USDC, which has become the dominant collateral asset for DAI. Similar to DAI, users deposit ETH in a smart contract called “safe,” from which they can borrow newly minted RAI tokens, which in turn increases the overall supply of RAI. Equivalently, RAI is burnt when lenders pay back their RAI and withdraw their collateral tokens. RAI was launched in 2020 by Reflexer Labs, who forked the codebase of MakerDAO and added some new macroeconomic policy instruments, such as negative interest rates, the “PID controller,” alternative forms of liquidation protection, and new auction mechanisms to keep the price within a relatively stable range. In terms of market capitalization, it is one of the smaller stable token projects. The price of RAI has hovered between 2.5 and 3 USD. FLX is the so-called “(un)governance” token for RAI through which different macroeconomic parameters are collectively voted on – though the aim of the protocol founder is to keep human intervention lean. The system was designed on the assumption that building decentralized stable assets requires the reduction of external dependencies on centralized services and making the protocol less prone to regulatory risk such as the “Stable Act” recently proposed by US authorities. Advocates of RAI like the innovation and the decentralization efforts; critics argue that the relative complexity of the protocol makes it difficult for people to participate as stakeholders in the system and for other financial institutions – centralized or decentralized – to integrate RAI. 
       
       
         
           Liquity (LUSD) 
         
          is a stable token with a hard peg to the U.S. Dollar. Similar to RAI, they only accept ETH as collateral to a smart contract to avoid centralization problems of DAI. As opposed to MakerDAO (DAI) or RAI, the macroeconomic policies cannot be adjusted by the token holders of a governance DAO. They are instead defined as fixed parameters in a smart contract, which ensures that the protocol works without human intervention. LUSD has an alternative instant liquidation mechanism to DAI or RAI. The protocol is designed to have a 0% interest rate for LUSD borrowers, and the minimum collateral ratio is comparably low (110% at the time of writing this book). LUSD was first and foremost designed to be a DeFi protocol offering liquidity services for ETH loans and to facilitate payment for those loans, not general payments. The economics behind the stable token design have to be understood from a liquidity provider perspective. Liquidity pools are discussed in later chapters of this book.  
       
       
         
           Nuon (NUON) 
         
          is an example of a crypto-collateralized stable token that is pegged to an inflation-adjusted USD to ensure that prices for goods and services are not affected by inflation when paying with the stable token. Instead of being pegged to the nominal value of USD, Nuon developed the “ 
         
           Truflation 
         
         ” algorithm, which adjusts to daily inflation rates by tracking price movements of around ten million goods and additional inflation data. They use the catchy term “flatcoin.” Like other crypto-collateralized stable tokens, the protocol incentivizes traders with arbitrage opportunities to maintain the peg. The token is overcollateralized and designed to automatically liquidate collateralized assets if the value of their collateral falls below a given level. Index-based stable tokens are not new, but they were usually fiat-collateralized. Global inflation rate hikes in 2022 have made inflation-resilient stable tokens more attractive. Financial service providers, such as “ 
         
           Coinbase 
         
         ,”also started to develop an inflation-pegged token. 
       
       
         One of the greatest challenges of crypto-collateralized stable tokens is the necessary over-collateralization in order to mitigate price volatilities of the collateral assets, which also reduces so-called “capital efficiency.” The term “capital efficiency” in the context of stable tokens refers to the question of how much capital in terms of collateral tokens is required to create one unit worth of tokens. The current financial system does not require these collateralization ratios, as the identity of the lenders and their credit history is always known, and in the case of default, there are legal measures to enforce repayment, such as seizing homes or wages. Many economists have warned that if price volatility of the underlying asset is too high, crypto-collateralized solutions are susceptible to black swan events. DAI and other crypto-collateralized stable tokens can end up under-collateralized if a crash is rapid enough that the system is unable to close enough CDPs, and buy back their DAI, before overall system collateralization drops below 100%. Another challenge is the lack of  
         
           oracle services 
         
         
           
             
               20 
             
           
         
          that deliver reliable price-feeds about asset prices across exchanges. Currently, a patchwork of centralized solutions are used, but they are prone to manipulation and attack. (I will go deeper into the topic of oracles in another book of the Token Economy Series titled “Web3 Infrastructure.”) 
       
       
         
         
         Algorithmic Stable Tokens 
       
       
         Stable tokens that are pegged to assets using legacy financial services might seem simple and obvious at first sight, but could also be perceived as somewhat contrary to the spirit of the underlying decentralized technology. This is similar to how Yahoo and many other search engines of the early 1990s tried to make Internet content retrievable by cataloging websites, just as one would catalog books or magazines in a physical library. While these types of search engines were popular and intuitive in the early days of the Web, they were not scalable at all, nor did they reflect the algorithmic potential of the underlying technology. Eventually, algorithmic search as offered by Google and other “search engines,” replaced the manual cataloging of an exponentially growing body of web content.  
       
       
         While asset-backed stable tokens (be it fiat-collateralized or crypto-collateralized) might seem tempting at first, a range of algorithmic solutions are on the rise that could possibly do more justice to the nature of smart contracts and Web3. Algorithmic stable tokens use an adaptive supply and demand mechanism, which is encoded into the token contract. The mechanism incentivizes the behavior of market participants. One of the earliest attempts to create an algorithmic stable token was introduced by DAI, but DAI always used collaterals, specifically over-collateralization. Algorithmic stable tokens are often under-collateralized or, in their purest form, not collateralized at all. Completely uncollateralized stable tokens are not backed by any external asset but only by an algorithmic supply and demand policy that is designed to reduce the price volatility of the token in question by offering economic incentives in the form of arbitrage 
         
           
             
               21 
             
           
         
          opportunities to market participants that collectively regulate the stable token price. The peg could be anything from a fiat currency, a basket of goods and services, to a suitable economic indicator.  
       
       
         Elastic supply mechanisms are designed to either stimulate expansion or contraction of the supply of the stable token in question, similar to how central banks control the supply of fiat currencies. If the demand for stable tokens rises or falls, the algorithm automatically adjusts to keep a stable price. If the price is too high, the mechanism will increase the supply. If the price is too low, tokens need to be “frozen” in one way or another. The question of how to increase and decrease token supply in an attack-resistant and resilient way has not been conclusively resolved. Depending on the mechanism, different algorithmic methods are used for expansion and contraction: (i) Rebase mechanisms; (ii) Seigniorage mechanisms; (iii) Fractional-reserve mechanisms. To understand algorithmic stable token design in detail, one needs to have a base understanding of macroeconomics (in particular monetary policy), financial markets, mathematics, and the technological building blocks of DeFi products and their interactions. Potential investors need to understand the level of risk involved when they put their money into such tokens. While algorithmic solutions are still experimental technology, very risky, and highly disputed, they pursue an important experiment nonetheless.  
       
       
         Rebase mechanism 
         : The primary mechanism behind rebase tokens is the adjustment of token supply. Each time the stable token price is over its target peg, the smart contract will mint more tokens. Conversely, the protocol will burn tokens to remove them from circulation if the stable token price drops below the target price. This algorithm is designed to act "globally" across the network of all stable token holders, which means that the burning and minting of tokens will change the balance across the wallets of all stable token holders. The number of tokens in one's wallet will therefore vary based on the weighted average of the stable token price over a specific time period (e.g. 12 hours). Each time the stable token price increases over its target peg, the token contract will release more stable tokens (inflation). Correspondingly, the mechanism is designed so that the smart contract will remove a certain percentage of stable tokens from circulation when the stable token price drops below the target peg (deflation). This type of inflation and deflation affects the token balance of each token holder across the network in proportion to their token holdings, which is why it is referred to as “rebasing.” The rebase mechanism automatically increases or decreases the token balance of each token holder according to the algorithmic monetary policy encoded in the token contract. One current example for a stable token with a rebase mechanism is “ 
         
           Ampleforth 
         
         .” “ 
         
           BASED 
         
         ” or the “ 
         
           Base Protocol 
         
         ” are examples for stable tokens that ceased operations after a short life span. Not all rebase tokens aim to maintain a completely stable price. Ampleforth, for example, claims on their website to be “synthetic commodity money” and therefore takes into account a certain level of price fluctuation. Their target peg is not a fiat currency, but the consumer price index (CPI) of the U.S. Dollar.  
       
       
         Seigniorage mechanism aka multi-token mechanism 
         : “ 
         
           Seigniorage 
         
         
           
             
               22 
             
           
         
          shares” are a concept for an algorithmic stable token that was first proposed by Robert Sams in 2014. Seigniorage mechanisms don’t use collateral at all and consist of at least two token types that are native to the stable token, without the need for external collateral tokens. The system is set up to be “self-collateralizing.” In addition to the stable token issued, a second token (also referred to as balancer token, seigniorage token, or share token) is issued with the purpose to absorb the volatility of the stable token. This is achieved by managing the total supply of the secondary token. At least one other token is necessary to facilitate the stability mechanism in the absence of a collateral asset; some projects even have several tokens to achieve the absorption. The secondary token mechanisms are designed with the purpose to encourage market participants to buy or sell the secondary tokens in order to maintain the price peg of the stable token. When the price of a stable token exceeds the intended peg, the secondary tokens are utilized to increase the supply of the stable token. As opposed to rebase mechanisms, the smart contract does not destroy circulating tokens that belong to someone. The smart contract is instead designed to buy stable tokens from other market participants to reduce the circulating supply, and increase the price. While the stable token supply can be easily expanded by minting new secondary tokens, contracting token supply requires more sophisticated mechanisms. The biggest question in this context is: Why should token holders agree to sell tokens, and how can they be meaningfully incentivized to do so? If the smart contract does not have enough newly minted tokens, it could issue  
         
           bonds 
         
          in exchange for a stable token in proportion to the tokens that need to be destroyed. These bonds are sold at a discount and can be paid out at a future date, with the bond holders being the first to be paid out. The discount serves as an incentive for holders to remove their stable tokens from circulation. While the idea of using smart contracts to replace certain functions of a central bank is intriguing, some mechanisms rely on partially unproven economic assumptions and untested monetary policies, especially around incentive design for contracting cycles. In some cases, stabilization is still partially maintained via centralized mechanisms. Furthermore, the challenge of decentralized and trustless price oracles has to be resolved. Many economists therefore predicted that algorithmic stable tokens wouldn't work, as most designs presented assumed unlimited growth of the system. Each contraction cycle initiates the potential for a future increase in the total supply of the stable token, as certain events could trigger death spirals in the price of bonds, increase the time to payout, and decrease the odds that each bond is paid, which could lead to a recursive feedback loop, and ultimately undermine the aim of supply contraction on a large scale. As a solution to this problem, some projects allow token holders to temporarily freeze tokens; other projects issue bonds that expire after a certain time. An early example of an algorithmic stable token was “ 
         
           NuBits 
         
         ,” which was launched in 2014. NuBits lost its peg to the U.S. Dollar for the first time in 2016, which they temporarily managed to recover, only to lose it again in 2018. In May 2022, a much-hyped algorithmic stable token project – “Terra(LUNA)” – collapsed, 
         
           
             
               23 
             
           
         
          and created a negative domino effect on many interacting DeFi and CeFi applications, leading to a general downward trend of crypto asset prices, mass layoffs in related startups, and even the collapse of some CeFi startups that used these stable tokens as collateral. Both events are good examples for the experimental nature of algorithmic stable tokens and provide a learning ground for future mechanism designers - in order to avoid bad design practices and acknowledge the systemic spillover risks of stable tokens for other DeFi and CeFi applications. 
       
       
         Fractional-reserve mechanisms 
          grew out of the algorithmic stable token movement but can be considered their own category as they combine the advantages of fully-algorithmic with the advantages of fully-collateralized stable token systems. They are designed to avoid over-collateralization, reduce the custodial risks imminent to fully-collateralized systems, and reduce the systemic risk intrinsic to pure algorithmic models. The concept was first introduced by  
         
           Frax Protocol 
         
          (FRAX) and is becoming increasingly popular as it combines a partial-collateralization mechanism with a two-token balancer mechanism used by algorithmic tokens. FRAX is pegged 1:1 to the U.S. Dollar while Frax Share (FXS) is the governance token of the stable token system. Token supply is only partially backed by a collateral, algorithmic stabilization is used as a complement. FRAX is minted using both FXS and USDC. The algorithmic mechanism uses on-chain collateral assets to a limited degree to stabilize price peg.  
         
           Neutrino Protocol 
         
          (USDN) and the  
         
           Fei Protocol 
         
          (FEI) are other examples that use this kind of mechanism. At the time of writing the book FEI has stopped operations, and USDN has not achieved stability. 
       
       
         One of the greatest challenges of algorithmic stable tokens is that they require a certain degree of demand; otherwise, the mechanism collapses. The mechanism is also designed on the assumption that all token holders are self-motivated, independent investors who are both interested and have the time to invest and profit from the arbitrage opportunities the protocol provides. It is the age-old myth of homo economicus, the profit-maximizing individual market participant who only makes rational economic decisions without any mental shortcuts, based on full information and with unlimited time at their disposal. Unfortunately, this type of assumption is not based on any hard evidence from financial market history in general or crypto-markets in particular. This flawed assumption has led to many mechanism design failures in the area of stable tokens and DAOs since the early days of alternative cryptocurrencies. One reason may be that software engineers – who have little experience in microeconomics, macroeconomics and institutional economics – make simplified assumptions about how economic interactions work on a large scale. Another challenge is that token holders – who often act on the basis of misinformation or a superficial understanding of the market mechanisms – may inadvertently take actions that cause their stable tokens to lose value. Furthermore, financial markets in general are known for their hype cycles which are very often driven by herd behavior rather than “rational” economic evidence-based thinking and subsequent actions. Such herd behavior is often unrelated to the actual underlying asset values, at least in the short term. Only a selected few market actors – those who are financially savvy and have the industry know-how – can protect their downside and potentially win in this poker game. The rest of the financially less literate investors might think that they are playing the same game, when in fact they are engaging in a game of roulette rather than poker, since they have not enough knowledge to protect their downside. For the mass of retail investors, crypto assets, just like stock market assets, are often just a game of pure chance. 
       
       
         Pure algorithmic token systems often did not use a collateral against which the stable token could be liquidated, and stable token holders risked losing – and in some cases actually lost – all their investments when the mechanisms crashed and there was a “bank run” effect. There seems to be a need for explicit but mindful stable token legislation. It remains to be seen if national policy makers will be mindful of the potential of Web3 to democratize money, or rather use such regulation as a backdoor mechanism to undermine decentralization efforts and protect current financial and monetary market institutions. 
       
       
         
         
         Central Bank Digital Currency 
       
       
         While a growing Web3 community has started to experiment with various privately or collectively issued stable token solutions, central banks have also started to look into tokenizing their own (fiat) currencies that will be settled on some kind of distributed ledger infrastructure. Tokenized fiat currencies, also referred to as “Central Bank Digital Currency” (CBDC), are the tokenized representation of a country’s fiat currency that is directly issued by the respective central bank. CBDCs would count towards the base money supply of a nation, together with other forms of money: cash and other cash equivalents, short-term deposits, and long-term deposits. CBDCs could be used for the settlement of smart contracts, since their tokenized equivalent can be managed by an underlying distributed ledger. CBDC would be a new form of electronic money, issued by central banks, that can be accessed directly by consumers. This means that account holders would have an account directly with the central bank instead of with a private bank. Every citizen would have their own CBDC wallet, which means that central banks would compete with commercial banks over customer deposits, as CBDCs can reduce the cost of managing the local and international payment system. Currently, the cost of managing the cash supply of a country is high, as are cross-border transactions. In the long run, CBDCs could eliminate the need for classic bank accounts, replacing them with easy-to-download mobile crypto wallets, and potentially increase inclusion of the underbanked. However, cutting out commercial banks and cross-border payment systems could destabilize the credit system and foreign exchange markets, at least in the short term. For an up-to-date look into the endeavors of governments to launch their own CBDCs, follow the information on this link:  
         
           https://cbdctracker.org 
         
           
       
       
         Some economists believe that CBDC could also be a method to achieve a full-reserve banking system, which means that they could challenge the practice of fractional-reserve banking and eliminate the need for deposit guarantees. Issuing central bank money directly to the public could also provide a new channel for monetary policy execution. This would allow for more direct control of the money supply and more granular financial stimulation packages such as government subsidies that can only be conditionally spent. This could complement or substitute indirect financial stimulation tools such as adapting interest rates or the practice of quantitative easing. From a stability perspective, the advantage of CBDCs would be that tokenized fiat money already comes with an inbuilt stability mechanism. The stability mechanisms are provided by established players like central banks in collaboration with the fiscal and monetary policy of a national government. Given worldwide inflation rates at the time of writing this book, however, one might have the impression that this argument looks a bit weak.  
       
       
         Before the announcement of a Facebook-led consortium (the  
         
           Libra consortium 
         
         ) that wanted to launch a stable token for Internet payments back in 2019, most central banks were skeptical about tokenizing their national currencies. Only a handful of central banks and the International Monetary Fund were looking into it. The Libra initiative might have been the black swan event that triggered many central banks to rethink their stance. According to the studies periodically conducted by the Bank of International Settlement, only 20% of governments were looking into tokenizing their currencies before 2019. By 2020, more than 80% of governments were considering tokenizing their government-issued currency. It is quite likely that, with the announcements of the Libra project, many central banks that were previously reluctant toward the issuance of Central Bank Digital Currencies, started to investigate the merits of tokenizing their national currencies. After all, Libra’s instant settlement possibilities would have allowed 2 billion Facebook users to access a Web3-native global payment system from one day to the other, which would have been a considerable threat to established players in the current global banking system.  
       
       
         “Synthetic CBDCs” (sCBDCs) refer to private institutions that issue tokens that are fully backed with central bank reserves. The question is whether CBDCs and sCBDCs will render private stable token efforts obsolete, or if they will become just one among the many other tokens in this new tokenized economy. It is quite likely that within the next five years, many fiat currencies will have a central-bank-issued tokenized equivalent. As central banks are moving into tokenizing their national currencies and national reserves, we will see if current private tokenized fiat currency projects such as USDT or USDC will remain legal or require more specialized licenses. 
       
       
         While there is some potential in CBDCs, there are also serious societal threats that can come with such a type of money, mostly around privacy. As opposed to cash-based systems, CBDCs are not anonymous. While the lack of anonymity is not a new phenomenon and already exists in the context of bank money, which makes up most of the total money supply and financial transactions of a modern economy, governments will have more direct access to their citizens’ spending logs – without a judicial warrant. Furthermore, CBDCs would make money prone to permissioned spending. From the point of view of the government this would mean finer-grained fiscal policy making possibilities:  Subsidies or tax breaks could be granted for conditional spending that can be better controlled than with current systems. But for individual people and organizations, CBDCs could mean a loss of autonomy over spending decisions if governments would start to micromanage and monitor people's spending habits. 
       
       
         
         
         Interdependencies & Building Blocks  
       
       
         The above-mentioned categories of privately issued stable token mechanisms (except for CBDC) have developed over time, but often don’t exist in their pure form anymore. Many mixed stable token mechanisms have emerged over the past two years that don’t always adhere to these classical categorizations. It is important to understand the basic parameters, building blocks, and interaction patterns of stable tokens in order to be able to comprehend the benefits and pitfalls of different stability mechanisms. Understanding the interdependencies between the parameters and components of a stable token mechanism is critical, since stable tokens have complex interaction patterns with a growing range of financial tools in the DeFi ecosystem at large. Flawed assumptions about the effects of the different building blocks of a stable token system with other stable token systems or other DeFi assets and applications can lead to massive collateral damages in the DeFi space, especially in the case of algorithmic stable tokens that don’t use collateral.  
       
       
         Peg 
         : The main purpose of a stable token is to maintain price stability against a target value – the peg. The peg might be hard or soft, or allow fluctuation within a certain base range. A stable token can never be stable against all global currencies or all assets, but only stable in a relative sense – in relation to one currency, a basket of currencies, one asset class, a basket of assets, or a macroeconomic indicator such as the consumer price index. In a globalized economy with over 200 different national currencies, in addition to commodities, stocks, bonds, and other a growing range of crypto asset classes which all have fluctuating values against each other, “stability” is therefore a relative term for a globally accessible payment token.  
       
       
         Collateral asset 
         : Is there a collateral asset? If yes, what asset or mix of assets will be held in reserve as a collateral? Are the assets on-chain (crypto-native assets such as BTC, ETC, etc.) or off-chain (assets of the real economy that are held in a vault)? It is important to note that the collateral assets don’t necessarily need to be identical with the assets against which the target price is pegged. The target price of a stable token, for example, could be pegged to USD, while an equivalent in ETH could be held in reserve as collateral. 
       
       
         Collateralization level 
         : If a collateral asset exists, one needs to determine the collateralization level, i.e. the percentage of assets in proportion to the peg that one needs to hold in reserve. To determine the mechanism, one needs to understand the price volatility of the collateral asset in proportion to the pegged asset, currency, index, basket, etc. To maintain a sustainable price stability against the pegged target, one can use the mechanism of full-collateralization (1:1), over-collateralization, or under-collateralization.  
       
       
         Stability mechanism 
         : The mechanism can be designed to be static, which means that it does not change over time. In this case the mechanism is hard-coded into the smart contract. The mechanism can also be dynamic – as would be the case with algorithmic stable tokens that adapt to market conditions. In this case the parameters are designed to adapt to market conditions within a predetermined range, and could also be adjusted by human intervention through a governance process of eligible network stakeholders. 
       
       
         Crisis resistance & risk management 
         : In order to evaluate the sustainability of the stability mechanism, one needs to understand the conditions under which mechanisms can maintain the envisioned stability or under which conditions the mechanisms might not withstand an unforeseen crisis. Given the complexities of a growing DeFi ecosystem, crisis resistance is one of the most important factors. It raises many questions around individual and institutional risk management as well as governance questions such as who is in control of the stable token system – who can intervene if unexpected situations occur and prevent maximum collateral damage? This became evident in the wake of the collapse of some algorithmic stable tokens in 2022, given the systemic spillover effects on other DeFi protocols and CeFi services that used these stable tokens as collateral.  
       
       
         Who issues the stable token 
         ? In order to understand the politics behind a stability mechanism, one needs to look at the type of issuer, understand how the issuer is institutionalized (private institution, governmental institution or DAO), and what legal liabilities the issuer might have to face. If the issuer is a private institution or a DAO, the question of which legislation they are under is crucial, since the coercive power of potential government action can create vested interests. When the issuer is institutionalized as a DAO, the question of how decentralized the DAO is, or who can participate in what kind of decision-making process, and what the power structures are, will be relevant in order to understand potential vested interests. 
       
       In summer 2021, private stable tokens had a total market capitalization of 100 billion USD. Asset-collateralised stable tokens accounted for 90% of the total market share. Fiat-collateralized tokens were the most dominant type, as they became popular for trading pairs in many DeFi protocols and among individuals in countries with extraordinarily high inflation rates - such as Turkey late 2021, early 2022 - where many people fled into dollar-backed stable tokens as a result of the devaluation of the Turkish Lira.  
       
         
         
         Impossible Trinity 
       
       
         Stable tokens always face “the impossible trinity” that conventional fiat currencies are confronted with as well. This trilemma was first mentioned by economists Flemming and Mudell in the 1960s and describes the trade-off between (i) exchange rate stability, (ii) autonomous monetary policy, and (iii)  
         
           capital mobility 
         
         
           
             
               24 
             
           
         
          that national currencies are facing. This means that it is impossible for fiat currencies to ensure a fixed exchange rate, free capital flows and an independent monetary policy all at the same time. In the context of stable tokens, capital mobility is a given due to the nature of blockchain networks and tokenization. Exchange rate stability is the goal, so autonomous monetary policy seems to become the trade-off. Additionally, a new trade-off emerges between the following goals: (a) exchange rate stability, (c) decentralization & autonomy, and (c) capital efficiency. Experience has shown that stable tokens can only guarantee two out of the three goals at the same time. These three objectives of stable token designs often conflict with each other, and need to be delicately balanced when designing a stable tokens system.  
       
       
         Exchange rate stability 
          towards a target range or a peg is the ultimate goal and the whole raison d’etre of a stable token. The most stable prices can be achieved with fiat-or commodity-backed stable tokens, while algorithmic solutions can be highly volatile or completely collapse when entering a death spiral. Fiat-collateralized or commodity-collateralized stable tokens can guarantee somewhat stable exchange rates; they are the least “decentralized” type of stable token.  
       
       
         Decentralization & autonomy 
          are important values in Web3-based applications, and would require that a stable token does not rely on a centralized service provider managing all collateral assets, who might be prone to corruption or censorship. The concept of “decentralization” in the context of stable token systems would also require an autonomous monetary policy. The monetary policy of an asset-pegged and asset-backed stable token such as USDT or USDC, depends on the monetary policy of the US federal reserve. The monetary policy of a crypto-collateralized stable token such as RAI depends on the monetary policy of Ethereum and the US federal reserve, since it floats around USD but is backed by ETH, and can be considered partially autonomous. Algorithmic stable tokens with no collateral have full autonomy but are also less sustainable, at least at the time of writing. 
       
       
         Capital efficiency 
          is a general financial term 
         
           
             
               25 
             
           
         
          that has been adapted to the context of stable tokens, and refers to the amount of capital required to create 1 EUR worth of tokens. Capital efficiency is related to collateralization levels that usually trade off with decentralization efforts. It can be seen as a new form of “capital mobility” in the context of Web-based stable token systems. In the case of crypto-collateralized stable tokens, price stability can only be achieved with over-collateralization due to the volatile nature of crypto asset prices, which in turn reduces the capital efficiency. Crypto-collateralized stable tokens can achieve higher levels of capital efficiency by leveraging the collateral assets for quick loan or arbitrage opportunities, but this opens a can of worms of other systemic risks. Algorithmic stable tokens, in turn, are extremely capital efficient, as they do not require much (or any) collateral, and they are operated by a decentralized protocol. However, at the time of writing, they have yet failed to prove that they can maintain price stability in the medium and long term, which is the ultimate goal of a stable token. 
       
       
         
         
         Challenges & Outlook 
       
       
         While many stable token projects attempt to achieve token stability, no clear best practices have been established yet – other than potential CBDCs which are centralized solutions, and do not live up to the claim of P2P money. The emergence of stable tokens is a relatively new phenomenon, many have only been operational for a short time, especially the crypto-collateralized and algorithmic stable tokens. Only a few have survived since their launch and those that have been operational for a while, often suffer from the collateral damage caused by the volatility of their underlying collateral assets, or from the stability mechanisms that have not been sufficiently stress tested. An increasingly complex web of composable DeFi solutions creates systemic risk potentials and spill over effects that need to be taken into account when designing a stable token system. 
       
       
         Economists argue that token systems with more autonomous token supply rules will never have stable exchange rates comparable to conventional currencies. However, if one considers a crypto token not as a competitor to a conventional currency but rather as a new, alternative asset, the outlook might be different. Conventional assets such as stocks are not expected to be stable in value either, nor has the lack of price stability hindered the adoption of stocks. Last but not least, it is important to point out that stable tokens may not be the only solution to token price volatility. Insurances or financial derivatives could provide alternatives or at least complementary methods in order to mitigate price volatility. Hedging is an investment strategy that can be used to reduce financial risk, balancing positions in the market to protect against price volatilities. A combination of DeFi applications could be used to create such P2P derivative and hedging solutions (more on this in the chapter “Tokenized Derivatives, Synthetics & Insurances.”) 
       
       
         Current adoption of stable tokens shows that Tether and similar fiat-collateralized tokens are leading the field in terms of market capitalization. DAI (MakerDAO) and similar solutions seem to be the most promising alternatives. For a while, algorithmic stable token projects without collateral claimed to offer full decentralization and automation, but have so far failed in their medium- to short-term risk management. While all types of smart-contract-based automation have shortcomings – such as the uncertain robustness of their mechanisms in the case of a black swan event – the combination of the best practices of various stable token models might prove to be the most resilient in the future. In this context it is important to note that all stable tokens, even the more decentralized and algorithmic ones, are in some way pegged to another underlying asset with varying pegging ratios and collateralization mechanisms. Once economies develop around the stable token and certain network effects materialize, the peg might start to matter less and the network effects of the actual economy that has evolved around that stable token will matter more. This could be the case when businesses are willing to accept a stable token because it is also accepted by other businesses. 
       
       
         Another issue is the so-called “oracle problem” which refers to the reliable and secure data about real real-word assets and their prices in a truly decentralized setup. If the token is pegged to the value of an asset, the system needs a decentralized way to receive data about the exchange rate between the stable token and the asset that it is pegged to, as well as the actual location of that asset. However, none of the existing solutions are fully decentralized or fully reliable yet. 
       
       
         All these challenges can be resolved over time with a more refined token mechanism design, but require much more research and development (R&D). Unfortunately, R&D is often replaced by real life trial and error – where the founding team launches a stable token project based on often untested assumptions. As a result, it is often the less experienced investors that risk losing a lot of money on stable token systems that have not been sufficiently researched. While there is much that the crypto community can learn from the history of applied monetary and financial market policy of nation states over the last century – its best practices as well as its failures – they will ultimately have to find their own way. The type of monetary policy and stability mechanisms that nation states have been applying have proven to be increasingly ineffective in a globalized economy, as we can see from recent economic shocks and a global trend towards hyperinflation.  
       
       
          An increasingly complex web of interdependent DeFi solutions creates systemic risk potentials and spill over effects that need to be taken into account when designing such stable token systems.  
       
       
         The collateral effects of an increasingly globalized economy – a result of a series of free trade agreements that only came into effect after the 1970s, around the same time the gold standard was abolished – as well as the emergence of the Internet and its mass adoption in the 1990s, all contributed to the emergence of a sector of Web2- and Web3-based new economic non-state actors who seem to be more flexible than nation states. There is an increasing sense among many members of society that political institutions try to regulate their own geographic silos, and have not sufficiently been able to adapt to social and economic revolutions that have been triggered by their free trade agreements and the Internet. It seems that nation states are currently facing the limits of what they can actually regulate in a globalized, Internet-based economy.  
       
       
         However, nation states still have executive powers over their citizens and other economic actors within their geographic boundaries and can undermine global Web3-based solutions through legislation. Stable tokens could furthermore face competition from CBDCs as alternative payment tokens that regulators can (try to) enforce, either directly (by banning or highly regulating privately issued stable tokens) or through regulatory backdoors. The future, thus, remains uncertain. Successful stable token solutions could resolve the bottleneck of using tokens as a unit of account, and therefore as a medium of day-to-day exchange. The question is how decentralized they will be, and whether they can live up to the founding spirit of Bitcoin and other Web protocols that followed. They have the potential to be a major catalyst for decentralized applications and a fluid token economy. However, stability is only one of many challenges to making tokens a medium of exchange for day-to-day payments. Without privacy, no token will be fungible, and without scalability and better wallet usability, businesses as well as consumers will not adopt this new technology. 
       
         
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       While the Bitcoin protocol introduced a groundbreaking consensus algorithm, it came with a rudimentary monetary policy that simply regulates and limits the amount of tokens minted over time. The protocol does not provide a sophisticated economic algorithm that regulates price stability. As a result, Bitcoin and similar protocol tokens are subject to price volatility and are currently impractical for day-to-day payments, at least in countries with relatively stable inflation rates.  ~ Short-term stability of value is one of the most important functions of money so it can serve as a unit of account within an economic system. Stability is a basic requirement for meaningful economic planning of all actors in an economy. Salaries, investments, and daily expenses such as rent, utility payments, and groceries cannot be reliably denominated or planned for with a medium of exchange that has extremely fluctuating exchange rates. Businesses and individuals are not likely to accept a token as a method of payment if its value can drop within a short amount of time. The same is true for Web3 tokens.  ~ Protocol tokens such as Bitcoin (BTC) or Ether (ETH) are volatile in value for several reasons. The main reason is the static monetary policy defined in their protocols: This means that the token supply policy does not adjust to macroeconomic conditions outside the Bitcoin or Ethereum system. No currency interventions are performed, which is what central banks usually do to keep their national currencies relatively stable against other currencies. ~ The lack of price stability mechanisms in the protocol of classic cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin has led to the emergence of so-called “stablecoins” or “stable tokens” in the Web3 community. Over the years, a range of attempts has been made to achieve token price stability. Various stable tokens have emerged from these innovation attempts that will be discussed in this chapter. ~ Traditionally, the crypto community distinguished between the following types of stable tokens: (i) fiat-collateralized or commodity-collateralized stable tokens, (ii) crypto-collateralized stable tokens, and (iii) algorithmic stable tokens. Lately (iv) central banks have also started looking into tokenizing their currencies which already come with inbuilt price stability mechanisms and are generally referred to as “Central Bank Digital Currencies” or “CBDCs.” This classification is a historical one – from the perspective of how the stable token ecosystem has evolved over time – and the concepts have become entangled.  ~ Price stability is understood as a relative lack of inflation, where a currency retains its purchasing power, or only slightly loses its purchasing power at a steady and slowly increasing rate. While most economists agree that hyperinflation is too disruptive to economic systems, low to moderate rates of inflation have become widely accepted or are generally considered as less harmful. Some economists believe that a low level of inflation might even be useful to conduct more sustainable monetary policy intervention in the long term. ~ Central banks and other monetary policymakers have the operational task of keeping the inflation rate of a national currency low and relatively stable. The tools they usually apply are a combination of modifying the interest rates, currency interventions and other open market operations, as well as interfering with the reserve requirements of commercial banks. ~ Stability is always a relative term, since fiat currencies and commodities have a fluctuating exchange rate against each other. A central bank can try to keep the currency it governs stable against the local consumer price index, but in a global economy of import and exports, such stability endeavors will only be able to affect locally sourced goods and services. While a national currency could become unstable by losing its purchasing power domestically due to high inflation rates, it could – at the same time – have much higher purchasing power in another country with relatively higher inflation rates. ~ In Web3, a new type of non-state actor in the form of a Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAO) has emerged – which can be considered a Web3-based “network state.” These new types of non-state actors have their own internal currencies as well as an entire ecosystem of economic services that build on these network states. This new type of non-state actor is an addition to the global interplay of currency, exchange rate and inflation, and generally adds to existing complexities, both in foreign exchange markets in particular and the financial landscape in general. ~ Fiat-collateralized and commodity-collateralized stable tokens: A simple way to achieve a token with a stable value is to back the token with a reserve of assets of the real economy that have relatively stable prices such as fiat currencies or certain types of commodities. Fiat-collateralized stable tokens are relatively easy to set up, but they have a centralization problem. An institution – usually operated by a private company – needs to manage the physical assets that act as collateral of the stable token, usually in a bank account or secure vault. Trust is outsourced to a single entity, which guarantees that the amount of stable tokens issued corresponds to the amount of actual assets held in a secure vault as collateral, making the stable token system subject to so-called “counterparty risk.” ~ Crypto-collateralized stable tokens: The stable token is collateralized with crypto-native assets such as ETH instead of a fiat currency or other real-world asset. The advantage of Web3-native collateral assets is that they can be managed entirely on-chain, and are audited by a publicly verifiable infrastructure – a blockchain network – instead of a third party who would need to hold physical collateral assets in secure vaults. One of the greatest challenges of crypto-collateralized stable tokens is the necessary over-collateralization in order to mitigate price volatilities of the collateral assets, which also reduces so-called “capital efficiency.” Many economists have warned that if price volatility of the underlying asset is too high, crypto-collateralized solutions are susceptible to black swan events.  ~ Algorithmic stable tokens have adaptive supply and demand mechanisms, which are encoded into the token contract. The mechanism incentivizes the behavior of market participants. Algorithmic stable tokens are often under-collateralized or, in their purest form, not collateralized at all. Completely uncollateralized stable tokens are not backed by any external asset but only by an algorithmic supply and demand policy that is designed to reduce the price volatility of the token in question by offering economic incentives in the form of arbitrage opportunities to market participants that collectively regulate the stable token price. The peg could be anything from a fiat currency, a basket of goods and services, to a suitable economic indicator.  ~ Central banks have also started to look into tokenizing their own (fiat) currencies that will be settled on some kind of distributed ledger infrastructure. Tokenized fiat currencies, also referred to as “Central Bank Digital Currency” (CBDC), are the tokenized representation of a country’s fiat currency that is directly issued by the respective central bank.  ~ Many mixed stable token mechanisms have emerged over the past two years that don’t always adhere to these classic categorizations. The basic parameters of stable tokens are the (i) peg, (ii) the collateral asset, (iii) collateralization level, (iv) stability mechanism. The questions of who issues the stable token, and how crisis resistant a token can be in case of unexpected events, are other important design questions.  ~ Stable tokens always face “the impossible trinity” that conventional fiat currencies are confronted with as well. This trilemma was first mentioned by economists Fleming and Mundell in the 1960s and describes the trade-off between (i) exchange rate stability, (ii) autonomous monetary policy, and (iii) capital mobility that national currencies are facing. In the context of stable tokens, capital mobility is a given due to the nature of blockchain networks and tokenization. Exchange rate stability is the goal, so autonomous monetary policy seems to become the trade-off. Additionally, a new trade-off emerges between the following goals: (a) exchange rate stability, (c) decentralization & autonomy, and (c) capital efficiency.
