Experience has shown that stable tokens can only guarantee two out of the three goals at the same time.

Where possible, the links will be updated on a regular basis to prevent the issue of broken links. It is only scannable on white or light colored backgrounds and might not work on dark backgrounds. The website is also accessible under following URL:  
         
           https://bit.ly/40yGhL2

         Asset Tokens & NFTs: Money with Attributes 
       
       
         
         Beyond the use case of currencies, tokens can also be used to create digital certificates for any real or virtual economic asset. While early crypto assets had fungible characteristics, such as tokens that represent currencies or commodities, real-world assets very often have unique properties, and are therefore non-fungible, which is why they are often referred to as non-fungible tokens.  
         The tokenization of assets of the real economy can replace entire back offices of asset management with smart contracts, which can make asset management more efficient and  
         simplify the implementation of 
          fractional ownership.  
         It 
          can also  
         facilitate the 
          attachment of unique properties to previously fungible asset classes, t 
         hereby creating new personalized asset classes 
         .  ~ 
       
       
         Blockchain networks and Web3 applications enable the tokenization of any asset of the real and virtual economy. Any physical good or share in a small- or medium-sized company can be tokenized and subsequently divided into representative tokens, which could be traded on an open market – all this at a fraction of what it would cost in the client-server world. Tools that represent various types of assets today, such as other digital certificates or paper certificates, are likely to be substituted by Web3 tokens in the future. First use cases are already emerging. Selected examples will be analyzed in more detail in this chapter.  
       
       
         Tokenization of assets refers to the process of creating a Web3-native digital certificate for any physical object, financial asset or digital file. The tokens represent the physical or digital counterpart and are collectively managed by a public blockchain infrastructure or similar distributed ledger. The term “asset token” is an economic term that refers to any type of asset – whether digital or physical. The term “real-world asset” (RWA) is used to describe physical assets such as commodities, physical art, real estate, or securities. “Security token” is a legal term that specifically refers to a type of asset token that is classified as a security according to financial market regulations. The interpretation of what constitutes a security, however, is subject to national legislation. The term “non-fungible tokens” (NFTs), has recently become popular, mainly being used by mass media to describe digital art tokens, but this is a misappropriation of the term. While NFTs can represent digital or physical art, they can also represent any other type of non-fungible asset or identity-based digital certificates. In this chapter we will focus only on the use cases where an NFT represents a unique asset. Non-fungible tokens are unique in nature, and represent assets that have more complex properties than fungible assets. They come attached with a set of metadata that describe the unique properties of that asset and have a more complex rights management logic built into the token contract.  
       
       
         Tokens that represent assets of any kind can provide more market transparency than existing financial systems currently offer. This could significantly reduce fraud or corruption along the supply chain of goods, services, and financial transactions. Due to the frictionless settlement infrastructure a public and permissionless blockchain network provides, the transaction costs of developing and managing the rights attached to the digital certificates representing these assets can be significantly lower compared to what it would cost in state-of-the-art systems. Tokenization can therefore increase the divisibility and liquidity, lower costs of price discovery, and create less fragmented markets, especially for real life assets that currently do not have high market liquidity. This reduces market friction, allowing more efficient marketplaces for certain assets: art or real estate, for example, usually have high economic buy-ins and can use tokenization to issue fractional ownership tokens. Real estate and art are asset classes that were not easily divisible before, and whose property rights can now be fractionalized at lower transaction costs than with established systems. Divisibility refers to the fact that one acquires a fraction of an asset, and if that fraction is designed to be transferable, this could generate more demand from a larger potential pool of buyers. The more easily divisible an asset is, the more transferable it becomes, as it can attract more investors with less economic means, increasing the market liquidity of that specific asset market. Consequently, the tokenization of real-world assets could be a breeding ground for completely new use cases, business models, and asset types that were not economically feasible before, and potentially enable new value-creation models. While the tokenization of real-world assets could lead to an additional market capitalization of trillions of Euros into the Web3, it needs a few prerequisites such as (i) a well-defined regulatory environment for the respective asset token classes, (ii) online exchanges specialized in the relevant asset tokens, (iii) trusted custodians who manage the physical assets in the case of co-ownership. 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 1: Security Tokens 
       
       
         Security tokens provide a new form of digital representation of existing securities, which come with embedded automated rights management. From a regulator’s point of view, these tokens are traditional securities that are simply represented and managed by a new technological infrastructure. Security tokens are not a new product or economic phenomenon, and therefore are fairly easy to understand and regulate. However, there is no common global understanding of what constitutes a security, as regulation differs from country to country. In some jurisdictions, the term “security token” applies to any token that represents a recognized asset or investment concept. Other jurisdictions have a more narrow definition. The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), for example, defines a token as a security if one invests money with the idea to profit from the efforts of someone else. More specifically, a token would be considered a security if there is: (i) an investment of money; (ii) an expectation of profits; (iii) money held by a common enterprise; and (iv) profits are a result of third-party efforts. In contrast, the European definition of securities includes standardized assets that are transferable and negotiable on capital markets, have no instrument of payment, and are comparable to equity or debt instruments. 
       
       
         In the current financial system, securities and other financial products often take a long time to settle. While operational 24-hour markets exist already today, they are rarely P2P, and the settlement process needs to be coordinated between various institutions, all of whom have their own private ledger to manage their assets. In spite of the fact that the processes have improved with the advent of computers and then the Internet, the settlement is often far from real time. Today, a complex document-handling process is performed by a series of separate ledgers, where data is managed behind the walled gardens of the servers of each stakeholder involved. This document-management nightmare creates many inefficiencies and time lags. Transactions can take a minimum of two work days to settle and are cost intensive. These time lags are not always technically necessary, but often a relic of old practices that have benefited traditional financial institutions. 
       
       
         Security tokens settled by Web3 infrastructure can facilitate frictionless settlement processes, without sacrificing legal protection. 
         
           
             
               26 
             
           
         
          Compliance comes built into the token contract. The self-enforcing code is an efficient answer to a complex aspect of trading securities, where regulation can vary depending on asset type or investor type, taking into account that stakeholders — the buyer, the seller, and the issuer — are often subject to different jurisdictions. The token contract replaces the intermediary and executes the settlement process between sellers and buyers, which could reduce brokerage fees. To be more precise, the consensus mechanism of the underlying blockchain infrastructure is used for settlement of updates, which can only happen according to rules encoded in the smart contract. Fully operational 24/7 markets could furthermore reduce settlement time to a few minutes or less. Paying out dividends could be conducted with a smart contract and on the fly, which is an upgrade from most state-of-the-art financial settlement systems.  
       
       
         The programmable nature of tokens also makes it cheaper and easier to formalize special conditions, which could allow for more personalized asset types that might have been theoretically possible before, but were not economically feasible to implement. However, any system for trading security tokens needs to incorporate a myriad of legal contracts. The implementation of security tokens is a complex techno-legal task and their feasibility often also depends on network effects. Third-party service providers have started to provide token standards designed to allow transparent issuance of asset tokens in general and security tokens in particular, including processes for KYC (Know Your Customer) and AML (Anti Money Laundering) requirements. More and more market players are announcing services around security tokens. Some established companies and stock exchanges across the world have announced or started to operate specialized trading platforms for tokenized securities. 
       
       
         Buying shares of small and mid-sized enterprises (SMEs) in foreign countries is currently almost impossible across borders – this could potentially become much more feasible over globally accessible Web3 infrastructure, but only once Web3 infrastructure becomes more adopted by mainstream institutions worldwide. The tokenization of SME shares could potentially add more liquidity and new opportunities for entrepreneurs and investors alike. This could create a more P2P form of microfinancing that does not need the intermediary services of international organizations such as the International Monetary Fund or the World Bank. However, opening up global markets to SME financing or co-funding comes with many operational and regulatory challenges – such as making sure that those tokenized SME shares actually come from a real SME that will respect one’s real-world rights of co-ownership. 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 2: Real Estate NFTs & Fractional Ownership 
       
       
         The tokenization of real estate refers to the process of creating a tokenized digital ownership certificate for an individual real estate object. The token represents the deed to a physical counterpart — such as an individual apartment, an apartment complex, or an entire office building. The deed to the real estate object would be issued as an non-fungible token – a “real estate NFT.” A token contract represents the deeds to the property and adds all relevant information in the form of metadata to the token – such as the size, the address, the price, the ownership history, and other relevant data. The hashed data of each deed can be registered on a distributed ledger to provide a universally shared dataset on all real estate related activities, such as previous owner or repairs conducted. The property right to the real estate object could then be divided into parts and sold to several (co-)owners. Even if a token represents a physical asset that is not divisible, as in the case of an apartment, the NFT representing the property rights is divisible. These fractional ownership tokens would represent the fractional property rights to the deed. Depending on the regulatory environment and how the smart contract is set up, these fractionalized tokens may become eligible for global trading. While the tokenized deed itself represents a unique asset, all fractions of that deed would usually be interchangeable (fungible). While tokenizing real estate is theoretically feasible and has a lot of potential, this use case comes with many practical challenges, most of which concern legal and regulatory questions that may vary from country to country or state to state. Unfolding the full potential of tokenization of the real estate market furthermore requires a few prerequisites: (i) Web3-compatible land registries, (ii) collateral management, and (iii) collective facility management. 
       
       
         Web3-compatible land registries 
         : The operational and legal process of all real estate transactions needs to be made Web3-compatible, from the land registry process to the general regulatory environment accepting smart contract settlement. All involved stakeholders of the real estate market – from developers and brokers to banks, real estate funds, and facility managers – also need to be Web3-compatible before such a use case can become feasible. 
       
       
         Collateral management 
         : The current process of taking out a loan for home ownership comes with a lot of regulatory oversight and requires due diligence from the bank side. Banks need to make sure that the person taking out the loan will also be able to pay back the loan, including the interest on the loan. The bank is, therefore, co-registered in the land titles in case the homeowner fails to pay back the loan, such that the bank can claim the property, sell it, and liquidate the real estate object to get back the funds they are owed. While fractional ownership is theoretically possible once tokenized deeds are an accepted legal instrument, one would need to resolve crisis management situations around co-ownership and collective mediation if one of the co-owner defaults on their obligations. Best practices for such edge cases have yet to be established. 
       
       
         Collective facility management 
         : In the case of co-ownership, the maintenance processes of a collectively owned real estate object also needs to be covered by the smart contract logic.  
       
       
         Global KYC process & international treaties 
         : In the case of cross-border co-ownership, a globally accepted identification system is needed. Currently, KYC regulations are mostly national, and do not work well cross-border. An internationally accepted Web3-native and regulatory compliant KYC process with adequate key management and key-revocation solutions would be an absolute prerequisite for real estate use cases, such that international buyers can interact with sellers without having to physically travel to the country where the potential seller lives. International treaties also need to make sure that cross-border law enforcement authorities can intervene in practice if a party to the smart contract defaults. 
       
       
         While tokenization of the real estate market introduces many interesting potential use cases, one also needs to account for potential threats and negative market dynamics. The most important opportunities and challenges are: 
       
       
         Easier rights management 
         : The real estate market is highly fragmented and centralized. Data is managed by a series of third parties such as banks, attorneys, notaries, and land registries who all use their own proprietary and costly software that, for the most part, is not interoperable. Smart contracts have the potential to facilitate rights management in the real estate industry, including the whole settlement process. Once real estate ownership rights are registered on a blockchain network, they can easily be managed by the public infrastructure and transferred to P2P, if it complies with regulations. Web3-based land registries could minimize bureaucracy and reduce market friction. The process is faster and cheaper and opens new avenues for more individualized rights management and ownership transfer.  
       
       
         Fractional ownership & new forms of real estate funding 
         : Even though real estate is one of the largest asset classes worldwide in terms of market capitalization, real estate ownership is not open to all members of society today. Many low-income households cannot afford to buy real estate. To receive a loan, buyers must have a high credit score, a steady and well-paid job, or a collateral of other assets. While the concept of fractional ownership already exists – one can buy real estate investment shares – most of the state-of-the-art solutions are not transparent and not very beneficial to smallhold investors. Tokenizing real estate would be the next step in the automation process, making it more efficient to issue and sell these fractional ownership rights at lower costs, and can create long-term benefits for smallhold investors and the market in general, if the above-mentioned challenges can be overcome. Private homeowners could issue fractional ownership tokens of an apartment they want to buy, which would allow them to raise funds without needing to go through a bank or take out a private loan. Anyone could become a co-investor to a home, and could then collect fractional rent in proportion to the amount of shares they hold or funds they have invested in the property.  
       
       
         Automated fractional rent collection 
         : In the case of co-ownership, rent collection is administered by the smart contract. If, for example, another person buys five percent of the tokenized value of your apartment, proportional rent could be paid out on a monthly basis automatically by the smart contract. In the case of a sale of the apartment at a future date, fractional token holders of that apartment could get their money back, which might also be automatically managed and enforced by the smart contract. 
       
       
         Tokenized real estate credit 
         : Instead of getting co-owners, one could issue tokenized credit certificates for taking out P2P loans. Potential lenders would issue tokenized micro-loans and collect fractional interest on their loans. The smart contract would settle the monthly repayment of the loan over a predefined period of time, by automatically collecting the principal to be paid back including the interest rates on the loan. 
       
       
         More inclusion & market liquidity 
         : Real estate is one of the most illiquid asset classes of our real economy, since buying and selling the asset requires a bureaucratic and often lengthy process; ownership does not change hands quickly. With fractional ownership tokens, real estate owners could sell off fractional shares of their apartments and create more opportunities for people with less disposable income and assets to invest into this asset class. People who were previously excluded from such investments for economic reasons could now invest in only a fraction of a whole unit, which would make the market more inclusive to those who have less economic means. 
       
       
         Global trading 
         : Depending on the regulatory environment and how the smart contract processes are designed, a real estate token may be eligible for global trading. In such a hypothetical case, an investor in France could easily buy fractional tokens in an office building in Canada with a few clicks on a real estate token exchange, without having to travel to Canada or having to find an attorney in Canada, to whom they would need to grant power of attorney and who would settle all bureaucratic issues against a relatively high fee. An investor in Mexico could fund a private apartment building in India.  
       
       
         Potentially negative market dynamics especially in combination with DeFi 
         : Tokenizing and fractionalizing real estate could also have potentially negative ramifications that could be mitigated through improved financial literacy and dedicated regulatory oversight. The potential interaction of tokenized real estate assets with DeFi — in combination with decentralized credit and lending tools — could create complex market dynamics and potentially also adverse market effects. There is much learning to incorporate from the housing market collapse in the United States that led to the financial crisis of 2008. When many people do not understand the complexities of what they are buying into, fractional ownership of real estate can become a dangerous investment game and result in the same “magical thinking.”  
       
       
         Most of the use cases mentioned above would require a regulatory environment that allows differentiated rights management and create a regulatory framework for potential edge cases, such as if the issuer of the tokens fails to pay rent to the fractional token holder. The token design of the real estate token needs to comply with regulatory standards and it also needs to have a meaningful governance mechanism to deal with unforeseen circumstances. There are a variety of established legal options for conflict resolution in fractional ownership situations, like so-called “drag along” and “tag along” rights 
         
           
             
               27 
             
           
         
          or “Dutch auctions,” 
         
           
             
               28 
             
           
         
          which could all be modeled into the token contract to arrive at a solution. When designing use cases for real estate tokens, it is also important to distinguish the type of rights that are granted to the different stakeholders in the token system: 
       
       
         Property rights 
         : Real estate tokens represent an asset and therefore the property rights to that asset. Anyone can buy and sell the property rights to these tokens over specialized real estate token exchanges that will emerge as the space matures. The property rights might be fractionalized and, depending on the use case, come with other rights attached (such as voting rights, access rights, or management rights.) 
       
       
         Access right 
         : When designing the access rights into a token contract, one needs to resolve the question of who is granted access to a real estate object and under which circumstance. While private homeowners might want to use fractional tokens to raise funds (via tokenized co-ownership rights or loans), they only want to pay the co-owners fractional rent and their creditors the tokenized principal plus interest on the tokenized loan – they would not want to grant them access rights to their home. The case might be different for a co-working space that is co-owned by its members. Depending on the membership types, one would want to grant different access rights that might be tied to the amount of hours per month that members would be allowed to use that co-working space. In the case of Web3-based home-sharing, access right tokens could be used to allow the next Web3-based Airbnb to issue access rights to a specific house for a specified time period (such a use case only makes sense if the property can be opened with a digital key that is connected to the Internet). 
       
       
         Voting rights & management rights 
         : Ownership rights also need to be detached from the management of the object. The governance rules of the token will need to regulate who gets to decide on the sale of a private home. In most cases, it would only be feasible to grant profit sharing rights, but not decision/voting rights on what happens to the real estate. In the case of co-operative ownership of a co-working space, special voting rights might be granted to members and co-owners of a co-working space. In this case, the token contract could also grant certain designated individuals special management rights and duties for the day-to-day management of the space.  
       
       
         Many countries are already looking into registering land titles on some kind of distributed ledger system, and many more will follow. A fast-growing ecosystem of service providers offering some kind of real estate tokenization is evolving, but most of them are still limited to tokenizing real estate baskets due to the lack of adequate regulatory frameworks, and also because most stakeholders in the real estate business are not yet operating on Web3-based infrastructure. 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 3: Art NFTs 
       
       
         While art NFTs and crypto collectibles are not new phenomena and have existed since the very early years of the first blockchain networks, they became increasingly popular within the Web3 community after the emergence of  
         
           CryptoKittles 
         
          collectibles back in 2016. The attention of mass media to art NFTs exploded in late 2020, early 2021, and was sparked by a series of events set in motion by prominent artists who converted their Web2-based digital content into unique Web3 NFTs. Beeple, a digital artist with over 2 million followers on Instagram, used Web3 infrastructure to tokenize selected Instagram pictures and sign them with his unique blockchain signature. He subsequently  
         
           sold his tokenized art collection for over 3.5 million USD 
         
         . One of the NFTs from this collection was resold at  
         
           Christie’s 
         
          for 69 million USD. Twitter co-founder Jack Dorsey minted his first tweet into an NFT via CENT, a decentralized application that allows anyone to register an “autographed version” of any tweet on the Ethereum network. The app allowed him to register the Twitter URL under which his first tweet is retrievable over the Web2, and to sign it with his Ethereum wallet, thereby creating a unique token that was cryptographically signed by him and could now be traded over Web3 infrastructure. His cryptographically signed tweet then sold for 1630.5 ETH, which at the time of the sale was worth an equivalent of 2.5 million USD. Around the same time, the  
         
           Nyan Cat meme 
         
         , a GIF that became popular in 2011, was remastered for its 10-year anniversary by the original artist who cryptographically signed his remastered GIF using Web3 infrastructure. This signed version of the GIF represented by an NFT was auctioned on the crypto art platform  
         
           Foundation 
         
          and was  
         
           sold for 
         
          300 ETH, which at the time of the sale was worth an equivalent of 590,000 USD.  
       
       
         This series of events set in motion a craze of issuing and buying art NFTs, sometimes for an inconceivable amount of money. As a consequence, NFTs were criticized for being overhyped, which – at that particular time – was certainly true. Hype cycles are neither unusual for new technologies nor in the art world. In fact the whole art world lives from overhyping an artist and driving FOMO (fear of missing out) for prices of an artist's work, so this is not necessarily a problem of tokenization, but an art world issue in particular. While I personally don’t see an added value in paying 2.5 million U.S. dollars for a digitally signed tweet by Jack Dorsey, or 69 million U.S. dollars for an Instagram picture by Beeple, others seem to think differently. But then again, I also don’t see the point of buying a Rembrandt or Picasso painting for millions of Euros, because I’d rather invest in something that I would personally perceive as more “useful.” However, enough other people have thought differently for centuries, and where there is demand and supply, there is also a market.  
       
       
         To understand the game-changing aspects of NFTs for digital art, one first needs to understand how state-of-the-art web architecture works. The client-server architecture (upon which the current Internet operates) does not allow us to distinguish original files from their copies. In the client-server Internet, a data packet is a data packet. One can only use digital rights management tools to limit access to that data packet and use legislation to enforce property rights and sanction pirating copyright-protected content. Blockchain networks introduced a useful new digital-native feature for artists, as it allows any content creator to create a hash of an original file signed with their private key, which is verifiable on a public and permissionless infrastructure that everyone can access. Whether or not certain prices might be overhyped, Web3 tokens have revolutionized the digital art space as they allow artists and other content creators to register and autograph existing digital files that contain their artistic work or unique speech with their private key, thereby creating a unique autographed version of that file which can be easily traded over Web3 infrastructure.  
       
       
         The uniqueness of digital art and the fact that files can be copied for free do not necessarily collide with each other. An Instagram picture could be publicly accessible for anyone to see over the Web2, but a collector could buy the autographed version of this digital art, and also get access to the original file in high resolution, as well as other rights the artist might want attached to their NFT. Creating a digital autographed version of a digital file allows artists and other content creators to build complex property rights, access rights or even governance rights into the token contract which could create interesting new dynamics in the art world. Whether or not it makes sense to create artificial scarcity for a digital file is probably subject to specific use cases and needs to be explored over time. Artists in the future might decide to make only paid copies available. Or they might decide to issue a freemium and premium version of their work. Only time will tell what new dynamics will emerge from the possibilities art NFTs provide.  
       
       
         Art NFTs are not limited to digital art only. They can also be useful in the context of physical artworks or performance art. Fine art, for example, is an asset class that makes up an integral part of the investment portfolios of many high-net-worth individuals. However, potential investors with less economic means typically have no access to this type of asset class, as the most attractive investment objects are expensive. Only high-net-worth individuals have the economic muscle to invest in this type of art. The pool of potential buyers is small, which results in low market liquidity of this asset class. To complicate matters, the maintenance of fine art is expensive and conducted in bunkers and usually not in the art investor’s living room. Furthermore, the buying and selling of fine art is currently accompanied by a heavy documentation process to prove the authenticity and provenance of an art piece, which is a prerequisite of guaranteeing value. The current system, therefore, relies on trusted third parties managing a patchwork of databases across the art supply chain, managing digital certificates that guarantee value, and also managing the physical artwork. The same is true for the music industry, film industry, and publishing industry – when it comes to settlement of royalties for artists, the current system depends on publishers, music labels, film studios, or online streaming services. Royalty settlement across all art industry branches mostly happens with a considerable time lag of several months to sometimes years. The process is inefficient and highly intransparent. Tokenizing the art and entertainment market could resolve many of the inefficiencies of the current systems, through tools like fractional ownership, provenance and digital rights management/settlement or crowdfunding. 
       
       
         Fractional investments 
         : Low-net-worth individuals, who have so far been excluded from this investment opportunity, would be able to buy a fraction of an expensive work of fine art. Some people argue that this could lead to an increased demand for high-end fine art investments, potentially increasing overall art prices and the production of new types of art, democratizing and diversifying the market. The main question that arises in this context is how to collectively manage the ownership of a physical piece of art. The artwork itself could, for example, be maintained by a custodian who has the experience to maintain an art collection, and the management cost would be borne by all of the co-owners. The payments would be managed and enforced by a smart contract. An early example for this was Andy Warhol’s painting “14 Small Electric Chairs,” which was first tokenized and then sold on the art investment platform “Maecenas” in 2018. The painting was tokenized and fractionalized on the Ethereum network, the ownership certificates were managed by a smart contract and became publicly verifiable, and could thus be traded on Maecenas. The film industry is another use case for fractional investment possibilities and automatic ROI settlement via smart contracts. 
       
       
         Provenance 
         : Tokenizing art could pave the way for a more transparent market where potential investors have access to verified artworks. Assigning provenance using tokens managed by a public infrastructure could resolve the challenges of conventional systems, such as counterfeiting. The current system relies on trusted third parties managing a fragmented patchwork of databases where these digital certificates are stored. Tokenized systems would use hashes and cryptography to publicly verify the provenance of artworks.  
       
       
         Rights management 
         : Tokenization allows for more transparent and direct management of intellectual property rights and subsequent real-time settlement of royalties for music, films, books or fine art. Artworks could easily be rented out to a gallery; musicians could collect their royalties faster. General revenue sharing between platform providers and artists could be managed by the smart contract on the fly as a song/movie/book was streamed or downloaded. The royalty fees could be settled directly, without publishers, film studios, or streaming services acting as intermediaries, who more often than not compensate the artists and contributors with a time lag of sometimes several years. Royalties could be settled in the form of tokens that are sent to the artist in real time, based on the number of people who viewed their art, streamed their movie or audio track, or read their post. Other revenue streams such as Youtube ad income could also be settled by art NFTs, as well distribution of access rights or community building tools such as NFT badges. 
       
       
         Crowdfunding/investing 
         : Art NFTs could also be used to crowdfund future art projects, if funders could own the art NFT as a fraction or as a whole. Anyone who contributes to the funding of a fine art project or film production could receive a proportional share of the tokenized value of that project once they accept the terms laid out in a smart contract. The artist could define the smart contract in such a way that they maintain a share of the artwork produced, while other token holders are free to sell their tokens on the open market, or alternatively cash out, should the piece be sold collectively. In such a setup, an artist could receive funding before production, while maintaining partial ownership of the art. Tokens could also allow galleries to pre-fund the purchase of artworks. 
       
       
         Derivative artworks 
         : The emergence of new derivative artworks could be another application of tokenized art. One could create convoluted smart contracts that give access rights to derivative artworks with the purchase of a real painting. One could add extra features, such as linking a digital file to the physical artwork, or integrating augmented reality features into the token – such as a video documenting the process of producing the art piece. Art NFTs can permanently connect physical artwork to its digital file, such that the digital file becomes part of the physical artwork and increases its value. They also open up new forms of artistic expression and value creation, like gamification, and could lead to the fusion of art, virtual reality, and gaming. 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 4: Collective Ownership & Micro-Investments 
       
       
         The exact business logic of a token contract managing fractional collective ownership or micro-investments depends on the use case. As previously outlined, an office building could be collectively bought by members of a co-working space, in which case the decision making could also be collectively managed. The tokens would represent property rights which come with voting-, management-, and access rights attached to them. The co-working space could be tokenized based on usage rights, where members would have a right to access a certain share of the space under specific conditions. Voting rights could be used to allow for governance. Management rights and duties could be used to delegate task management to certain members. 
       
       
         Collective ownership could also be useful for many NGOs or grassroots efforts. A community of neighbors could buy and collectively operate a renewable energy-powered micro-grid, as it is more realistic for a collective of neighbors to cover the cost than for an individual. The smart contract would send monthly revenues from the excess energy produced and sold to all members of the collective, in proportion to the shares they own. 
       
       
         A similar setup could also be attractive for taxi drivers. Many drivers lack the money to invest in their own car, and thus work for a company to provide the infrastructure. They have to share their revenues or pay a fixed rent to the vehicle’s owner. Fractional collective ownership tokens would allow several taxi drivers to collectively purchase a car, instead of renting it from someone, and split up the shifts as well as the costs and revenue resulting from buying, maintaining and using the car for their rides. A smart contract could collect a portion of everyone’s revenues, allocated for the respective expenses. 
       
       
         An investor in France could easily buy tokenized shares of a restaurant in Canada with a few clicks over a token exchange that specializes in micro-investment of Small and Medium Sized Enterprises (SMEs). An investor in Mexico could fund an apartment building in India using a token exchange that specializes in real estate NFTs trading fractional investment in individual real estate objects. 
       
       
         Collective fractional ownership tokens could furthermore manage the commons of a larger community, and settle the right of an individual to the benefits from community-owned assets. The country of Norway has granted their residents a share of their oil sales, either directly or in the form of wealth funds. Such a process could be tokenized to reduce settlement costs, while increasing transparency and accountability.If the Norwegian Central Bank would introduce tokenized Norwegian Krone at some point in the future, the revenues could be paid out in CBDC tokens. 
       
       
         
         
         Use case 5: Energy Tokens 
       
       
         Similar to the traditional financial industry, the current energy industry also has a complex reconciliation process of settling transactions across a web of stakeholders – from producers, to consumers, to infrastructure providers. Many of the stakeholders involved still use outdated private infrastructure, which is often not interoperable. A series of third-party intermediaries such as brokers or financial institutions are necessary to draft the terms of all necessary contracts and settle secure transactions to make the energy market seamless in the backend. This creates bureaucratic overhead that is ultimately also reflected in the energy price. Furthermore, the European Union and many other countries have started to legally mandate a decentralization of the energy production market. This new market structure will be harder to coordinate with traditional solutions that were designed for centralized systems. Web3 provides the ideal publicly accessible and globally available infrastructure for market players to coordinate in such a more decentralized setup. The tokenization of kilowatt hours (kWh) of energy could fundamentally change the dynamics of producing, distributing and using electricity, but only once all stakeholders in the energy industry have shifted to Web3-based systems and are equipped with the necessary hardware wallets to connect energy producers and consumers with blockchain networks. KWh transaction logic can be embedded in the token contract and automatically enforced and settled by a blockchain network that provides a publicly verifiable execution layer. This can reduce the operating costs of energy distribution, create more inclusion on the investor side, make energy  provenance more traceable, and help create systems that incentivize the reduction of energy consumption.  
       
       
         P2P energy trading 
         : While P2P energy trading units already exist today, the production and consumption devices along the supply chain of energy never interact autonomously or directly. They require an intermediary party – usually an energy provider – to settle all economic transactions between the producers and consumers of energy. In Web3, kWh that individual households produce and consume could be tokenized and settled over blockchain networks. An energy NFT could be created for each kWh of energy produced – it would represent the property rights to the  kWh and could include additional metadata about energy provenance. In such a setup, consumers of kWh can also become a producer of kWh, if they sell their excess production of solar energy generated on the rooftops of their houses via tokenized trading systems. This, however, requires that the smart meters – which are attached to all energy production and consumption units – come equipped with a hardware wallet. The hardware wallet represents the “last mile” from Web3 to the real world and makes the machines more addressable and machine data Web3-compatible. The hardware wallet provides a unique blockchain address to a production and consumption unit, which is critical as addressability is a prerequisite for smart meters to be able to autonomously settle transactions of energy traded and payments received. In such a setup, accounting- and tax-related data could also be automatically settled over Web3 infrastructure. 
       
       
         Micro-investments, micro-loans & citizen power plants 
         : Across the globe, regulators have been passing legal frameworks for the creation of local energy communities and micro-grids as a means to facilitate the transition towards more renewable and sustainable energy production. However, the current market structures seem to be inadequate to realize this vision. Consumers are practically excluded from participating in renewable energy production, since solar production units are capital intensive investments, and wind production units even more so. Individual consumers would also need to allocate time they might not have to deciding, buying, and managing their solar systems, and paying taxes on the revenues they generate. With tokenized micro-investments, renewable energy producers would not have to foot the investment and maintenance costs alone. Tokenization could allow individual households to become co-investors in alternative power plants operated by third party providers from which they could also buy their energy – consumers of alternative energy could become “ 
         
           prosumers 
         
         .” 
         
           
             
               29 
             
           
         
          Tokenization could allow anyone to make fractional investments in “ 
         
           citizen power plants 
         
         ” that are managed by specialized energy providers to the degree of their economic capability. The investment tokens in a citizen power plant could come with voting rights attached, to allow for co-governance. Alternatively, tokenization could allow individual households who decide to buy and manage their own energy production units to receive tokenized micro-investments – the co-investors would receive fractional returns settled by the token contract. Anyone could become a fractional investor or shareholder in the private infrastructure of another individual. A third use case could be tokenized lending, where any individual could grant tokenized micro-loans for the infrastructure costs on the rooftops of other producers, and in turn receive tokenized yield (i.e. interest), which could be automatically paid by the token contract in regular intervals. 
       
       
         KWh provenance & green purchase agreements 
         : Energy providers today either buy energy and/or produce energy from different sources (such as fossil fuel, renewable, nuclear power plants). They then store and pool these different energy sources in a way so that there is no energy shortage even at peak times or in case of downtimes. Consumers, 
          on the other hand, can choose to pay for renewable energies by subscribing to a renewable energy plan. However, this does not mean that the exact kWh that they receive actually came from a renewable power plant, even though investing in photovoltaic, solar, and wind farms is a key element to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and complying with the Paris Agreement on Climate Change. All energy produced from different sources gets fed into the same power grid. Different supply lines for different sources of energy into the power grid do not exist. If a consumer decides to pay a higher price and only consume “green energy,” there is no way to provide the consumer with information on where the energy actually comes from. The green energy contract only means that the energy provider has renewable energies in their total energy mix, and the consumers pay for the markup on these renewable energy sources. Tokenizing kWh along an infrastructure of smart meters which all have a blockchain wallet can ensure that the information about the provenance of a kWh will be attached to each single energy token that is swapped for money and fed into one's home. While energy is considered a commodity and therefore a fungible good, tokenization of kWh and the settleme 
         nt of these kWh over blockchain infrastructure can provide publicly verifiable metadata about the provenance of each individual kWh produced and consumed. In the case of energy from a grid, for every point in time one would be able to know the percentage of energy sources , with a clear distinction between sustainable and non-sustainable. In the case of power drawn from a decentralized grid (such as a citizen power plant which is based on renewables), the proof of sustainability would be 100%. This would make kWh tokens more unique and less fungible than their analog counterparts, as each tokenized kWh can come with additional metadata attached about where it was produced, how it was produced, etc.  
       
       
         Incentivize the reduction of energy consumption 
         : Tokenization can be used for incentive programs that aim to reduce overall energy consumption. Energy consumption behavior that can be broken down by household or even by device could help energy consumers monitor their consumption behavior – and change it. Based on this data, additional tokenized incentives similar to loyalty programs could be issued: households could earn voucher tokens that they could spend for other network services. Alternatively, these voucher tokens could potentially be converted into voting rights or other governance rights within an energy production network. 
       
       
         The prerequisite for the use cases mentioned above is that all industrial machines along the energy supply chain need to be (i) connected to the Internet and (ii) have a unique blockchain identity, and (iii) a new form of so-called “ 
         
           trust  
         
         
           anchor 
         
         ” 
         
           
             
               30 
             
           
         
          consisting of three components: a hardware wallet, a data logger and an energy meter. 
       
       
         
         
         Use case 6: Data Tokens 
       
       
         With the emergence of the Internet – especially in the case of Web2-based applications – data has become a valuable asset class. It has created an economy around behavioral data which uses data science to build AI applications on top of it. However, this novel type of data economy also faces many challenges around the (i) potential misuse of private data, as well as the (ii) availability of private data for data science purposes. Many valuable data science applications need access to private data. Precision medicine is a healthcare application that would need such data; traffic control and street map applications are another example where real time traffic data is used for suggesting alternative routes that help us to avoid getting stuck in traffic. However, in this novel data economy, many bad practices and pathologies surrounding the misuse of private data have emerged. Because of privacy concerns, more and more institutions and individuals are hesitant to make private data available. In addition to that, a  growing number of data privacy regulations legally prevents them from doing so. Aside from a handful of data giants such as Google, Amazon or Facebook, most institutions with data access don’t have the relevant expertise to build AI solutions on this data. On the other hand, many AI startups have excellent AI expertise – but little access to relevant data. Due to valid concerns over security, privacy and trust, data therefore often remains inaccessible to those who would like to build useful applications on top of it .  
       
       
         The “ 
         
           Ocean Protocol 
         
         ” aims to offer a solution to these challenges with a tokenized data marketplace that allows anyone to easily share and monetize data in a privacy-preserving manner, while maintaining the provenance regarding who published, purchased and consumed the data. Their goal is to build an open and permissionless data economy. All transactions around the data marketplace are recorded and managed by the underlying blockchain infrastructure that guarantees a tamper-proof audit trail. A set of smart contracts and other tools allows anyone to publish and consume data services by using two types of tokens – “data NFTs“ and “datatokens.” The marketplace allows anyone to tokenize datasets they own or manage by creating unique data NFTs and monetizing them. Data publishers can sell the property right to the dataset (data NFTs) – provided  it complies with local regulations – or grant access rights by issuing datatokens. All tokenized assets (data NFTs) and access rights (datatokens) can be managed P2P over blockchain infrastructure. The tokens could potentially also serve as collateral in other DeFi products. Data providers use the services of the marketplace to mint a dataNFT and allocate a certain number of datatokens to it; data consumers can purchase access rights to these datasets with datatokens. While the private datasets remain on the devices owned or controlled by the data owners, they can be accessed by paying third parties for computer processing in a privacy-preserving manner. This way, data scientists or AI practitioners can gain access to private and encrypted data that was previously inaccessible. Data publishers can monetize datasets that are useful to data consumers such as data scientists and AI developers. On the seller and buyer side, the access rights can be managed by a blockchain wallet provided by the Ocean Protocol, or any other EVM-compatible wallet. In this setup, the wallet also works as a data wallet, which means that it does not only manage the asset tokens but also the data access rights – all while preserving privacy.  
       
       
         Publishing data 
         : Data owners can post their dataset in an Ocean marketplace by entering the dataset’s URL, adding a title and description, and hitting 'publish'. This URL becomes encrypted and registered on-chain. Data owners retain control of their data, since the data never leaves their devices. The publisher can also use a Web3-based service such as IPFS for a content-addressable URL. Instead of a file, the publisher may run a compute-to-data service. The publisher then sets the price of the access rights that has to be paid in datatokens. To publish a dataset, publishers use the marketplace tools to create/mint/deploy a data NFT. In this process, the publisher also deploys a new datatoken contract (against the data NFT) to the chain, then mints datatokens.  
       
       
         Discovering data 
         : The market itself provides a visual discovery platform where buyers can see what datasets have been offered by the sellers. Buyers can browse, search, and filter data in a marketplace. 
           
       
       
         Consuming data 
         : Anyone can pay the data publisher with datatokens to get access to their data. To access a dataset,  datatokens need to be sent to the wallet of the data publisher to make a service request. The software then loads the encrypted URL, decrypts it, and provides the requested service (send static data, or enable a compute-to-data job).  
       
       
         The Ocean Protocol has also developed a mechanism for dealing with IP or privacy violations. Violations according to local law can be reported by anyone. On a technical level, the corresponding Ethereum address of the dataset and/or the publisher is tagged as “in purgatory.” Once tagged, these datasets or data publishers are displayed differently on the marketplace and their use and actions may be restricted. To resolve the issue, the data publisher has to submit a request in writing to the Ocean Protocol Foundation, who will then analyze the matter and decide whether to revoke purgatory status. The protocol is community-governed through the vehicle of a DAO. A more detailed analysis of this use case can be found in another book of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.” 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 7: Attention Tokens 
       
       
         When they started their operations, Web2 platforms, particularly social media platforms and search engines, did not have a direct business model to generate income from the services they provided. Eventually they understood that they could sell the only asset they had – a growing body of user data. They created new data-driven innovative marketing products referred to as “targeted advertising,” which fundamentally revolutionized the advertising landscape. This led to a so-called “ad-tech” ecosystem that is predominantly controlled by two companies today: Alphabet (Google) and Meta (Facebook). Algorithmic methods extract information from this raw data to evaluate which customers are the most relevant for which advertiser. While this has created more effective advertising methods, users today have little or no direct control over what happens with their personal data behind the walled gardens of Web2 servers.  
       
       
         Collecting lists of consumers, grouping them by specific characteristics, and selling these lists to marketing companies is not a new thing, but the Internet radically reduced the costs of collecting and processing such lists and allows a much more personalized approach than in the analogue world. Today, anything from our web-browsing history to the location-based data of our everyday movements is being tracked by the companies whose services we use. This data is then resold to the data brokers of the marketing industry. Paired with machine-learning applications, ads can now be personalized at an unprecedented level. Sophisticated methods have been developed to profile the behavior of Internet users – linking datasets that are collected by different companies across different user accounts, devices, and sometimes even offline data. This is done by using pseudonymous identifiers that refer to individuals, such as email addresses, phone numbers, and cookies. 
       
       
         As a result of all this, big data companies have become a honeypot for data breaches and privacy violations. The “Cambridge Analytica Files” that revealed how Facebook data was used to manipulate the British and U.S.  elections are an example of such practices. 
         
           
             
               31 
             
           
         
         
             
         
         Targeted advertisement combined with personalized data feeds is seen by many as a tool to undermine the autonomy of users and has also led to the emergence of opinion “echo chambers.” Furthermore, the advertising industry suffers from intransparencies along the supply chain of data brokers and service providers. Advertisers buying “custom audiences” have no direct insight into what really happens behind the walled gardens of ad-tech providers. They have to trust the third-party providers that ads are properly delivered. It is estimated that more than 7 billion USD in online ad fraud was committed in 2016 – from misplaced to outright malicious ads. In the current client-server-based Internet, both users and advertisers have little direct control over what happens with their data.  
       
       
         Tokenization could reinvent the advertising industry and Basic Attention Token (BAT) is one of the more seasoned projects in the crypto space which tried to tokenize advertising. It has established a system where advertising is performed P2P, in the “ 
         
           Brave browser 
         
         ” – a decentralized application that communicates with the Ethereum network and manages two tokens in the system: BAT (Basic Attention Token) and BAM (Basic Attention Metrics). Brave is a privacy-preserving browser by default and comes with inbuilt ad-blocking. The browser also provides an analytics dashboard to monitor and manage features such as incentive programs, ad matching algorithms, and attention measurement systems. 
       
       
         The BAT token 
          is designed to be used as a payment token between publishers, advertisers, and users in a way that (i) users are compensated for viewing ads in a privacy-preserving manner, (ii) publishers receive a bigger stake of the ad revenue than they would today, and (iii) advertisers can gain a better return on investment, as well as more accurate data. Users can opt in to earn BAT tokens based on their attention if they activate ads in their browser, which means that the browser shows relevant ads as system notifications based on their browsing history. 
       
       
         Basic Attention Metrics (BAM) 
          allows for the accurate tracking and reporting of user attention directly in the browser. In spite of the fact that the browser constantly tracks one’s attention, this data never leaves the user's device. In-device machine-learning algorithms determine relevant content for personalized advertising based on the attention data. The “attention value” for each ad depends on how long the ad is viewed, and other metrics, such as the number of ad pixels that are visible in proportion to relevant content. Data analysis is performed directly on the browser – this allows for targeted advertising without revealing the base data to the advertiser. Advertisers can have direct access to reliable metrics without the need for third-party tracking and without compromising the privacy of the user. This also improves the effectiveness of targeted advertising over Web2-based systems that rely on intermediary services.  
       
       
         Advertisers pay publishers BAT tokens to display personalized ads, which are filtered by the algorithm in the Brave browser, based on locally collected data only. This means that users maintain ownership and control over their data. When delivering an ad, the advertisers send BAT tokens in a locked state using a smart contract. If and when users view the ads, the smart contract unlocks the BAT tokens, which compensate the user with up to 70% of the advertising revenue. The publisher hosting the advertisement receives the rest, which could incentivize them to deliver relevant quality content instead of random spamming with irrelevant ads. Users can get compensated for their time and attention, and in turn, spend these tokens for other online activities, such as tipping artists and content creators for their free online content. This tipping option works in a similar way to services such as 
         
            “Patreon 
         
         ,” but without  the need for a third-party service. One could also use BAT tokens to pay for subscriptions, digital goods, and other services in the future.  
       
       
         While BAT and Brave provide a promising new system to monetize attention and guarantee more reliable target advertising, the current advertising industry might be the biggest bottleneck to BAT adoption. Google and Facebook currently dominate the ad-tech industry, with an estimated market share of roughly 70%. However, if Facebook and Twitter lose their user base, the tide might be shifting towards Web3-based social media services. For now, however, their large user base makes them popular among advertisers and publishers. While they offer advertisement options in-line with their newsfeed or search results, BAT currently only offers display ads, which is not as attractive to advertisers. BAT also started to offer DeFi services for swapping other tokens through their wallet, and this might in fact create more traction than the ad model alone. In the long run, it is quite likely that the BAT ecosystem or a similar attention token could become a mainstream method for micro-payments on social media, not only for advertising payments but also for rewarding content creation and content curation. Apart from BAT, many other projects have started to develop similar solutions, often with specialized content types of social media platforms. 
       
       
         
         
         Use Case 8: CO 
         
           2 
         
          Tokens 
       
       
         Over the last two decades,  
         
           carbon capture 
         
          and  
         
           carbon sequestration 
         
         
           
             
               32 
             
           
         
          services emerged as a result of a global attempt to fight climate change. They were the result of a series of international treaties such as the  
         
           Paris Climate Agreement 
         
          and  
         
           Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)  
         
         defined by the United Nations 
         
           
             
               33 
             
           
         
          and climate action demands made by individuals and institutions. These treaties and movements set in motion the creation of carbon markets where certification authorities issue CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates, which individuals and institutions can buy to offset their own CO 
         
           2 
         
          footprint – either voluntarily, or because they are mandated to do so. As a result, CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates have become a new asset class.  
       
       
         The term “carbon offsetting” refers to the act of funding the removal of  
         
           carbon dioxide 
         
          or other  
         
           greenhouse gasses 
         
          from the atmosphere. The unit of account that is used to measure the amount of carbon that is being reduced and offset is denominated in tonnes of CO 
         
           2 
         
         e (carbon dioxide-equivalent).  In the case of pre-selling of certificates, the revenues generated from the sale of CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates are used to fund carbon capture and sequestration services. In other cases, certificates are sold after the fact. The type of project that is typically funded with the selling of CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates is the production of  
         
           renewable energy 
         
          – such as biomass, biogas, wind or hydroelectric – or projects that support the reduction of consumption – such as energy efficient electric devices. Projects that contribute to the reduction of various types of agricultural or industrial pollutants such as landfill methane are also funded by  
         
           carbon offsetting 
         
          programs.  
       
       
         Carbon accounting systems are a prerequisite for CO 
         
           2 
         
          markets, as they intend to provide standardized measurement and accountability for carbon emission production and reduction. The term “carbon accounting” refers to the process of measuring how many units of  
         
           carbon dioxide equivalent 
         
          an individual or organization emits. At the time of writing, a public and global accounting system of who produces carbon emissions and who captures carbon emissions doesn’t yet exist. Some institutions such as nation states, local communities and companies already quantify their carbon footprint calculations in so-called “national inventories” and “corporate environmental reports,” and the adoption rate of such carbon accounting systems is growing. But these systems have not existed for a long time, and still need to be improved as they have their limitations – especially regarding measurement accuracy and trustworthiness. While the Paris Agreement and other international treaties created the conditions for such an accounting system and subsequent carbon offsetting markets, we are far from collecting accurate and trustworthy global data, and rewarding all those individuals and communities who naturally contribute to carbon offsetting.  
       
       
         At the time of writing, CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates can be traded on a growing ecosystem of carbon markets mainly divided into (i) mandatory aka compliance driven markets, and (ii) voluntary markets. The  
         
           European Union Emission Trading Scheme 
         
         , for example,  is a compliance market where public and private institutions such as companies, local communities or nation states can buy carbon offsets in order to comply with mandatory and legally binding limitations (caps) on the amount of CO 
         
           2 
         
          they are allowed to emit per year, given the international treaties they have signed. Failure to comply with these caps results in legal penalties. Voluntary markets, on the other hand, offer individuals and institutions the possibility to buy carbon offset certificates on a voluntary basis. Companies often buy certificates from voluntary markets for their “Corporate Social Responsibility” (CSR) reports, or to receive better ESG (Environmental, Social, and Corporate Governance) 
         
           
             
               34 
             
           
         
          ratings. These voluntary markets are offered by a wide range of certification programs such as the  
         
           Puro Standard 
         
         , the  
         
           Verified Carbon Standard (VERRA) 
         
         , the  
         
           Gold Standard 
         
         , and the  
         
           Climate Action Reserve 
         
         . Individuals and institutions can buy these certificates to offset their own (private or institutional) carbon emissions. As of today, only CO 
         
           2 
         
          has a market, but certain standardization bodies such as  
         
           Verra 
         
          are starting to include biodiversity factors into the equation when issuing CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates. 
       
       
         Carbon offsetting systems today are still in their early stages and face considerable transparency challenges. The current systems often suffer from information asymmetries and time lag regarding money flows and revenue sharing. Prices for CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates are seldom readily available and markets are illiquid. Carbon offset unit (COU) transactions are usually brokered over the counter through a web of middlemen. Individuals cannot get private accounts at the major carbon registry bodies, which excludes them from participating directly in these markets. Quality control of carbon capture and carbon sequestration projects is almost nonexistent, and emission reductions are often grossly overestimated. As a result, the quality of carbon offset can vary considerably. Another issue are so-called “zombie credits” where providers receive money for past projects that have already been accounted for elsewhere. Funding institutions need to combat carbon bandits to avoid the double or triple selling of carbon offsetting certificates. Incentives for participating in voluntary markets are driven by consumer trends around environmentalism with the aim to polish one’s reputation, and have so far often resulted in acts of so-called “greenwashing.” 
         
           
             
               35 
             
           
         
          Environmental, social, and governance (ESG) measures are incomplete, financial incentives are broken, and often don’t align with general market dynamics.  
       
       
         CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates that are issued over Web3 networks are publicly verifiable by design and create more institutional accountability for all stakeholders involved in carbon markets. Web3-based CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates and other sustainability proofs are currently being driven by a growing ecosystem of Regenerative Finance (ReFi) applications. The term “ReFi” has been adapted from DeFi (Decentralized Finance) and refers to a range of tokenized projects that address the different sustainability goals to combat climate change, support conservation practices such as biodiversity protection, or the creation of a more equitable and sustainable financial system. Most projects are currently focusing on CO 
         
           2 
         
          markets and tokenizing CO 
         
           2 
         
          certificates. While this is a great first step, “pure” carbon offsetting markets provide so-called “carbon centric” solutions, which do not resolve other important and related sustainability issues. Pure carbon offsetting systems do not prevent natural biodiversity destruction or wildlife crime. There is a growing awareness that carbon capture and sequestration is only one part of the equation of creating regenerative financial systems. An increasing number of institutions have started including biodiversity, water retention and similar ecosystem services 
         
           
             
               36 
             
           
         
          into the equation of such certificates. There is a growing awareness within the ReFi scene that the dependence on existing carbon markets – where the certificates are first issued – amplifies the issues of those markets, such as non-transparent credits, seemingly unlimited supplies of poorly verified credits, and many more. The topic of ReFi will be discussed in more detail in the next chapter. 
       
       
         Web3 tokens can also be used to create personalized incentives for individuals and institutions to reduce their CO 
         
           2 
         
          footprint through loyalty programs, without the necessity of participating in standardized carbon markets. A series of Web3-based applications have started to experiment with the question of how to reward individuals and organizations who can prove they have reduced CO 
         
           2 
         
          emissions with a token that is created (minted) upon such proof. Tokens can, for example, be issued to reward sustainable mobility behavior, incentivize riding a bike, walking, or using public transportation instead of using a car. Alternatively, one could be incentivized with a token for every “Proof-of-Energy-Consumption-Reduction.” Tokens can also be issued to incentivize the production or consumption of renewable energies. One could also be incentivized for planting trees (“Proof-of-Tree-Planted”), or cleaning a beach (“Proof-of-Bottles-Recycled”), or for the reduction of food waste – all of which indirectly contribute to the protection of biodiversity and maintenance of natural CO 
         
           2 
         
          sequestration services. Depending on the use case, such reward tokens are issued upon proof of CO 
         
           2 
         
          emission reduction; alternatively, other sustainability proofs could be exchanged for other services provided by the issuing authority and their partners. The incentive mechanisms can vary greatly. Tokens might be tied to the identity of a user (non-transferrable), or they might be designed to be tradable (transferable). They might be designed to expire after a while, or have unlimited durability. Examples for such projects are: “ 
         
           Earth Dollar 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Eco Coin 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Klima Thaler 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Solar Coin 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Sun Exchange 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Sweatcoin 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           Vienna Kultur-Token 
         
         .” The reward mechanisms rely heavily on somebody valuing the tokenized proofs. If these tokens are accepted as a method of payment or for discounted payments, they might become useful – but only if a lot of people come together and create critical network effects. 
       
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       Blockchain networks and Web3 applications enable the tokenization of any asset of the real and virtual economy. Any physical good or share in a small- or medium-sized company can be tokenized and subsequently divided into representative tokens, which could be traded on an open market – all this at a fraction of what it would cost in the client-server world.  ~ Tokenization of assets refers to the process of creating a Web3-native digital certificate for any physical object, financial asset or digital file. The tokens represent the physical or digital counterpart and are collectively managed by a public blockchain infrastructure or similar distributed ledger. The term “asset token” is an economic term that refers to any type of asset – whether digital or physical. The term “real-world asset” (RWA) is used to describe physical assets such as commodities, physical art, real estate, or securities. ~ Non-fungible tokens are unique in nature, and represent assets that have more complex properties than fungible assets. They come attached with a set of metadata that describe the unique properties of that asset and have a more complex rights management logic built into the token contract. NFTs can represent digital or physical art, they can also represent any other type of non-fungible asset or identity-based digital certificates.  ~ Tokens that represent assets of any kind can provide more transparency in marketplaces than existing financial systems currently offer. This could significantly reduce fraud or corruption along the supply chain of goods, services, and financial transactions. Due to the frictionless settlement infrastructure a public and permissionless blockchain network provides, the transaction costs of developing and managing the rights attached to the digital certificates representing these assets can be significantly lower compared to what it would cost in state-of-the-art systems. ~ “Security token” is a legal term that specifically refers to a type of asset token that is classified as a security according to financial market regulations. The interpretation of what constitutes a security, however, is subject to national legislation. They provide a new form of digital representation of existing securities, which come with embedded automated rights management. Security tokens settled by Web3 infrastructure can facilitate frictionless settlement processes, without sacrificing legal protection. Compliance comes built into the token contract. The token contract replaces the intermediary and executes the settlement process between sellers and buyers, which could reduce brokerage fees.  ~ The programmable nature of tokens also makes it cheaper and easier to formalize special conditions, which could allow for more personalized asset types that might have been theoretically possible before, but were not economically feasible to implement. Buying shares of small and mid-sized enterprises (SMEs) in foreign countries is currently almost impossible across borders – this could potentially become much more feasible over globally accessible Web3 infrastructure, but only once Web3 infrastructure becomes more adopted by mainstream institutions worldwide. ~ The tokenization of real estate refers to the process of creating a tokenized digital ownership certificate for an individual real estate object. The token represents the deed to a physical counterpart — such as an individual apartment, an apartment complex, or an entire office building. The deed to the real estate object would be issued as an non-fungible token – a “real estate NFT.” A token contract represents the deeds to the property and adds all relevant information in the form of metadata to the token – such as the size, the address, the price, the ownership history, and other relevant data. The hashed data of each deed can be registered on a distributed ledger to provide a universally shared dataset on all real estate related activities, such as previous owner or repairs conducted.
