The property right to the real estate object could then be divided into parts and sold to several (co-)owners.  ~ Even if a token represents a physical asset that is not divisible, as in the case of an apartment, the NFT representing the property rights is divisible. These fractional ownership tokens would represent the fractional property rights to the deed.  ~ Web3 tokens have revolutionized the digital art space as they allow artists and other content creators to register and autograph existing digital files that contain their artistic work or unique speech with their private key, thereby creating a unique autographed version of that file which can be easily traded over Web3 infrastructure. The client-server architecture (upon which the current Internet operates) does not allow us to distinguish original files from their copies. In the client-server Internet, a data packet is a data packet. One can only use digital rights management tools to limit access to that data packet and use legislation to enforce property rights and sanction pirating copyright-protected content. Blockchain networks introduced a useful new digital-native feature for artists, as it allows any content creator to create a hash of an original file signed with their private key, which is verifiable on a public and permissionless infrastructure. It allows for more sovereign rights management of intellectual property. ~ Web3 provides the ideal publicly accessible and globally available infrastructure for energy market players to coordinate in a more decentralized setup. The tokenization of kilowatt-hours (kWh) of energy could fundamentally change the dynamics of producing, distributing and using electricity. KWh transaction logic can be embedded in the token contract and automatically enforced and settled by a blockchain network that provides a publicly verifiable execution layer. This can reduce the operating costs of energy distribution, create more inclusion on the investor side, make energy  provenance more traceable, and help create systems that incentivize the reduction of energy consumption.  ~ Data, attention and CO2 certificates are other assets classes that can be tokenized, and upon which a range of marketplaces can emerge.

         Decentralized Finance (DeFi) 
       
       
         Decentralized finance (DeFi) is an umbrella term for a set of smart-contract-based applications where smart contracts replace many roles of classic financial intermediaries. Token transfers, tokenized credit and lending services, and other financial services are automatically executed by a public blockchain infrastructure based on the conditions formalized in smart contracts. A growing body of tokenized asset classes are becoming accepted as collateral in DeFi applications, including real-world assets, which in turn could potentially convert many assets or access rights of the real world into so-called “bankable funds.” 
         
           
             
               37 
             
           
         
          This chapter will outline how the tokenization of more and more asset classes, combined with a growing body of composable plug-and-play financial products, might challenge our current definition of what constitutes money, finance, and the real economy. ~ 
       
       
         Decentralized finance (DeFi) refers to a collection of Web3-based financial service protocols that were designed to operate autonomously without a privately operated middleman acting as clearing institution. The original vision was to build a more efficient, transparent, censorship-resistant, and open decentralized financial system using smart contracts. In a true DeFi setup, the role of the smart contracts is to connect all counterparties to a financial transaction, using conditional rulesets of who is allowed to do what in the financial environment provided by a specific DeFi application. The smart contract — or a series of smart contracts — computationally formalize(s) and automatically enforce(s) these rulesets, thereby replacing many roles of classic financial institutions. Token transfers, tokenized credit and lending services and other financial services get automatically triggered by transactions from one of the counterparties to the smart contract. If the conditions of a smart contract are fulfilled by the transaction, the transaction will be executed accordingly. The collectively maintained ledger of transactions acts as a permanent record for token ownership and token transactions, with more frictionless asset settlement than traditional financial services (TradFi) or centralized finance (CeFi) services operating on the intersection of Web3. The publicly verifiable nature of blockchain networks provides more transparency and accountability than centralized financial services as well as fast settlement execution. Individuals have full control over their funds, which means that they control their private keys and don’t have their tokens stored with a custodial service such as a centralized exchange.  
       
       
         The term “DeFi” was probably mentioned for the first time in 2018 in a  
         
           Telegram chat 
         
          between Ethereum developers and entrepreneurs. However, Bitcoin predates the term DeFi and was the original DeFi application long before the term was even coined. In 2008, the  
         
           Bitcoin Whitepaper 
         
          introduced the concept of an algorithmic governance infrastructure for financial transactions, using a “chain of blocks” that are cryptographically secured by a P2P network of participants. Bitcoin’s vision and open source code spurred an innovation cycle of open financial services that built on a publicly maintained infrastructure. Even though terms such as “blockchain,” “Web3” and “DeFi” were all coined many years after Satoshi published his initial Bitcoin Whitepaper, the concept of DeFi dates back to the conception of Bitcoin – which in turn was a result of decades of applied research: researchers and activists advocating  
         
           financial cryptography 
         
          collectively coordinated their findings over a bulletin board and a  
         
           mailing list 
         
          dedicated to financial cryptography. 
       
       
         Early developers of the first DeFi applications used the versatility of the Ethereum network to expand Bitcoin’s vision to a wider array of tokenized financial services where buyers, sellers, lenders, and borrowers could interact wallet to wallet — without the need for clearing institutions to mediate their transactions. Today, many DeFi applications are not wallet-to-wallet anymore, but wallet-to-smart-contract, where the smart contract (or a series of smart contracts) replace(s) the role of traditional financial players. Liquidity pools are a good example for smart contracts that have replaced traditional institutions via decentralized exchanges. This topic will be discussed in detail in the chapters  “Token Exchanges & NFT Marketplaces” and “Tokenized Credit & Lending.” 
       
       
         Over the years, a range of easy-to-use DeFi applications have emerged. While it all started out with tokenized P2P payment systems (aka Bitcoin), the innovation soon shifted to improving the privacy, stability or scalability features of Bitcoin. Alternative blockchain networks or cryptocurrencies were created – they often used the Bitcoin codebase which they modified to create more privacy-preserving, stability-preserving or more scalable cryptocurrency systems. One of the most notable innovations were privacy tokens, stable tokens and ultimately also the Ethereum network which provided an infrastructure to create any type of special purpose token with a few lines of code without having to create your own blockchain network. The next innovation cycle built on the Ethereum network. Innovators (and unfortunately also scammers) started to issue their own special-purpose tokens which were then sold via so-called ICOs (initial coin offerings) as a fundraising mechanism for Web3 projects. Many investors saw this as a get-rich-quick scheme and tried to invest in potentially promising technology early on. This culminated in the ICO craze of 2016 and 2017, where many projects used the smart contract features of the Ethereum network to issue tokens for fundraising purposes, thereby taking advantage of easy token issuance in a highly unregulated environment. Mixed in with many failed projects and intentional scams were also many useful projects, which used their raised funds to build additional P2P financial services such as decentralized credit and lending services, decentralized exchanges, and a growing body of P2P derivatives and P2P insurance services. The term “DeFi” started to gain traction in Summer 2020  – often referred to as “DeFi Summer” 
         
           
             
               38 
             
           
         
           – with the emergence of  many credit and lending protocols that promised high yields. This led to a DeFi boom, drove crypto prices up and even attracted many traditional financial investors and intermediary services. 
       
       
         From an infrastructure perspective, DeFi applications originally were predominantly built on the Ethereum network. An increasing range of DeFi applications are now being built on other types of blockchain networks and second layer networks such as “ 
         
           Avalanche 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Arbitrum 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Binance Chain 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Optimism 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Polygon 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Solana 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           zkSync 
         
         .” DeFi solutions that are built on the Bitcoin ecosystems are also well under way. However, the Ethereum ecosystem still seems to be the leader in the DeFi space, probably because of being a first mover and due to network effects resulting from their token standards, or EVM-compatible token standards, which makes the DeFi space more composable and allows for a more seamless developing environment and user experience. 
       
       
         From a regulatory perspective 
         , the DeFi space was largely unregulated in the early years. This Wild West environment attracted both developers with serious intentions as well as all sorts of tech-cowboys and scammers. The ensuing gold rush atmosphere fostered productivity and pathology alike. The lack of explicit regulation and know-how, combined with a lot of insider trading, untested smart contracts, as well as the complexities resulting from composable protocols, all combined with a handful of bad actors or power players, led to market asymmetries. A few people made a lot of money and a considerable number of retail investors lost a lot. The misunderstanding of what really constitutes DeFi, and how it is different from CeFi, also led to many market failures and scamming possibilities. 
       
       
         
         
         History of Finance & Potentials of DeFi 
       
       
         “Finance” deals with money, credit and capital assets – in the form of bonds, shares, stocks, options and futures – and is a subfield of economics. It includes personal finance, corporate finance and public finance. The main purpose of finance is to bridge capital flows (in the form of loan or credit) between those who have capital (savers and investors) and those who need it (individuals, companies and governments). Financial instruments such as money, credit and more refined forms of capital assets have evolved over millennia. Funds can be raised in different forms by taking out private loans, issuing corporate bonds or government bonds, or by selling equity of a company or corporation in the form of stock or shares. Lending is mostly brokered between borrowers and lenders through financial intermediaries such as retail banks, investment banks and different types of venture capital institutions.  
       
       
         Modern banking and finance most likely originated around the times of the first big settlements and city states such as the Babylonian empire, where palaces and temples started to be used for the safe storage of commodities and other valuables such as grain, cattle and – somewhat later – precious metals. The increasing trade between these settlements required increased forms of credit and lending. The writings in the  
         
           Code of Hammurabi 
         
          – a Babylonian legal text from 1550 B.C. – include laws that govern banking operations, such as the level of interest rates and the laws regarding who is allowed to give out loans against interest, and to whom. The first exchanges that resemble modern stock exchanges formed around the 16th century. In the late 19th century, the  
         
           global financial system 
         
          as we know it today started to take shape. It is broadly defined by a growing body of international legal agreements and institutions – such as central banks, multilateral treaties, and intergovernmental organizations – that regulate and manage the international flows of financial capital. The current global financial system was formed on the heels of mass migration, industrialization, technological advancements such as telecommunication, and economic globalization in general. Internet-based financial technology companies have been the latest addition to the stakeholder set of financial intermediaries in this global financial system. But Web2-based financial services were only the vanguard for Web3-based decentralized financial services, which are the next level of financial innovation. 
       
       
         The settlement systems used by the classic financial industry often still rely on outdated infrastructure. It is estimated that more than 40% of financial institutions today conduct  
         
           their asset settlement over COBOL-based systems 
         
           – a programming language from the 1950s that was predominantly used to program  
         
           mainframe computers 
         
         . Today, only a few developers still master this programming language. The concept of composability –  typical for smart-contract-based blockchain networks  –  is non-existent in traditional financial systems that build on private computer infrastructure such as mainframe computers. Transparency does not exist. Data exchange is process heavy. As a result, traditional finance involves a large bureaucratic overhead and is therefore cost-intense. It requires a range of intermediary services to address important functions such as (i) mitigating counterparty risk, (ii) market making, and (iii) securing assets from being stolen. Due to the nature of the client-server based Internet architecture, even modern Web2-based financial services providers are process-heavy and not transparent to their customers. While intermediary functions also need to be taken care of in DeFi, they are addressed with smart-contract-based mechanisms and executed on a publicly verifiable infrastructure with the following advantages: 
       
       
         Full control 
         : If users choose “self-custodial” wallet solutions, they remain in possession of the private keys and are in full control of their funds. Self-custodial wallets — which are the whole point of Bitcoin and all DeFi applications that followed — can potentially disintermediate many financial services that currently provide services to mitigate counterparty risk, act as market makers, or secure funds from being stolen. Token holders manage their assets with their own wallets, rather than having them managed by a bank or custodial services such as token exchanges that offer custodial wallets without giving private keys to their customers. However, full control also means full responsibility over managing one’s private keys. If you lose them, you lose access to your assets.  
       
       
         Instant asset settlement & globally accessible 24-hour markets 
         : Due to the public nature of distributed ledgers, DeFi applications are designed to be globally accessible by anyone around the world with an Internet connection and a Web3 wallet. Once the smart contract is deployed, DeFi applications self-execute with less human and institutional intervention — except for infrastructure maintenance such as code upgrades, bug fixes, and dispute resolution. Asset transfer or exchange is settled in a matter of minutes (depending on the block creation times and network congestion), as opposed to classic financial markets, where finality of asset settlement can take up to 3 days. 
       
       
         Transparency, accountability & open source business logic 
         : Traditional financial institutions work with private systems that are not publicly verifiable. They are only audited by third parties. Among other things, clients of financial institutions have to pay these institutions relatively high fees to get access to old bank records once they close their accounts. In DeFi, all token transactions are publicly verifiable, at all times, and free of charge — reducing market friction and increasing the interoperability of financial services. While this level of transparency has certain advantages in terms of institutional accountability, and open composable systems, it also raises very serious privacy issues which are addressed by a growing body of privacy-preserving payment solutions such as “ 
         
           Monero 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Zcash 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Tornado Cash 
         
         ” etc. Furthermore, the trading mechanisms of a DeFi application are encoded as open source code, which means that anyone can inspect how the system works – at least in theory. Loopholes can be fixed collectively and systems can be built on top of each other. In reality, however, one needs to be able to read the code and understand the economic dynamics of a market in order to be able to contribute to collective code maintenance.  
       
       
         Modularity & composability 
         : Due to the nature of blockchain networks, DeFi applications can be built in a modular way, at least among decentralized applications that operate on the same blockchain network. This modularity in combination with the open source nature of DeFi applications has led to fast-paced innovation of open and decentralized financial applications profiting from “swarm intelligence.” On the flip side, modularity also adds levels of complexity, which excludes many builders and investors who are not tech and finance savvy and can be front-run by more knowledgeable players in the market.  
       
       
         Pseudonymity 
         : Early DeFi applications, starting with Bitcoin, only required pseudonymous (blockchain) accounts without lengthy identification and application processes. This made Bitcoin and the successive landscape of DeFi services more inclusive for a large group of underbanked populations, which account for an estimated 25% of the adult population worldwide. At the time of writing, however, more and more DeFi solutions – especially those operated by privately owned companies or somewhat institutionalized DAOs – require a full KYC process due to regulatory pressure, which is not surprising as this is also mandatory for the traditional financial system. 
       
       
         Governance 
         : The governance structure and mechanisms of DeFi institutions are also public. Truly decentralized financial applications offer governance rights to their network participants – that is, the possibility for token holders to autonomously co-manage the DeFi infrastructure they are part of. As opposed to TradFi and CeFi, where the key actors are privately or government-operated institutions such as central banks, retail banks, investment banks and other types of payment processors or financial institutions, DeFi has created its own set of Web3-based, collectively operated financial institutions. DeFi applications can be owned and maintained by their users through some form of a decentralized organization whose bylaws are executed by smart contracts, often referred to as Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAOs). Their level of decentralization and autonomy may vary and in the worst case be non-existent or restricted, which is often a result of imbalances in the power structures, and raises questions of how decentralized or autonomous the respective DAO really is. In their best practice, DAOs have a number of financially sovereign and distributed stakeholders who interact independently or in tandem with each other. These governance structures are still in their infancy, and often not as decentralized as they claim to be. An in-depth analysis of governance structures of DAOs will be the subject of another book in the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose Driven Tokens.” 
       
       
         
         
         DeFi vs. CeFi 
       
       
         In theory, DeFi could give individuals more control and sovereignty over their own assets, and introduce more transparency into an emerging decentralized financial system as a whole. Full control is facilitated with self-hosted wallets and the public-private key infrastructure of blockchain networks. Transparency is a result of the nature of smart contracts and their underlying blockchain infrastructure.  
       
       
         While the initial idea of Bitcoin and DeFi was sovereign asset management, the reality of the vast majority of token holders today is far from sovereign. Most token holders use the services of financial intermediaries that have emerged around the Web3 – most predominantly, centralized exchanges (CEX). These centralized exchanges act on the intersection of the Web3 and are privately operated on private server infrastructure. They are not decentralized at all, nor do they operate on public blockchain infrastructure. They offer custodial services to their customers by managing their tokens with so-called hosted or custodial wallets. Their customers have no control of what happens to their tokens. Centralized token exchanges are therefore the “new banks” of Web3. They are prone to the same systemic risks as traditional financial institutions, with the added risk that until now, they have been poorly regulated in many countries. In the case of centralized exchanges and other centralized credit and lending services, customers cannot control whether their tokens are used as collateral for other financial investments without their knowledge, and whether they are potentially over-leveraged. 
       
       
         In DeFi, the information on the state of all tokenized assets that are held in collateral of the smart contracts, and information about how much these collaterals are leveraged to purchase other crypto assets, is publicly verifiable at all times. This means that all market participants can understand the current dynamics of the financial markets, because there are no backdoor deals. CeFi users, on the other hand, have restricted information, depend on the disclosure tactics of their financial services providers, and can’t identify potential threats. CeFi services often accept short-term tokenized deposits which they lend to others, leveraging tokenized positions at sometimes up to 20-to-1 ratios. Their operations are secured by business deals with counterparties that act as investors, based on paper contracts whose terms are not publicly verifiable. Customers rely on market rumors and on inside know-how, as they don’t have insight into the balance sheets of their banks or centralized token exchanges. They don’t know how their collateral assets are being managed, and whether they might have been under-collateralized in backdoor financial deals. 
       
       
         Many cases of embezzlement and mismanagement of customer funds that we have seen in 2022, as well as hacks of CeFi institutions such as centralized exchanges, are a symptom of systemic risks associated with private and centrally operated institutions that are black box to their clients. The collapse of  the centralized exchange “ 
         
           FTX 
         
         ” and their market maker company “ 
         
           Alameda Research 
         
         ” in 2022 are the best example for the lack of transparency and control over one’s funds using legacy financial infrastructure, and the resulting systemic risks of traditional finance. 
         
           
             
               39 
             
           
         
       
       
         The same is true for the collapse of the Terra(LUNA) stable token in 2022, which led to some cascading effects: CeFi service providers such as “ 
         
           Celsius 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Three Arrows Capital 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Nuri 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Voyager Digital 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           BlockFi 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           Genesis 
         
         ” and similar services that went bankrupt were venture-backed or bank-funded financial technology start-ups who had over-leveraged the tokenized funds of their clients, which is typical for CeFi institutions. As opposed to standard practice in traditional finance, these over-leveraged CeFi operators could not restructure or renegotiate their tokenized liabilities after the fact, requiring a change of the terms and conditions. In a smart contract world such backdoor deals are not possible, unless they are provisioned for in the contract, in which case the rules are transparent to everyone, and no investor gets preferential treatment over another. Many of these CeFi companies therefore collapsed. 
       
       
         Regulatory oversight advocates argue that better government regulation can mitigate these systemic risks – which has been true to a certain extent for traditional finance – but the whole point of DeFi is to use public blockchain infrastructure for preventing such risks in the first place, by baking the regulation into the smart contract. 
       
       
         Most people today still have problems distinguishing real DeFi from CeFi in order to be able to assess the strengths and weaknesses of both systems, which is a prerequisite for educated financial decisions – and educated financial reporting. This has led to the public misconception that “DeFi has failed.” The opposite is true. While the whole crypto space took a nosedive in terms of crypto asset prices in May 2022 in the wake of the Terra(LUNA) collapse, DeFi protocols such as Compound, Aave, Uniswap, MakerDAO did not collapse because they were not prone to the same systemic risks as their CeFi counterparts, especially as many DeFi protocols are over-collateralized by design. 
       
       
         This is not to say that DeFi is perfect or that all CeFi services are to be avoided. At the time of writing this book, avoiding CeFi services is not even possible. A positive development after the FTX fallout was that more CeFi services started using proof-of-reserve mechanisms and similar on-chain proofs to enhance their centralized services with the possibilities of blockchain transparency. “ 
         
           Kraken 
         
         ,” which is a centralized token exchange, was the thought leader who popularized this practice long before the FTX fallout. Many big centralized exchanges worldwide seem to be catching up now. In addition, DeFi has its own challenges which will be discussed in more detail in the following chapters. After all, Terra(LUNA) was a DeFi protocol (an algorithmic stable token) that collapsed, but in its aftermath it was mostly CeFi institutions that took the hit. Furthermore, the risks involved in investing in an experimental stable token such as Terra(LUNA) were publicly known. In the light of these obvious risks, the CeFi institutions that collapsed should have known better  – they should not have over-leveraged their positions, especially given their fiduciary duties to the customers whose tokenized assets they were managing. 
       
       
         
         
         Composability & Money Legos 
       
       
         Due to the open source nature and composability of the Web3 stack, DeFi protocols can easily plug into each other like lego pieces, which is why DeFi components were referred to as “money legos” in the early days of DeFi. By combining various DeFi protocols with each other, anyone can create completely new financial products without having to build all the financial components from scratch. Token holders can pick and choose financial infrastructure components – that is, money legos – to build personalized financial products on top of other existing financial components. “Stable tokens,” “decentralized exchanges,” and “decentralized lending” can be combined to produce a completely new real estate product. Any private person could – in theory, and once the regulatory environment allows it – tokenize their real-world apartment, and use the tokens as collateral for a P2P lending protocol specialized in real estate assets, all while avoiding the level of bureaucracy that would have come with legacy financial systems.  
       
       
         The concept of composability is not restricted to Web3 and it can be also found in other computer architectures, natural systems and (wo)man-made systems – from biology, physics, chemistry, human communication, and other sociological phenomena. In the absence of composability – when information and know-how are restricted – creativity and innovation are also restricted. In social systems or computer systems, such restrictions are created artificially:  through legal structures such as IP (intellectual property), behind the closed doors of offices, sealed papers, and walled gardens of server systems. Cartels are formed, and composability, creativity, and innovation are restricted as a result. Wherever composability exists, even in a limited way – as in the case of Web2-based Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) – it has proven to spur evolution and innovation cycles.  
       
       
         DeFi composability is an innovation booster for Web3 applications, similar to what APIs were for proprietary Web2 systems. APIs allow anyone to create interconnected Web2 services, on top of the data provided by other Web2 services. But Web2 
         - 
         based APIs are permissioned and highly complex, which means that they only allow others to access a restricted set of data that the owners of a Web2 service decide to share with other application programmers. In Web3, composability is  
         the  
         default mode.  
       
       
         C 
         omposability can also be a  
         double-edged 
          sword, as it can create security-, sustainability-, or privacy challenges which all need to be considered when designing DeFi applications. While composable DeFi applications are rapidly evolving, they are still nascent and often prone to exploits and attacks that could result from unintended programming mistakes that don 
         ’ 
         t correspond with the intended business rules. The governance logic of the underlying smart contracts needs extensive auditing and bug fixing before they are launched, especially in light of a growing and complex network of interoperable DeFi applications that will be automatically enforced and can create unintended domino effects.  
       
       
         
         
         Challenges of DeFi 
       
       
         While DeFi has much potential, it also opens a new can of worms of challenges that need to be considered when designing, regulating, or investing in such services. At the time of writing, the greatest challenges are wallet usability, secure inter-blockchain token transfer and economic attack vectors on smart contracts that often result from the complexities brought on by the composability of these DeFi systems. 
       
       
         Wallet usability 
         : Blockchain wallets are the equivalent of bank accounts or trading accounts in the traditional financial system. They provide addressability, identification, and secure authentication in a sovereign way. The wallet is a piece of software that communicates with the blockchain network, and from which a token holder can securely send and receive their tokens. Losing one's private keys equals losing access to one’s funds. While the tokens are still attached to one’s blockchain address, without private keys, one cannot authenticate and access them anymore. It is the account that one needs to interact with a DeFi application. At the time of writing this book, the usability issues of self-hosted wallets exclude many people who don’t have the time and mental bandwidth to understand this new form of sovereign asset management. Many token holders prefer the convenience of “not having to worry,” or might have also become somewhat used to the conveniences of password recovery mechanisms that Web2-based financial services offer. As a result, centralized exchanges and credit and  lending services are often preferred over truly decentralized financial services. This undermines the decentralization efforts, because it allows only tech savvy token holders and companies to build products on DeFi protocols and take advantage of innovative financial products. 
       
       
         Blockchain interoperability 
         : Blockchain networks and other distributed ledgers are isolated systems. They have no native mechanisms to communicate with each other. This means that one blockchain network does not have the information about the state of tokens managed in another blockchain network. Only the nodes within a specific blockchain network can securely communicate with each other and coordinate the state of all token transactions within their own network. Bitcoin tokens, for example, cannot be sent over the Ethereum network, because only the full nodes in the Bitcoin network have the information on which Bitcoin address holds which Bitcoin tokens. Interoperability efforts aim to create solutions where different blockchain protocols can communicate with one another, such that tokens that are managed by different blockchain networks can be transferred from one network to another. This is particularly important for DeFi protocols that are operated by one blockchain network, but would also like to include tokens of other blockchain networks. Without interoperability, DeFi applications can only offer financial solutions to token holders of one blockchain network, which would create lock-in effects and is contrary to the spirit of open access and decentralization of Web3. In a fully interoperable environment, a user from network A could send tokens to another user on network B without the need for an intermediary, such as a centralized exchange. However, as long as full blockchain interoperability is not given, exchange services will be needed to swap one token for another using the intermediary services of either centralized exchange, or smart-contract-based solutions of decentralized exchanges. While a wide range of interoperability solutions are being developed and deployed, many of these solutions are still being worked on and contain technical and economic attack vectors. 
       
       
         Exploitable smart contracts 
         : When the business logic of a smart contract is not completely thought through, or the code implementation does not coincide with the intended purpose, DeFi contracts can be exploited for unintentional use. Such exploits can only be conducted by technology and finance savvy individuals and institutions, to the disadvantage of less competent market players – i.e. the majority of retail investors. DeFi smart contracts would therefore need better auditing for a sustainable and inclusive decentralized financial ecosystem. Without quality control of the smart contract and auditing of the code regarding economic security, consumer protection cannot be provided. 
       
       
         Complexities resulting from composability 
         : While the composable nature of smart contracts makes them an ideal breeding ground for collective innovation, it also leads to complexities and in-transparencies that most market participants will not grasp. Only a few competent individuals and institutions are usually able to understand these complexities and/or have access to data mining services and AI tools to exploit potential unintended vulnerabilities in a smart contract. As a result, they can frontrun the rest of the market, to their own benefit and to the detriment of all others who don’t have the means to do so. There is much the DeFi (and CeFi) space can learn from the financial market crash in 2008 where – as a result of overly complex and entangled securitization-driven derivatives – the global financial system crashed because few people understood the cascading effects of the bundling of assets and the interrelation with other assets. A similar and not at all surprising market fallout happened in 2022 in the wake of the collapse of the stable token TerraUSD. This crash ultimately took down many CeFi institutions that had over-leveraged or under-collateralized their financial positions. As long as such power imbalances remain, “inclusion” and “decentralization” are relative terms in the context of DeFi. Power imbalances might shift once everyone has a personal AI assistant making computationally complex decisions for them, but this is a future scenario. For now, only high-net-worth individuals and institutions as well as a few financially savvy individuals have access to trading bots infrastructure and can front-run small-hold investors, which is also the case in traditional finance. 
       
       
         DeFi governance 
         : Many DeFi projects are increasingly shifting governance decisions from companies or foundations to special purpose DAOs. MakerDAO was one of the first DeFi protocols to shift governance away from their foundation to the token holders. CeFi services, such as centralized exchange  
         
           Shapeshift 
         
         , have also started transitioning to DAO governance. These projects often claim to do so with the purpose of “decentralizing” governance and making it more “community-based.” However, it is often unclear – or not adequately reflected – who constitutes the “community” and how the power structures are distributed. In most cases, it is not the community at large that makes the decisions – even though they are affected – but rather the community of protocol founders and investors.  
       
       
         Oracle problems 
         : Oracle services are crucial for DeFi solutions, as they supply data from outside the blockchain system (e.g. live asset prices) upon which the DeFi application operates. The smart contracts can then adjust lending and borrowing rates according to predetermined parameters. Oracles are intermediary services that retrieve, verify and transmit real-life data into a smart contract. However, the security, authenticity, and trustworthiness of third-party oracle services that deliver data from outside the system can be compromised. If the data is compromised, so will be the action of the smart contract. Most early oracle services were centralized, with all the typical counterparty risks of centralized services. Decentralized oracles services can mitigate this counterparty risk by retrieving data from multiple sources, and applying consensus mechanisms which rate the data sources based on their reputation and trustworthiness. Fully decentralized financial applications will need to build on decentralized and consensus-based oracles instead of trusting in centralized third party oracle providers. 
       
       
         Mempool attacks & MEVs 
         : In traditional finance, transactions are registered by the private computer systems of the financial service provider and are usually required to be handled on a first-come-first-served basis. Due to the nature of blockchain networks, transactions are validated in a different way. Tokens are sent over the network by creating transaction requests, which in turn are collectively verified, validated and propagated further by all participating network nodes. In the Ethereum network, which is one of the most widely used blockchain networks for DeFi applications, the network nodes pool transactions – in a so-called “mempool,” “memory pool,” or “transaction pool” – until they are included in a block by a miner or a validator. The same is true for blockchain networks that work similar to the Ethereum network. Since transactions are received by different nodes at different times, handling them on a canonical first-come first-serve basis is not possible. Additionally, to prevent spam and balance network loads, the person sending a token needs to pay a transaction fee to the miners or validators. This has created a sophisticated “fee market” over the years, where transactions of token senders who are willing to pay a higher fee are prioritized by the miners or validators. Since any network participant can store unfinalized transactions in their own mempool, they can analyze incoming transactions. This level of transparency started to be abused by malicious network actors. As a result, DeFi transactions that influence an economic state within the network – such as the exchange rate between two assets – can be manipulated: trading bots can be programmed to quickly transmit a new transaction with a higher fee, which puts them right before and/or after the target transactions, and create arbitrage opportunities from the price change. Such tactics are typically referred to as “front-running” and “sandwich attacks.” 
         
           
             
               40 
             
           
         
          Over time, miners realized that they could benefit from their ability to order transactions as they wished. By including, excluding, or changing the order of transactions during the block production process (either by fees or by other metrics), they can benefit themselves, instead of leaving the arbitrage opportunity to trading bots. This is referred to as “Miner Extractable Value” or “Maximal Extractable Value” (MEV). “ 
         
           Flashbots 
         
         ” is an intermediary service that was created to make this tactic a bit more inclusive. The platform allows searchers to submit MEV transactions to miners without revealing them to the public mempool. They offer auctions for blocks where anyone can become a “builder” by actively maintaining a mempool, analyzing it for arbitrage opportunities, building a transaction block that extracts as much value as possible, and then putting a price on that block offering it to validators (called proposers). Proposers could buy blocks off builders and generate a bit of extra income for themselves. While this mechanism is arguably better than having shady bots extract value from users, is still less than ideal – and does not prevent the extraction of value, it just redistributes it to a potentially larger number of market participants. MEV is not an issue in the Bitcoin network, but most DeFi applications currently don't build on Bitcoin. Bitcoin transactions have immutable self-contained outcomes. They boil down to a list of inputs spending existing UTXOs, and a list of outputs to be created. All the amounts involved are independent from addresses' transactions in the mempool, the position in the block, or even what block a transaction is included in. 
       
       
         
         
         ReFi, MachineFi & DeSci 
       
       
         The application scope of DeFi can build on a versatile range of tokenized assets and usage rights, and has forked into various subcategories that focus on special use cases such as Regenerative Finance (aka ReFi), Machine Finance (aka MachineFi), and the funding of scientific endeavors through Decentralized Science (aka DeSci). 
       
       
         Regenerative Finance (ReFi) 
          combines concepts of  
         
           regenerative economics  
         
         with decentralized finance. Regenerative economics advocates economic practices that take into account  
         
           negative and positive externalities 
         
         
           
             
               41 
             
           
         
          in the production and distribution of goods and services. The aim is to balance private and public interest, by taking into account the positive and negative impact certain economic practices have on  
         
           common goods 
         
         , such as the availability of clean water, clean air, forests, and other natural resources. ReFi has become an umbrella term for a series of tokenized projects and DAOs aiming to use the advantages of blockchain networks and tokenization to address a series of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): to combat climate change, support conservation practices, or creating a more equitable and sustainable financial system. Some examples have already been mentioned in the analysis of CO 
         
           2 
         
          tokens in the previous chapter. At the time of writing, many ReFi projects focus on CO 
         
           2 
         
          reduction solutions and voluntary carbon markets through on-chain proofs such as Proof-of-Carbon-Emission-Reduction or Proof-of-Biodiversity-Protection, to bring more real time accountability into carbon markets and other sustainability proofs. Examples of ReFi initiatives are “ 
         
           Celo Climate Collective 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Katalyst.Earth 
         
         ,”“ 
         
           KlimaDAO 
         
         ,”“ 
         
           Open Forest Protocol 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Toucan Protocol 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Reneum 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           ReFiDAO 
         
         .” 
       
       
         Machine Finance (MachineFi) 
          is a subarea of DeFi that focuses on making machine resources and machine intelligence Web3 compatible so that machines can seamlessly interact with DeFi applications, creating a more decentralized machine economy. The prerequisite for MachineFi is the combination of a set of technologies such as blockchain networks and other Web3 protocols with internet connected machines, artificial intelligence and other emerging technologies. Any IoT (Internet Of Things) device that has a unique blockchain identity could, in theory, settle machine transactions autonomously over DeFi protocols. Machines can deliver proofs of real-world activity (machine productivity) or status (such as presence) and can become intelligent and autonomous economic agents that interact according to the rules set in a DeFi protocol. Tokenization of machine resources has many aspects, from co-funding capital intensive machines with high upfront costs, such as solar power plants, to the generation of a set of productivity and sustainability proofs that these machines could provide. The co-funding of machines through co-ownership tokens can make industrial assets more inclusive than in our current economic system. Examples were laid out in the previous chapter when describing the use case of KWh tokens. The composable nature of DeFi protocols can create an array of new products and services, and participation potential for completely new asset classes.  Examples for protocols working on MachineFi are “ 
         
           RDDL 
         
         ,”  
         
           IoTex 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           Peaq 
         
         .”  
       
       
         Decentralized Science (DeSci) 
          is a movement within Web3 that uses blockchain networks and other Web3 protocols to generate higher institutional accountability over the funding, research, dissemination, and rights management process for scientific research and its outputs. Among other things, DeSci allows for more diverse funding sources and mechanisms, and an open funding process with less time lag. Examples for new tools and tokenized incentive mechanisms that are being experimented with are alternative voting concepts such as “ 
         
           quadratic voting 
         
         ” and alternative forms of investment funding such as “ 
         
           retroactive public goods funding 
         
         .” Examples of DeSci initiatives and protocols are “ 
         
           VitaDao 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           SCINET 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Blockchain for Science 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           DeSci Foundation 
         
         .” 
       
       
         
         
         Bankable Funds & the Future of Finance 
       
       
         Web3 tokens represent a new heterogenous asset class that can fulfill a diverse range of economic functions. They can represent property rights in the form of money (payment tokens), securities (security tokens), or any other fungible asset (asset tokens), but also a series of non-fungible asset tokens (NFTs). We are currently witnessing a growing body of tokenized asset classes, including the tokenization of real-world assets, where the tokenized property rights to these assets can become easily tradable. By creating a tokenized digital twin 
         
           
             
               42 
             
           
         
          for real-world asset classes – such as real estate, kWhs or physical art – the property rights to these assets can become easily exchangeable over token exchanges, and they can also be used as collateral in DeFi applications. The term “digital twin” in the context of tokenization refers to tokenized certificates that represent the property rights to the physical or digital counterpart of an asset which are collectively managed by all participants of the underlying blockchain network upon which the token has been issued. 
       
       
         DeFi protocols have already started to contribute to an increase in liquidity for existing native crypto assets, and can do the same for real-world assets and digital files of the Web2, once these assets become tokenized. As described throughout some examples of the previous chapter, DeFi protocols could potentially convert many assets or access rights of the real world that were previously “non-bankable” into “bankable funds.” Bankable funds are forms of payment that are accepted at financial institutions and easily liquidated into local currencies such as checks and money orders. Art NFTs, real estate NFTs, energy NFTs or data NFTs can only become a potential medium of exchange under the condition that they can be converted into cash with short notice using a DeFi protocol, or if they become increasingly accepted by online merchants as a direct method of payment. This will only be feasible if a regulatory environment of international financial markets supports the process, and once mass adoption of the Web3 manifests and necessary network effects kick in. Technical challenges such as wallet usability and blockchain interoperability will also need to be resolved.  
       
       
         Once the P2P swapping of tokens is possible across blockchain networks, anyone will be able to exchange any token P2P, wallet to wallet, without any intermediary – but only under the premise that self-hosted wallets are easily usable and will not be prohibited by regulatory frameworks worldwide. While some governments have already started to pass legislation in this direction, this cultural battle has not been decided yet. The tokenization of real-world assets and their subsequent collateralization in DeFi protocols could, therefore, contribute to the merging of the money system with the financial system and the real economy – making their distinctions increasingly difficult. It could also impact the role of central banks as geographic monopolists of money. 
       
       
         Many economists remain skeptical that cryptographic tokens can permanently replace conventional currencies since: (i) There are strong network externalities that favor existing conventional currencies; (ii) There is also a lack of sophisticated token supply rules leading to socially desirable stability and liquidity of tokenized economies. Furthermore, (iii) from a classic economic perspective, it seems impossible to pre-specify socially optimal “lender of last resort” rules in a smart contract. The term “lender of last resort” refers to a safety-net institution, usually provided by central banks, to reduce the risk of a lack of liquidity of financial systems as a result of financial panics and bank runs. It represents government-guaranteed liquidity to financial institutions. The lack of such a lender of last resort makes financial systems susceptible to financial crises and systemic panics. 
       
       I am confident that economic methods and practices that meet these challenges will find their way into the smart contracts and token governance rules of future token systems. We can already see this happening in the cases of stable tokens, in spite of the economic design challenges these tokens still face. Furthermore, many central banks are currently looking into tokenizing their central bank currencies (CBDCs). This development might in fact also create the legal and technical framework for mass adoption of Web3 wallets and tokenization. Adequate legal frameworks will be needed as these new structures transition from an early innovation phase to a more mature infrastructure phase.  
       
         
         
         ￼ 
         
           
         
       
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       Decentralized finance (DeFi) is an umbrella term for a set of smart-contract-based applications where smart contracts replace many roles of classic financial intermediaries. Token transfers, tokenized credit and lending services, and other financial services are automatically executed by a public blockchain infrastructure based on the conditions formalized in smart contracts. ~ The collectively maintained ledger of transactions acts as a permanent record for token ownership and token transactions, with more frictionless asset settlement than traditional financial services (TradFi) or centralized finance (CeFi) services operating on the intersection of Web3. The publicly verifiable nature of blockchain networks provides more transparency and accountability than centralized financial services as well as faster settlement execution.  ~ The role of the smart contracts is to connect all counterparties to a financial transaction, using conditional rulesets of who is allowed to do what in the financial environment provided by a specific DeFi application. The smart contract — or a series of smart contracts — computationally formalize(s) and automatically enforce(s) these rulesets, thereby replacing many roles of classic financial institutions. If the conditions of a smart contract are fulfilled by the transaction, the transaction will be executed accordingly. Individuals have full control over their funds, which means that they control their private keys and don’t have their tokens stored with a custodial service such as a centralized exchange.  ~ DeFi applications can be built in a modular way due to the interoperable nature of token standards, at least among decentralized applications that are built on the same blockchain network. This modularity in combination with the open source nature of these financial applications has led to fast-paced innovation of financial applications profiting from “swarm intelligence.”  ~ Composability can also be a double-edged sword, as it can create security-, sustainability-, or privacy challenges which all need to be considered when designing DeFi applications. While composable DeFi applications are rapidly evolving, they are still nascent and often prone to exploits and attacks that could result from unintended programming mistakes that don’t correspond with the intended business rules. The governance logic of the underlying smart contracts needs extensive auditing and bug fixing before they are launched, especially in light of a growing and complex network of interoperable DeFi applications that will be automatically enforced and can create unintended domino effects. Modularity also adds levels of complexity, which excludes many users who are not tech and finance savvy and can be front-run by more knowledgeable players.  ~ The governance structure and mechanisms of DeFi institutions are also public. Truly decentralized financial applications offer governance rights to their network participants – that is, the possibility for token holders to autonomously co-manage the DeFi infrastructure they are part of. As opposed to CeFi, where the key actors are privately or government-operated institutions such as central banks, retail banks, investment banks and other types of payment processors or financial institutions, DeFi has created its own set of Web3-based, publicly operated financial institutions. DeFi applications can be owned and maintained by their users through some form of a decentralized organization whose bylaws are executed by smart contracts, often referred to as Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAOs). Their level of decentralization and autonomy may vary and in the worst case be non-existent or restricted, which is often a result of imbalances in the power structures, and raises questions of how decentralized or autonomous the respective DAO really is.  ~ While the initial idea of Bitcoin and DeFi was sovereign asset management, the reality of the vast majority of token holders today is far from sovereign. Most token holders use the services of financial intermediaries that have emerged around the Web3 – most predominantly, centralized exchanges (CEX).  ~ CeFi institutions act on the intersection of the Web3 and are privately operated on private server infrastructure. They are not decentralized at all, nor do they operate on public blockchain infrastructure. They offer custodial services to their customers by managing their tokens with so-called hosted or custodial wallets. Their customers have no control of what happens to their tokens. Centralized token exchanges are therefore the “new banks” of Web3. They are prone to the same systemic risks as traditional financial institutions, with the added risk that until now, they have been poorly regulated in many countries. CeFi services could mitigate these risks with on-chain proof-of-reserves. ~ In DeFi, the information on the state of all tokenized assets that are held in collateral of the smart contracts, and information about how much these collaterals are leveraged to purchase other crypto assets, is publicly verifiable at all times. This means that all market participants can understand the current dynamics of the financial markets, because there are no backdoor deals. CeFi users, on the other hand, have restricted information, depend on the disclosure tactics of their financial services providers, and can’t identify potential threats.  ~ While DeFi has much potential, it also opens a new can of worms of challenges that need to be considered when designing, regulating, or investing in such services. At the time of writing, the greatest challenges are wallet usability, secure inter-blockchain token transfer and economic attack vectors on smart contracts that often result from the complexities brought on by the composability of these DeFi systems. ~ The application scope of DeFi can build on a versatile range of tokenized assets and usage rights, and has forked into various subcategories that focus on special use cases such as Regenerative Finance (aka ReFi), Machine Finance (aka MachineFi), and the funding of scientific endeavors through Decentralized Science (aka DeSci). ~ Web3 tokens represent a new heterogenous asset class that can fulfill a diverse range of economic functions. By creating a tokenized digital twin for real-world asset classes – such as real estate, kWhs or physical art – the property rights to these assets can become easily exchangeable over token exchanges, and they can also be used as collateral in DeFi applications. DeFi protocols could potentially convert many assets or access rights of the real world that were previously “non-bankable” into “bankable funds.” ~ Art NFTs, real estate NFTs, energy NFTs or data NFTs can only become a potential medium of exchange under the condition that they can be converted into cash with short notice using a DeFi protocol, or if they become increasingly accepted by online merchants as a direct method of payment. This will only be feasible if a regulatory environment of international financial markets supports the process, and once mass adoption of the Web3 manifests and necessary network effects kick in. Technical challenges such as wallet usability and blockchain interoperability will also need to be resolved.

Where possible, the links will be updated on a regular basis to prevent the issue of broken links. It is only scannable on white or light colored backgrounds and might not work on dark backgrounds. The website is also accessible under following URL:  
         
           https://bit.ly/3m10zh7
