         Token Sales 
       
       
         
         As opposed to native blockchain tokens that are minted upon successful validation and 
         / 
         or mining of a block of transactions in regular intervals (such as in Proof-of-Work or Proof-of-Stake), token sales introduced a fundraising mechanism for Web3 projects where tokens are issued against a direct financial investment, often before the project is even operational. Smart contracts are used to issue newly minted blockchain tokens in exchange for payment tokens, entirely on a 
           
         P2P basis. While token sales disintermediated fundraising mechanisms for a  
         few brief years 
         , the  
         shape  
         of token sales has evolved over time and has become re-intermediated  
         – 
          mainly due to regulatory requirements. ~ 
       
       
         Token sales allow the issuance of newly minted project tokens in exchange for other tokens using smart contracts. They introduced a mechanism for pre-issuing tokens against a direct financial investment, before the project becomes operational. Tokens would be minted before the launch of a project, and issued to investors. In such a setup, a certain portion of the token supply, or the entire token supply, was released to network participants before the launch of a project, in many cases even before any code was written. In addition to being a crowd financing tool, token sales also introduced new mechanisms for creating a minimum viable economy. This was very different from how the Bitcoin economy started: Bitcoin never had a token sale, and Bitcoin tokens were never pre-issued, but have been, and still are, continuously minted upon Proof-of-Work, each time a block of transactions is created. The first token sales were referred to as Initial Coin Offerings (ICOs). As the term “token” entered the mainstream, “token offerings” or ITO (Initial Token Offerings), and in the specific cases of securities, STO (Security Token Offering), became the accepted term of the hour. Soon, alternative forms with alternative descriptions followed, such as Reverse ICOs, Equity Token Offering (ETOs), Initial Exchange Offerings (IEOs), Initial Dex Offering (IDO), Simple Agreement for Future Tokens (SAFT), or DAICOs. For the purpose of generalization, I use the term “token sales” throughout this book.  
       
       
         The main idea of token sales was to raise money for Web3 projects by pre-selling tokens to supporters. These tokens would typically be paid for with BTC and ETH, as those were most easily exchangeable for fiat currencies at the time of these early token sales. Unlike highly regulated Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), or the practice of token sales today, many of the early ICOs were conducted without lawyers, financial intermediaries, or regulatory approval, and therefore seemed more similar to crowdfunding. Token sales first became popular after the Ethereum network went live, because it provided a public infrastructure that made it easy for anyone to issue new tokens and exchange these newly issued tokens against other forms of payment tokens with relatively little effort. Investors were often crypto enthusiasts rather than professional investors. Over time, professional investors also became interested, probably as a result of the high returns on investments that were possible in the bull markets of 2015 to 2017 – this led to the first mainstream crypto boom cycle. In hindsight, token sales were probably the first decentralized financial application beyond P2P money transfer, before the term DeFi even became popularized.  
       
       
         Eventually, as the space matured, economic and regulatory realities set in. Intermediaries such as exchanges and other service providers started to emerge. These services facilitated investor relations and the process of meeting regulatory requirements, but also re-intermediated the process of issuing tokens. While the first ICOs were truly P2P, where tokens were swapped between a project’s blockchain address (wallet) and the investors’ blockchain address, token sales today are conducted by new, highly specialized financial intermediaries. 
       
       
         
         
         History of Token Sales 
       
       
         The first token sale was conducted in 2013, when the founders of “ 
         
           Mastercoin 
         
         ” issued newly minted Mastercoin tokens against the payment of Bitcoin tokens. The sale happened entirely on a P2P basis and was settled over the Bitcoin network. It ended up raising around 500,000 USD worth of BTC for the Mastercoin project. The success of this fundraising campaign inspired other projects that followed to also use the Bitcoin blockchain for P2P crowdfunding purposes. In 2014, the Ethereum project conducted a token sale that lasted forty-two days and raised around 18 million USD worth of BTC, breaking all crowdfunding records at the time. This initial crowdfunding money was used to develop the Ethereum Whitepaper into an operational blockchain network. Once operational, the Ethereum network allowed the creation of a decentralized application for any type of P2P value exchange, using a smart contract with just a few lines of code. This smart contract functionality later became popular among other developers and entrepreneurs conducting token sales, as Ethereum smart contracts simplified the process of issuing and trading newly issued tokens for other tokens. This sparked a series of record-breaking token sales mainly conducted in 2016 and 2017. 
       
       
         “TheDAO” – a decentralized investment fund – was an example of one of the earliest token sales conducted on the Ethereum blockchain. Resulting in the biggest token sale at its time, TheDAO collected an equivalent of 150 million USD in ETH within a four-week time period. Everybody willing to invest was guaranteed a proportional share of the future revenues of that decentralized investment fund. However, TheDAO experiment ended prematurely with a spectacular and highly controversial draining of project tokens, due to a vulnerability in the smart contract. This was dealt with by a hard fork of the Ethereum network to reverse the single transaction that started the draining of the funds. This action was criticized by many advocates of the ideas that “code is law” and “immutability of the blockchain.” Heated political discussions followed and ultimately made the Web3 space more aware of necessary security audits of smart contracts, as well as questions regarding the maintenance and governance of decentralized applications. The scope of the fundraising success of TheDAO, paired with the dramatic events and controversial hard fork of the Ethereum network that followed, brought a lot of mainstream attention to this new type of fundraising vehicle and inspired many more projects to raise funds through the mechanism of a token sale.  
       
       
         While most of the earlier token sales had experienced engineers fundraising for research and development of alternative blockchain protocols, ICOs now became a fundraising vehicle for any type of project, some of which were not even related to blockchains or the Web3. Many of these newer token sales launched without any serious business plan, merely marketing a simple idea without any proof of expertise or development. Often, the role and function of the token was unclear, giving the impression that a token was merely issued for fundraising purposes on a simple marketing promise. These token sales were often opaque about the type of token they issued, as well as the purpose and economic design of the token. In these Wild West years of the Web3, it was hard to identify whether token sales resembled crowdfunding or crowd-investing vehicles, since they often bore the characteristics of both. As opposed to typical crowdfunding – where the investment is considered to be either a donation or a pre-buy of a product – early token sales gave supporters the chance of a return on their investment.  
       
       
         Before launching a token sale, developers would present a so-called whitepaper describing the technical specifications of a project. As more and more projects started to discover token sales as a tool for conducting fundraising – even projects with little overlap with core Web3 development – the whitepapers started to resemble business plans and often lacked technical or economic specification of how the teams wanted to make their projects happen or how the token economics really worked. This led to many intentional and unintentional scams where many investors lost a lot of money. 
       
       
         During the ICO boom of 2016-17, more than 800 token sales were conducted, raising an equivalent of about 20 billion U.S. Dollar, mostly based on a simple promise and often on no product at all. 
         
           
             
               43 
             
           
         
          Some spectacular token sales single-handedly raised over a billion, such as Telegram (1.7 billion USD) 
         
           
             
               44 
             
           
         
          or EOS (4.1 billion USD). 
         
           
             
               45 
             
           
         
          Many of the token sales were oversubscribed, and sold out within a matter of minutes or seconds, which means that it is likely that potentially much more money could have been raised. After this two-year rally, the market sentiment started to change in 2018. Most tokens ended up having a much lower valuation than the total money raised, often just within a few weeks or months after successful fundraising. At some point, many tokens only had value if investors could get in early and buy at a discount, just to sell right after the public token sale ended and at a higher price, hoping that someone else would buy their tokens for more than they paid, which was often the case. In finance and economics, this phenomenon is referred to as “greater fool theory.” So-called pump-and-dump tactics proliferated, where coordinated groups manipulated prices on mostly illiquid tokens. Coordinated buying action can create artificial demand for a token, which drives prices up quickly, in order to make it appear as the new rising star to outsiders. As they were trying to profit from this sudden price hike, the insiders of the group would quickly sell their tokens before prices fell. While such schemes are illegal in most jurisdictions, persecution was difficult, as identities were easier to obfuscate than in regular financial markets, which are also prone to such tactics. 
       
       
         Another challenge that many of the early project founders faced was the lack of experience in portfolio management, since most early project founders were engineers – not entrepreneurs or asset managers. This resulted in a lack of managerial scrutiny and a high burn rate of the funds raised in a token sale, as many of the funds were raised in highly volatile tokens such as BTC or ETH. Securing the value of these raised funds through adequate portfolio management is the job of an asset manager, not of a blockchain engineer, and many projects failed, or almost went into bankruptcy, just because of that. The Ethereum project, for example, successfully raised 18 million USD. However, as a result of a price drop in BTC in the weeks following their token sale (from 600 to 200 USD), the 18 million USD dropped to 6 million. Ethereum Classic development project (ETCDEV) had similar problems when their token price plummeted and left them with no funding. Steemit is another project that had to cut their team due to value loss of tokens. 
       
       
         According to some statistics, more than 70% of projects turned out to be intentional or unintentional scams that could not or would not allocate their received funds as promised. The Wild West days were over when regulators became more aware of token sales. The project founders issuing tokens realized that they had to be more precise in their definition of the purpose and the properties of their tokens. The teams needed to identify whether the token constituted a security or not, and to ascertain which tax related questions applied and what other laws would be relevant in the context of the token sale and the ongoing operation of their tokenized applications they were building. The level of scrutiny rose with time, especially as institutional investors entered the crypto market. As investors became more critical and regulators started to be more vigilant, the market consolidated and crypto prices declined. The market consolidated and flushed out many non-viable projects. 
       
       
         A range of service providers began to specialize in what regulators refer to as “security tokens” – from lawyers, to investment advisers, to insurers and custodians. The impact of KYC and AML requirements, as well as securities legislation worldwide, dampened the attractiveness of ICOs. Projects need a registered company backing their ICO and needed to deal with a lot of bureaucratic overhead, which ultimately became too much of a hassle.  
       
       
         Because of the legal and marketing challenges involved, Web3 projects started to target institutional and “authorized investors” instead of private investors for fundraising purposes. Token sales subsequently shifted from public token sales to a pre-sale that was limited to a small number of wealthy investors before the launch of a public sale. Over time, the increase of private pre-sales outgrew the public token sale process. Institutional investors were a more homogenous investor class, which could also support the project founders with non-financial means – through market experience and connections. These VCs would receive the tokens at a discount price, ahead of public trading, and would very often dump them once public trading became available. These so-called “pump-and-dump schemes” went on until more sophisticated vesting mechanisms, typical to traditional Venture Capital investments, were introduced. As ICOs died out, the third party providers that had facilitated them – often asking a 30% commission on the final amount of tokens sold – also went out of business.  
       
       
         While this introduced a more professional fundraising environment for founders of Web3 projects, it also led to the re-centralization of traditional startup investments. The P2P character of investments vanished, and only institutional investors with a financial market license were included in early token sales. Some argue that this created a protection layer for the average investor, since the projects would get vetted and supported by professional investors – who could afford to lose all the money they put into a project – before it was ready to launch to average smallhold investors. The FTX collapse in 2022 shows that this argument does not necessarily hold: a range of professional, well respected VCs did not do their due diligence of vetting the operations, and clients and investors of the FTX exchange lost their money. 
       
         
         
         
       ￼ 
       
         
         
         Initial Exchange Offerings (IEOs) 
       
       
         Many of the early token sales had poor investor protection mechanisms embedded. They lacked standardized procedures and general accountability. Furthermore, the teams soon became overwhelmed with conducting the crowd investing process entirely P2P, while trying to comply with regulation. Instead of focusing on their main job – developing the application they were raising funds for – project teams had to communicate with investors over various social media channels, meet regulatory requirements of identifying their investors to comply with KYC and AML laws, and try to get listed on at least one exchange. Token needs to be available for trading on at least one exchange, otherwise potential buyers and sellers will not have a market. This makes exchanges the gatekeepers of this new token economy. Before the emergence of IEOs, project founders had to set aside a significant chunk of funds raised to pay for the fixed listing fees that centralized exchanges required, or allocate a certain percentage of the funds raised to the exchanges. Unless a token got listed on an exchange, it had hardly any market value, as the investment in the token was almost impossible to liquidate without a market maker. Listing tokens on an exchange became a bottleneck for many projects, since exchanges had a hard time keeping up with running due diligence on potential new tokens that were in line to be listed. As a result, the listing fees became competitively high. Since exchanges don’t tend to disclose their fees, one can only rely on the information given out by individuals, who disclosed that listing fees could cost up to 2.5 million EUR. Only a third of tokens issued in 2017 ever became listed on any exchange after their token sale. As a result, the investors of these tokens had a hard time selling their tokens, unless they knew a buyer personally. Even when the token got listed on an exchange, tokens holders were often not able to trade their tokens because of lack of liquidity and market depth. Relatively small amounts of tokens traded could lead to considerable price volatilities. Many token holders therefore had no other choice than to hold on to their tokens, sometimes for a very long time period, as any minor sale could cause prices to fall.  
       
       
         Eventually, centralized exchanges turned out to be the ideal third-party service provider for token sales. They were able to alleviate the bureaucratic, marketing and distribution overheads, and the practice of Initial Exchange Offerings (IEO) profilerated. IEOs are intermediary services: instead of offering the tokens directly on their projects website and conducting the token transactions P2P via smart contract mechanisms, token issuers can raise funds by offering their token on a centralized exchange, which will settle the token sale for them. In an IEO, token issuers send their newly issued tokens to the exchange, which then sells the tokens to their existing customers. Issuers of tokens can reduce the organizational overheads and bureaucracy related to registering for a token sale with regulatory authorities and conducting a KYC process, leverage the existing user base of the token exchange as potential investors, and reduce marketing costs. IEOs have become a convenient one-stop shop for Web3 projects to conduct a token sale, while automatically listing the token for future trading on the exchange. With IEOs, issuers of tokens reduce the risk of never getting one’s tokens listed. Exchanges, on the other hand, benefit from offering such services, as they can potentially attract new customers that might want to invest in the listed token.  
       
       
         IEOs also reduce bureaucracy and risks for potential investors. Investors who are already registered with the exchange that conducts the token sale don’t have to undergo a separate identification process. They can potentially pay with any of the tokens they have already deposited on that exchange and mitigate the risk of a “gas war,” which can drive up transaction fees. 
         
           
             
               46 
             
           
         
         
             
         
         This reduces the overall barriers to investing in newly issued tokens. On the downside, however, as long as most token exchanges are not decentralized, IEOs also eradicate the P2P nature of early token sales and are a step towards re-centralization. 
       
       
         IEOs are considered more trustworthy, at least in theory. Since exchanges have a reputation to lose, they can be expected to audit the issuer, perform an analysis of the technical aspects, and assess the potential of a token, reducing the possibility for scammers to sell their tokens, thereby providing a certain level of investor protection. In practice, however, some of the less scrupulous exchanges will list anyone who pays them to do so, either with an upfront fee or with a cut of revenues generated through their token sale. Some centralized exchanges carrying out IEOs still exploit their users and often do not conduct a proper KYC process, especially when regulators are not doing their job. 
       
       
         While IEOs might seem to have similarities to IPOs, there are some fundamental differences. At the time of writing this book, IPOs tend to only take place when a business has proven its ability to perform. As opposed to IEOs, there is a pre-marketing process that relies on having been in business for a while, and IPOs also require considerably more time to prepare. In the case of IEOs, the exchange conducts all due diligence on the project and decides to list the token, but they have vested interests. IEOs involve fewer third parties, and often list novel tokens from ventures that don't have much of a track record. Behind IPOs, there are usually investment banks in combination with other traditional financial market institutions. While all of these players have their own vested interests, there are more stakeholders involved in the process, which leads to a certain level of checks and balances.  
       
       
         
         
         Airdrops 
       
       
         An “Airdrop” refers to the transfer of free tokens, often from the wallet of the Web3 project owners to the wallet of individual users. The transfer is often unsolicited. Any type of network token can be airdropped – asset tokens, access rights tokens, governance tokens, or other hybrid token types. The  
         
           first airdrop was probably conducted back in 2010 
         
          by Gavin Andresen – a core Bitcoin developer from the early days of the Bitcoin network – who set up a website that offered anyone with a Bitcoin address five BTC for free. The idea was to incentivize developers who were not yet mining BTC to play with the technology and be able to pay for network fees, without having to go through the complicated process of buying BTC, as token exchanges did not exist back then. However, the term “Airdrop” was only coined in 2014 by “ 
         
           Auroracoin 
         
         .” The purpose of the project was to give the Icelandic people an alternative form of money to the Icelandic Króna in the wake of the 2008 financial crisis, where Icelanders were subject to strict capital controls that locked them into their national currency. Auroracoin was an attempt to bootstrap a network effect, serving as a catalyst for Bitcoin trading and bringing cryptocurrencies into the domestic market without requiring real cash. In the process referred to as “Airdrop,” every Icelandic resident could claim 31.8 AUR (roughly 385 USD at the time) if they registered their permanent resident ID on Auroracoin's website. The idea of distributing tokens for free was soon adopted by other projects and became a popular marketing tool in 2016/17. Many Web3 projects started to use airdrops with the aim to promote their services, often targeting crypto influencers in the hope to get social media endorsements. Beyond promotional purposes, airdrops also proved to be a useful tool for spreading a small amount of tokens necessary for a minimum viable economy, to create network effects for the application they were promoting. As the practice of airdrops evolved, some projects even started to conduct unsolicited airdrops. 
       
       
         Traditional airdrops 
         : The first airdrops only required a recipient to register with the new project by providing their (pseudonymous) blockchain address for the distribution event. As tokenization and the practice of airdrops became more mainstream, financial market authorities and other regulatory bodies required a more sophisticated and personalized identification processes that complied with financial market regulation.  
       
       
         Bounty airdrops 
         : Bounty airdrops gained importance when the first Web3 projects started to conduct token sales for fundraising purposes targeting institutional money instead of private investors. Only Venture Capital companies (VCs) who did not represent the core user base held the newly issued tokens. The projects had to find other forms of incentivizing people to use their network services and achieve a minimum viable economy. Airdrops for bounty programs were introduced to reward users of a Web3 application for promotional work or other productive tasks they performed for the project, such as sharing social media posts. Bounty airdrops were also used to reward people for receiving information, such as signing up for a newsletter or following the social media page of a Web3 project. 
       
       
         Exclusive airdrops 
          refer to project tokens that are sent to a curated group of people, often crypto influencers or VCs, whose wallet addresses are publicly known. Exclusive airdrops are also used to incentivize loyalty in users who had an established history with the project, usually without hard conditions involved.  
       
       
         Holder airdrops 
          are conducted conditional on the amount of one’s token holdings, either in proportion to their stake in the network (in the case of fungible tokens), or conditional upon the amount of NFTs held. They can also be distributed based on a lottery system. 
       
       
         While Airdrops turned out to be a useful tool for bootstrapping a user base for token use cases, they also raise a series of legal questions, especially regarding their taxation – should they be treated as a gift (gift tax), capital, or income? If airdropped tokens are taxable as ordinary income, at which value will they be taxed – the market value on the date the tokens were dropped, or a later date? If the tokens are subject to capital gains tax, will one be taxed on the increase of the tokens value during one’s holding period, even if one burns the tokens?  
       
       
         
         
         DAICOs, SAFTs & SAFEs 
       
       
         DAICO 
          is a concept that was introduced by Vitalik Buterin in 2017 and stands for Decentralized Autonomous Initial Coin Offering. The main idea behind it was to have tokens that are governed by a DAO which constitutes the collective of investors with the aim to avoid mismanagement by the fundraising team. Teams looking to raise funds for a project would publish a DAICO contract that would define the parameters of the token sale. Investors would then be able to contribute ETH to the contract, based on the price curve of the sale. At the end of the contribution period, only a certain amount of total tokens raised could be taken out at a time. This amount would be voted upon by the investors of the project DAO, which in practice would have allowed token holders to allocate periodic budgets for the development team. While the idea sounded promising, and is in tune with the decentralized spirit of Web3, it was never really implemented in a project, probably because more centralized solutions were adopted that were more in line with traditional financial market practices, such as IEOs, as well as SAFEs and SAFTs. 
       
       
         A  
         Simple Agreement For Future Tokens (SAFT) 
          is a legal framework that has been developed for fundraising against a future promise of tokens, while remaining legally compliant under U.S. securities law. The SAFT framework has also been adopted in some European countries and other jurisdictions, but is not necessarily legally compliant with securities law in all jurisdictions across the globe. Instead of issuing project tokens against a stake in the network, investment money is accepted against a promise to give the investors project tokens, once the decentralized application is launched and the tokens are in fact issued. This means that at the time of signing the SAFT and accepting the invested funds, project tokens are not yet sold to the investor. Project developers can use the money raised through a SAFT to develop the network and technology, and only issue the tokens once the network is deployed. Under such an agreement, it has become customary that a discounted rate is granted to early investors. 
       
       
         A  
         Simple Agreement for Future Equity (SAFE) 
          is a modified SAFT that allows investors to convert the money they invested into equity at a later date. SAFT or SAFE are both non-debt financial instruments. This means that investors who give out money against a SAFT face the possibility that they will lose their investments. As opposed to P2P token sales – where tokens are sold to an unfiltered list of interested participants – SAFTs and SAFEs only address a handful of preselected and accredited investors, who have the legal permission to deal in securities and have the professional experience and necessary funds to conduct high-risk investments. They have much more similarities to traditional  
         
           angel investments 
         
         
           
             
               47 
             
           
         
          or  
         
           venture capital 
         
          (VC) 
         
           
             
               48 
             
           
         
          funding and have drawn a lot of criticism from early blockchain and Web3 enthusiasts, as they create new entry barriers for smallhold investors who now have no access to discounted early-bird rates. 
       
       
         But the criticism goes further than that. In the early years of token sales, many VCs or angel investors took advantage of the unregulated environment for engaging in pump-and-dump schemes and front-running, often at the expense of necessary due diligence towards other investors or the teams running the projects. VCs would buy tokens before retail investors were allowed in, at a discounted rate, and then dump their tokens onto the retail market. Their holdings were either not vested at all (locked against premature sale), and even if they were vested, the periods were too short. VCs could make easy money on pump-and-dump schemes that would not be allowed under regulatory oversight, often at the expense of the project founders and retail investors. This generated fool-proof earning possibilities for VCs. At the time of writing, the crypto market has started to develop more traditional practices. With a SAFT, accredited investors get a promise of tokens for a discount, but they have a lock-up period of at least 2 years. The teams have to define milestones with implementation fulfillment, implement a  
         
           testnet 
         
          or minimum viable token system/network to ship. Only then are tokens premined and exchanged according to the regulations defined in the SAFT. In some instances, VCs can even use their tokens to stake them in a DeFi product. Whether or not such a mechanism is sensible or counterproductive probably depends on the exact use case. A public token sale usually follows a SAFT or a SAFE, and is executed by KYC/AML-compliant custodian institutions such as centralized exchanges, and increasingly also traditional banks. While the legal fees of setting up a SAFT or similar agreements are relatively low, enforcing such agreements in case of dispute can get complicated, especially if both parties reside in different jurisdictions. 
       
       
         
         
         Issuance Mechanics & Clauses 
       
       
         Similar to IPOs, the different institutions conducting token sales have experimented with different types of pricing mechanisms that apply throughout the different stages of the sale. Tokens can be issued at a fixed price throughout the entire duration of the token sale. The price of the token bought could also increase over the duration of the sale, so early stage investors get a better price (risk discount) than later stage investors. Other price mechanisms – such as so-called “Dutch auctions” – decrease the price over the duration of the sale, which means that the token sale starts at the highest price per token, and decreases with time. Fixed prices don’t consider investor demand and ultimate price discovery occurs on the secondary market, once the token is being traded on an exchange. Increasing prices and dutch auctions, on the other hand, can account better for investor demand but can create other undesirable market dynamics. Various forms of bonding curve 
         
           
             
               49 
             
           
         
          mechanisms have also been experimented with by various projects, and for a time were quite popular as price mechanisms for continuous token sales.  
       
       
         Token sales conducted via exchanges predominantly use lotteries instead of first-come-first-served mechanisms. Using a lottery system supposedly increases demand and lowers token turnover. Most IEOs require potential investors to have a minimum amount of their native exchange token to be able to participate in an IEO. In such a setup, the number of tickets that an investor can claim to enter into the lottery for access to an IEO could depend on the amount of exchange tokens held in their account. Another parameter that is relevant in a token sale is the number of tokens issued. A project can issue a fixed amount (e.g. 1 million tokens), or an unlimited amount. Tokens might even be distributed as a percentage of total funds raised.  
       
       Another important mechanism to consider when conducting a token sale is the provision of some kind of freezing or cool-off period, also known as vesting period, during which investors are not allowed to transfer their tokens. This is to prevent early investors from market manipulation, in particular the dumping of tokens bought at a discounted price. Without this provision, the market price would crash as soon as the token is listed on an exchange. As previously explained, cool-off periods have become standard practice and are typically applied to big token holders who bought into the project earlier at a discount price. If cool-off or vesting periods are not applied appropriately, later-stage investors should take this as a red flag. It should also be a red flag for founders when less serious VCs try to strong-arm them into skipping these clauses, because the potential premature dumping of tokens by early investors will not create a sustainable economic environment for their projects. 
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       Token sales allow the issuance of newly minted project tokens in exchange for other tokens using smart contracts. They introduced a mechanism for pre-issuing tokens against a direct financial investment, before the project becomes operational. This was very different from how the Bitcoin economy started: Bitcoin never had a token sale, and Bitcoin tokens were never pre-issued, but have been, and still are, continuously minted upon Proof-of-Work, each time a block of transactions is created.  ~ The first token sales were referred to as Initial Coin Offerings (ICOs). As the term “token” entered the mainstream, “token offerings” or ITO (Initial Token Offerings), and in the specific cases of securities, STO (Security Token Offering), became the accepted term of the hour. Soon, alternative forms with alternative descriptions followed, such as Reverse ICOs, Equity Token Offering (ETOs), Initial Exchange Offerings (IEOs), Initial Dex Offering (IDO), Simple Agreement for Future Tokens (SAFT), or DAICOs.  ~ Due to the bureaucratic, marketing and distribution overheads, the practice of Initial Exchange Offerings (IEO) profilerated. Centralized exchanges turned out to be the ideal third-party service provider for token sales. Token issuers send their newly issued tokens to the exchange, which then sells the tokens to their existing customers. This reduces the organizational overheads and bureaucracy related to registering for a token sale with regulatory authorities and conducting a KYC process, leverages the existing user base of the token exchange as potential investors, and reduces marketing costs. Issuers of tokens reduce the risk of never getting one’s tokens listed.  ~ An “Airdrop” refers to the transfer of free tokens, often from the wallet of the Web3 project owners to the wallet of individual users. The transfer is often unsolicited. Any type of network token can be airdropped – asset tokens, access rights tokens, governance tokens, or other hybrid token types. Many Web3 projects started to use airdrops with the aim to promote their services, often targeting crypto influencers in the hope to get social media endorsements. Beyond promotional purposes, airdrops also proved to be a useful tool for spreading a small amount of tokens necessary for  a minimum viable economy, to create network effects for the application they were promoting. As the practice of airdrops evolved, some projects even started to conduct unsolicited airdrops. Various types of airdrops exist such as (i) traditional airdrops, (ii) bounty airdrops, (iii) exclusive airdrops or (iv) holder airdrops. ~ A Simple Agreement For Future Tokens (SAFT) is a legal framework that has been developed for fundraising against a future promise of tokens, while remaining legally compliant under U.S. securities law. The SAFT framework has also been adopted in some European countries and other jurisdictions, but is not necessarily legally compliant with securities law in all jurisdictions across the globe. Instead of issuing project tokens against a stake in the network, investment money is accepted against a promise to give the investors project tokens, once the decentralized application is launched and the tokens are in fact issued. This means that at the time of signing the SAFT and accepting the invested funds, project tokens are not yet sold to the investor. Project developers can use the money raised through a SAFT to develop the network and technology, and only issue the tokens once the network is deployed. Under such an agreement, it has become customary that a discounted rate is granted to early investors. ~ A Simple Agreement for Future Equity (SAFE) is a modified SAFT that allows investors to convert the money they invested into equity at a later date.  ~ SAFTs and SAFEs only address a handful of preselected and accredited investors, who have the legal permission to deal in securities and have the professional experience and necessary funds to conduct high-risk investments. They have much more similarities to traditional angel investments or venture capital (VC) funding and have drawn a lot of criticism from early blockchain and Web3 enthusiasts, as they create new entry barriers for smallhold investors who now have no access to discounted early-bird rates.  ~ Similar to IPOs, the different institutions conducting token sales have experimented with different types of pricing mechanisms that apply throughout the different stages of the sale. Tokens can be issued at a fixed price throughout the entire duration of the token sale. The price of the token bought could also increase over the duration of the sale, so early stage investors get a better price (risk discount) than later stage investors. Other price mechanisms – such as so-called “Dutch auctions” – decrease the price over the duration of the sale. Various forms of bonding curve mechanisms have also been experimented with by various projects, and for a time were quite popular as price mechanisms for continuous token sales. Token sales conducted via exchanges predominantly use lotteries instead of first-come-first-served mechanisms. Using a lottery system supposedly increases demand and lowers token turnover.  ~ Another important mechanism to consider when conducting a token sale is the provision of some kind of freezing or cool-off period, also known as vesting period, during which investors are not allowed to transfer their tokens. This is to prevent early investors from market manipulation, in particular the dumping of tokens bought at a discounted price. Without this provision, the market price would crash as soon as the token is listed on an exchange. If cool-off or vesting periods are not applied appropriately, later-stage investors should take this as a red flag. It should also be a red flag for founders when less serious VCs try to strong-arm them into skipping these clauses, because the potential premature dumping of tokens by early investors will not create a sustainable economic environment for their projects.

         This QR code will lead you to a website that contains all the references to the source materials used for the research of the chapters and should also provide a reading list for those who are interested in a deeper dive into the topics presented in this chapter. Where possible, the links will be updated on a regular basis to prevent the issue of broken links. It is only scannable on white or light colored backgrounds and might not work on dark backgrounds. The website is also accessible under following URL:  
         
           https://bit.ly/40TQOA1
