         Token Exchanges & NFT Marketplaces 
       
       
         
         Token exchanges offer a market 
         place where 
          buyers and sellers of tokens can meet and exchange one token type for another. While they are important players in this new token economy, they are still predominantly operated by centralized  
         institutions 
         , which makes them vulnerable to hacks, mismanagement, or censorship. Decentralized exchanges try to mitigate these risks by  
         using public blockchain networks as their execution layer. They have introduced novel forms of technical 
          swapping mechanisms 
          as well as new market mechanisms. 
         ~ 
       
       
         Blockchain networks and other distributed ledgers allow the transfer of tokens without intermediaries, but they only allow sending tokens from one address in a blockchain network to another address in the same network. Tokens of one blockchain network cannot be natively transferred to other networks, since the nodes in one network don’t have information about the state of tokens managed in another network. As a result, the buying and selling of tokens requires the intermediary services of an exchange.  
       
       
         Buying and selling can be difficult and time consuming at times, depending on the popularity and legal status of the particular token one wants to buy. Some tokens might only be listed on smaller exchanges or marketplaces, where token pairings or token/fiat pairings are often limited. In such a case, buyers need to register on all the exchanges or marketplaces that list the tokens they want to buy or sell. They might have to register on exchanges or marketplaces that they would otherwise not use, swap tokens several times, and pay transaction fees each time they do so. As a result, trading tokens can be a bureaucratic and expensive endeavor. Potential buyers of a token might decide not to buy if the process is too complicated. Exchanges and marketplaces that list many tokens and have good token pairings have, therefore, become more popular among buyers and sellers. They are a convenient one-stop shop to trade between multiple token types, without the need to register on another exchange or marketplace. While this is understandable from a user perspective, it also led to market concentration and an oligopoly of a limited number of exchange services. 
       
       
         Exchanges and NFT marketplaces are the new financial intermediaries in Web3. They can decide whether they list a particular token or not, and have therefore become the market makers and gatekeepers in this emerging tokenized economy. The market-making power of exchanges became evident in August 2016, when the Ethereum network performed a controversial protocol upgrade (“hard fork”) as a result of TheDAO exploit. 
         
           
             
               50 
             
           
         
          The upgrade was successfully accepted, which means that most node operators upgraded their software to the new protocol. The node operators who did not upgrade and remained on the network with the old network rules became the “shorter chain” – the version of the Ethereum blockchain with less support and therefore less computing power. This shorter network – which is now referred to as “Ethereum Classic” – initially did not have any market value. Forking a network is not an unusual occurrence, as blockchain networks often perform protocol upgrades for technical reasons, and there are always a number of node operators who maintain the old protocol rules. In this particular case, however, the “hard fork” was highly politicized and controversial. “ 
         
           Poloniex 
         
         ,” a centralized token exchange, decided to list the Ethereum Classic network token, which created a market for buying and selling them. As the demand for these tokens rose, other exchanges started listing the token as well. While this was not the first fork of a blockchain network, it was the first time the token of a minority network was listed on an exchange, giving it an economic value. 
       
       
         
         
         History of Exchanges – The Wild West Times 
       
       
         While it has become easy to buy tokens using exchange services and NFT marketplaces today, buying or selling Bitcoin (BTC) was not easily possible in the first years after the launch of the Bitcoin network. Back then, anyone could earn BTC by performing mining operations on their personal computer. They could then send and receive the BTC that they had mined P2P over the Bitcoin network – from one Bitcoin address to another Bitcoin address – just for fun. More was not possible at that time. To exchange BTC for other currencies or goods and services, one needed to find a buyer – either offline or online – and directly exchange the BTC one wanted to buy or sell. Such exchanges were conducted in the hope that the counterparty would hold true to their end of the bargain. The Bitcoin community was small, people often knew each other, and most of the trades worked out somehow. Even if a trade failed for some reason, it wasn't a disaster, as BTC was only worth a few cents and, back in those day, could easily be mined with a home PC.  
       
       
         The first “Bitcoin Market” was announced in 2010 on “ 
         
           Bitcointalk 
         
         ” – an online forum created by Satoshi Nakamoto, where Bitcoin developers and enthusiasts could meet and discuss Bitcoin’s ideology, economy, and technological implementations. This Bitcoin Market offered a floating exchange rate and an escrow service for BTC. If someone wanted to buy BTC, they needed to send the equivalent amount in fiat currency via PayPal to the seller. Bitcoin Market held the seller’s BTC in escrow until the receipt of the USD was verified by the seller. As the demand for Bitcoin and other emerging cryptocurrencies grew, so did the interest in buying and selling them through more convenient means. It was around that time that the first full-service exchanges emerged, most notably “ 
         
           Mt. Gox 
         
         .”  
       
       
         Mt. Gox was a website that originally offered a marketplace for online card games. It was founded before the emergence of Bitcoin and unintentionally became a cryptocurrency exchange in 2010, when Mt. Gox started accepting BCT as payments for their “magic cards.” 
         
           
             
               51 
             
           
         
          Eventually, Mt. Gox became a convenient place for an increasing number of people who wanted to trade their BTC through the backdoor of buying and selling magic cards with other currencies. The founder of Mt. Gox abandoned the online card exchange and started focusing on trading BTC. Around 2011, other cryptocurrency exchange services such as “ 
         
           Tradehill 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           VirWoX 
         
         ” started to appear. In addition to BTC, these new exchanges also traded other virtual currencies that had existed pre-Bitcoin – such as the Linden Dollar, the internal currency of the virtual reality platform “ 
         
           Second Life 
         
         .” They also offered alternative payment methods and market mechanisms for buying and selling tokens. By 2013, and in spite of a market crash and hack of the exchange,  Mt. Gox was settling around 70% of all global BTC trades. Tradehill came in second place. 
       
       
         Exchanges originally only offered a marketplace to trade BTC and other fungible tokens that have money-like or commodity-like properties. As more and more non-fungible token classes emerged – especially after the Ethereum network went live – marketplaces for these non-fungible tokens with focus on special classes of NFTs also started to emerge. Over time, both exchanges and NFT marketplaces started to increasingly offer additional services to their customers, such as earning interest on deposited tokens.  
       
       
         All early exchanges were centralized services that held the tokens of their clients in custody, managing all token transactions on their private servers, which defied the “decentralized” narrative of Bitcoin. But at that time, there was no other practical alternative. Centralized exchanges act as custodians and brokers of tokens, and their clients have no control over what really happens with their tokens. The first exchange services were particularly susceptible to internal and external security breaches, mainly due to lack of experience of their founders and – as a result – a lack of due diligence. Most regulators probably did not have them on their radar or considered them as too much of a niche to explicitly regulate so, for the most part, they did not actively comply with financial market regulation yet.  
       
       
         
         
         Centralized Exchanges (CEX) 
       
       
         Centralized exchanges perform intermediary services between buyers and sellers of tokens and also provide on-ramp and off-ramp services between the fiat world and the crypto world. Similar to banks and fund managers, who offer custodial services and manage the money of their clients, centralized token exchanges take the tokens of their clients into their custody and safeguard their private keys for them. Customer tokens are controlled by the operators of the exchange services through so-called “custodial wallets” or “cloud-wallets.” As opposed to many early crypto enthusiasts, who manage their tokens with self-hosted wallets – in the form of hardware wallets, software wallets, or paper wallets – and are always in control of their private keys, newcomers often prefer to outsource wallet management to online exchanges, where the customers don’t have to worry about private key management. Customers of centralized exchanges can only access their tokens via a web interface of the exchange, by using a Web2-based password which is managed by the centralized exchange and can be reset by their system administrators (in case a customer forgets their passwords). Many centralized exchanges don’t even create dedicated user wallets for their clients, making a private key useless in the first case. While this might be more convenient for customers, it contradicts the idea of P2P money, where token holders are in full control of their funds, and fully responsible for private-key management at all times. 
       
       
         Centralized exchanges are owned by private companies and charge their users transaction fees for using their services. They also profit from offering different prices for buying and selling the same asset, pocketing the difference. The majority of token holders today use centralized exchanges, because it is the most convenient way to buy tokens with fiat currencies. Centralized exchanges operate on classic client-server technology. They are not subject to the same security mechanisms as blockchain networks; their customers surrender full control over their tokens to outdated systems. All token swaps are brokered “off-chain” on the private order books of these exchanges, which are stored on their private server infrastructure. This means that the order book transactions of centralized exchanges are not accessible to customers or other outsiders. While centralized exchanges do offer convenient wallet management and valuable brokering services, they are vulnerable to hacks, mismanagement, outright fraud or censorship. 
       
       
         Hacks of private server infrastructure and mismanagement have been the biggest issues in the past, especially in the early years, when the market was less mature and also less explicitly regulated. “ 
         
           Mt. Gox 
         
         ,” which was the largest Bitcoin exchange at the time, suspended trading and filed for bankruptcy in 2014 after approximately 850,000 BTC went missing, most likely stolen due to continuous mismanagement. Many other cases followed. “ 
         
           Bitfinex 
         
         ,” ”Bitstamp,” “Binance,” “Bithumb,” “ 
         
           Coincheck 
         
         ,” “Poloniex,” or “ShapeShift” are examples of centralized exchanges that were hacked over the years. At the time of writing this book, the most prominent case of mismanagement (and allegedly also fraud) is the case of cryptocurrency exchange “ 
         
           FTX 
         
         ” in 2022, which was explained in a previous chapter. All these incidents have led to the misconception in the general public that blockchain networks or crypto assets are not safe. The opposite is the case, and these incidents are a testament to the reason why Bitcoin and other blockchain networks were created in the first place. Among early crypto enthusiasts, the vulnerability of centralized exchanges gave rise to the mantra “not your keys, not your crypto.” This refers to the fact that unless you control your private keys yourself – which is only possible with a self-hosted wallet – your assets are vulnerable to exchange hack, fraud and potentially also government seizure.  
       
       
         However, security breaches and mismanagement due to human failure are not the only issue exchanges face. Liquidity of token supply and demand is another essential challenge that a small exchange can face. Smaller exchanges with low trading volumes were, and still are, particularly vulnerable to extreme price volatilities such as  
         
           flash crashes 
         
         
           
             
               52 
             
           
         
          and  
         
           price spikes 
         
         
           
             
               53 
             
           
         
          which could be triggered even by small buy and sell orders. This made smaller exchanges subject to market manipulation and less attractive for users in general, and led to centralization around a few dominant players in the market.  
       
       
         As more and more centralized exchanges became regulatory compliant, cryptocurrencies and other crypto assets also became mainstream since the on-ramping into crypto became easier for average users and led to the influx of many new users into crypto. However, centralized exchanges that are regulatory compliant must meet  
         
           Know Your Customer (KYC) 
         
          requirements as well as  
         
           Anti-Money Laundering (AML 
         
         ) and  
         
           Combating the Financing of Terrorism  
         
         
           (CTF) 
         
          laws. This means that customers have to fully identify themselves when registering on an exchange, which raises serious privacy and potential censorship issues. Blockchain networks – by design – offer a high level of transparency of all token transactions and more institutional accountability than legacy financial systems. This level of transparency was designed in the light of pseudonymous blockchain identities, not a full (real life) identification of users as required by these laws. Pseudonymous blockchain identities are broken with KYC and AML compliant exchanges.  
       
       
         It is important to understand that KYC and AML laws were designed based on the logic of a traditional banking system that operates on private ledger infrastructure, not for public ledger infrastructure. Due to the pseudonymous nature of blockchain transactions, any government institution, private security company, and really anyone with the necessary know-how and access to the identity data of the users of an exchange can run big data analysis on pseudonymous token transactions, correlate it with other data footprints of a users, and discover all blockchain addresses of that user. In the light of potential data breaches, KYC and AML laws cannot be enforced one-to-one in a Web3 environment without breaching citizens’ privacy rights.  
       
       
         In spite of all the limits and challenges of centralized exchanges, it is important to acknowledge the role they played in onboarding new users to crypto and the market making functionalities for existing users, especially in the early years of crypto. While it is a fact that centralized exchanges are subject to the same systemic risks as traditional financial institutions, there are also many cases of centralized exchanges which have been able to avoid security breaches and mismanagement due to good governance, ensuring strict security management, and maintaining due diligence over customer funds. Some centralized exchanges have even gone further to include the power of blockchain networks in the systems.  
         
           Kraken 
         
         , for example, is a centralized exchange that developed a policy they called “ 
         
           Proof-of-Reserve 
         
         ,” so that clients can verify if their deposited tokens are held in full reserve. 
         
           
             
               54 
             
           
         
          With such a system, the customer of a centralized exchange can independently verify if their balance was included in the Proof-of-Reserves audit by comparing select pieces of data with the “Merkle root.” 
         
           
             
               55 
             
           
         
          Publicly verifiable proofs give users real-time oversight of how their funds are being managed. This includes user-verifiable cryptographic proof that each account was included in the sum, and digital signatures proving that the custodian has control of these wallets. Kraken has been a thought leader and developer of best practices for centralized exchanges, showing how on-chain cryptographic proofs can be used to create more accountability and transparency for Web2-based opaque systems. In an attempt to maintain their customer base, other centralized exchanges started to adopt these practices in 2022, but only after the downfall of FTX had made the systemic risks of centralized systems obvious to the average user. Many of them started to withdraw their deposited tokens from centralized exchanges to decentralized exchanges or to self-hosted wallets that are not connected to the Internet. 
       
       
         
         
         Decentralized Exchanges (DEXs) aka Automated Market Makers (AMMs) 
       
       
         Decentralized exchanges (DEX) are applications executed by a blockchain network where smart contracts replace the brokerage functions of a centralized exchange, while granting full control over the tokenized funds to their users. Token trades are settled on-chain and therefore have an audit trail that is publicly verifiable. The smart contracts are usually created by the protocol founders and maintained by stakeholders who have been granted voting power over protocol upgrades. Decentralized exchanges have increasingly become “community-governed” through the vehicle of Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAOs). This means that both the decentralized application as well as the voting processes of all DAO members are executed over blockchain networks. The smart contracts, and the protocols through which they are governed, are a new type of intermediary institution in this emerging Web3, albeit more automated and publicly verifiable. These new institutions are also created and managed by people, and therefore susceptible to similar systemic risks or human error, misjudgement and misconduct – but all token transactions are publicly verifiable almost in real time without the need for third party audits. Potential vulnerabilities and attacks cannot be covered up. At the time of writing this book, decentralized exchanges charge no – or very low – brokerage fees. Buyers and sellers only pay for the blockchain transaction fees and a fee to liquidity providers, in case liquidity pools are used. To understand the mechanisms of decentralized exchanges, a few core concepts of asset markets in general need to be explained: 
       
       
         Order books 
         : Order books are mechanisms that have traditionally been used by financial institutions to collect and record all buy and sell orders with the aim to match buyers and sellers, usually on a first-come-first-served basis. These books used to be analog (hence the name), but are today recorded in electronic form. Various matching mechanisms can be applied to execute the buy and sell orders recorded in an order book. In public blockchain networks, order books are a challenge, as each order would need to be designed as a separate on-chain transaction, which congested blockchain networks and made trades expensive. Furthermore, as described in a previous chapter, due to the nature of some blockchain networks, transactions cannot be validated on a first-come-first-serve-basis and are validated and executed in a different way. This invites “front-running” and “sandwich attacks” (read more about the issues around “Miner Extractable Value” in the chapter “Decentralized Finance”). 
       
       
         Market makers 
         : In traditional asset markets, professional traders or financial institutions provide the necessary liquidity for trading pairs, which is why they are referred to as “market makers.” Market makers are wholesale traders who act as the trading counterparty on both sides of a trade. They have exclusive insight into the private order books of traditional exchanges. Their role is to create multiple bid-ask orders to match the current orders of retail buyers and retails sellers and therefore create guaranteed fluid instant trading operations, in the hope of clearing their open positions with future buyers and sellers. If token holder A decides to buy ETH at a price of 100 EUR on a centralized token exchange, the exchange tries to find a token holder who is willing to sell the same amount of ETH at the stated exchange rate worth 100 EUR. If the exchange cannot find a suitable match for a buy and sell order, they would use the services of market makers to provide liquidity. The user could now buy the ETH on the exchange (from the market maker) for 100.2 EUR, while they can also sell it to the exchange (to the market maker) for 99.8 EUR. The price difference is referred to as “the spread.” The spread allows the market maker to earn money – in return, they provide more liquidity to the market by buying more than they can sell at the same price, and they bear the risk for doing so. Even though the spreads might seem small, market makers usually have enough volume and trading skills to make considerable profits. Stock exchanges, commodity exchanges and centralized token exchanges rely on these professional traders, as they have the deep pockets necessary to guarantee that buyers and sellers of tokens can always and without delay find a counterparty for their trade at current market rates. The exchange executes all operations between buyers, sellers and market makers by ensuring that trading orders are matched accordingly. In spite of the fact that insider trading is illegal in most jurisdictions worldwide, the exclusive insight that market makers have into private order books also gives them enormous market power and front-running opportunities. 
       
       
         Liquidity challenges & slippage 
         : Depending on the number and size of buy and sell orders, and availability of market makers, a market can have more or less “liquidity” aka “market depth.” If the number of buyers and sellers is low, any trade, even of small volume, might affect the price of the asset on that market. The difference between the expected token price at the time of trade and the price at which the token trade is finalized is referred to as “ 
         
           slippage 
         
         .” It depends on the available liquidity in a market. The first decentralized exchanges had low trading volumes and therefore low liquidity of tokens, which made them prone to slippage. A lack of liquidity on a market can generate large price spikes, which could be triggered by just a  single token trade, and be potentially exploited by arbitrage seekers. Arbitrage is a generally accepted market function to smooth out price differences across marketplaces and avoid large price spikes in the first place. But when these large price spikes do occur, they can be intentionally exploited by malicious actors in DeFi, who profit from such opportunities while creating an avalanche of collateral damages to other market participants. While slippage is an issue both in centralized and decentralized exchanges, decentralized exchanges initially lacked a minimum viable market. In order to avoid considerable slippage – in the absence of more users who wanted to buy or sell tokens over decentralized exchanges in the early years of crypto – they came up with the solution of liquidity pools. 
       
       
         Liquidity pools 
         : The concept of “liquidity pools” was introduced to address the liquidity and slippage issues that decentralized exchanges faced in the absence of traditional market makers. Traditional market makers are replaced by so-called “automated market makers” that use the services of liquidity pools. These liquidity pools are managed by smart contracts which each hold a token trading pair in escrow. Buyers and sellers can trade their tokens against this pair when using the services of a decentralized exchange according to predefined conditions. Liquidity pools usually consist of two token types that form a trading pair. Users have to deposit both tokens of a token trading pair into the liquidity pool of that trading pair in the ratio of their exchange rate. In doing so, they provide market liquidity into a pool which others can use to buy and sell tokens from. They need to provide both sides of the market in correct proportions, as their role is to provide liquidity without impacting the current market price. These so-called “liquidity providers” specify an amount of a token they want to provide to a pool, and the smart contract will automatically specify how much of the other token the provider needs to deposit. In return, they receive part of the fees generated by the trades. Additionally, they can also become traders and buy or sell the tokens in the pool. Users of a liquidity pool never trade with other users, but instead trade against a price-finding algorithm which is determined by a mathematical formula and encoded into the smart contract. 
       
       
         Liquidity providers 
          are a new type of market participant who supplies tokens to the liquidity pool, without necessarily wanting to trade them. In theory, anyone with an internet connection and the right tokens can become a liquidity provider to a liquidity pool – independent of the amount of tokens they hold 
         
           
             
               56 
             
           
         
          – and earn a form of “interest” or ”yield” on their deposited tokens. This is quite different from centralized marketplaces, where only institutional investors or high-net-worth individuals with deep pockets have the capacity to become market makers. Liquidity providers to decentralized exchanges are incentivized to deposit their tokens in return for a share of the transaction fees paid by the traders. This fee is paid by both the buyer and the seller of tokens each time they use the services of a decentralized exchange. Liquidity providers are sometimes additionally incentivized with native protocol tokens of the decentralized exchange they are contributing to. In case of a trading pair, liquidity providers deposit an equivalent value of both tokens that define the trading pair in their pool. The first liquidity provider to deposit both tokens can define the exchange rate they believe is appropriate, which is adjusted with every subsequent deposit or withdrawal. The exchange rate will be corrected by other market participants – such as arbitrage traders – who will correct the rate if it is off and profit from their trade. Liquidity providers might experience so-called “ 
         
           i 
         
         
           mpermanent loss 
         
         ” or “divergence loss” if the price of their deposited tokens diverges from the time they were deposited to the time they are withdrawn. If the loss is greater than the fees earned, the liquidity provider realizes a loss, which would not have happened had they held on to their tokens instead. Even if one might not actually lose money, the gains might be less than if one had just held on to the tokens. Such price divergence can give traders or arbitrageurs profitable opportunities, until their trades bounces back in line with the wider market.  
       
       
         Automated Market Makers (AMMs) 
          are alternative market making mechanisms executed by a smart contract which replace the need for traditional exchanges that work with an order book system. AMMs consist of two elements: (i) One or several pools of tokens (aka liquidity pools), and (ii) the algorithm 
         
           
             
               57 
             
           
         
          upon which the price of a trade is determined and executed. The concept of AMMs was introduced to a wider audience in a  
         
           blog post by Vitalik Buterin 
         
          in 2017. In an AMM setup, one does not need a counterparty to initiate or complete a trade. Instead, AMMs provide a smart-contract-enabled three sided marketplace, where the trades are conducted between a buyer, seller and liquidity providers. The market mechanism is encoded in the smart contract, which executes all transactions when the conditions to a trade are met, thereby eliminating the need for centralized exchange services.  
         Vitalik’s proposal was quickly adopted by the first exchanges, who soon started to develop alternative AMM mechanisms. In 2018, “ 
         
           Uniswap 
         
         ”  
         was the first decentralized exchange to implement 
          the concept of liquidity pools allowing any pair of fungible Ethereum tokens with a 50-50 ratio 
         . “ 
         
           Curve 
         
         ” adapted the initially proposed mechanism by focusing on liquidity pools that bundled similar assets such as stable tokens, and were therefore able to improve liquidity rates, increasing the efficiency of trades at lowest costs involved. “ 
         
           Balancer 
         
         ” introduced the concept of dynamic liquidity pools, which allowed users to bundle up to eight different assets in any ratio.  
       
       
         Price finding algorithm 
         : The purpose of the algorithm in an AMM is to have an automated mechanism to find the exchange rate between two assets in a pool. The price finding mechanism used by AMMs differs from traditional financial markets and follows a so-called “bonding curve.” A bonding curve is a mathematical formula that determines the price of an exchange rate by factors such as time, amount of lender and borrowers, and the timing of their borrowing and lending. The parameters of the curve can vary depending on the type of bonding curve. The price finding mechanisms of AMMs in their current state still have many shortcomings and need to be refined. This is probably one of the reasons why Vitalik Buterin – in this initial blog post on AMMs – emphasized that they could not be the only tool for decentralized trading of tokens, as he thought that non-AMM exchanges were necessary for keeping AMM prices accurate.  
       
       
         AMMs vs. order books 
         : While order books are a proactive market-making mechanism that requires a match-making system to match all pending orders in the market, AMMs are passive mechanisms: their rules are predetermined and encoded in the algorithm, and cannot be flexibly adjusted in the case of unforeseen events. AMMs are more inclusive and allow anyone with financial means to become a liquidity provider to a liquidity pool, as long as they meet the conditions of the smart contract. Any user can become a market maker and earn a portion of the trading fees the platform receives as reward for providing liquidity. Combined with the liquidity pool contract, liquidity providers are, therefore, the new market makers in Web3. AMMs are also considered to be more in tune with the composable nature of DeFi in general, and enable more innovation – but also more risky financial products. At the time of writing, almost all decentralized exchanges have adopted AMMs with liquidity pools, since liquidity pools have been a very successful bootstrapping tool for generating liquidity. Because AMMs have become the dominant market mechanism for decentralized exchanges, many have replaced the term “decentralized exchange” with “automated market maker.” For the sake of consistency in this book, I will keep referring to decentralized exchanges as a generic concept. Furthermore, some people argue that AMMs do not render order books completely obsolete, as order books allow a much higher level of flexibility than AMMs, and might be more suitable for a particular set of users.  
         While 
          AMMs currently dominate the space, decentralized exchanges could use alternative mechanisms such as atomic swaps (which will be explained later in this chapter) or similar on-chain token swapping methods, combining them with brokerage mechanisms via smart contracts – from order books to brokerage via liquidity pools.  
       
       
         
         
         Challenges of Decentralized Exchanges 
       
       
         While AMMs have resolved the challenges of market making in blockchain-based systems, and theoretically generated more inclusion for smaller investors to become market makers, they still face many challenges. Many decentralized exchanges of the first generation failed, mostly due to a myriad of technical, legal and economic challenges. Examples were “ 
         
           Komodo 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           WavesDex 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           OasisDex 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Radar Relay 
         
         ,” “BarterDex,” “ 
         
           Bisq 
         
         ,” “StellarDex,” and “ 
         
           EtherDelta 
         
         .” Order book systems were not efficient on a blockchain network, both from a technical and economic point of view, and the lack of liquidity (and resulting slippage) was the other big bootstrapping challenge. The learning curve was steep and ruthless to these early movers. Most of them did not survive or lost market share to the next generation of decentralized exchanges, often within a time span of just a year. At the time of writing this book, the decentralized exchanges that are leading the market are “ 
         
           Uniswap 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Curve 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           PancakeSwap 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Balancer 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           SushiSwap 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           SUN 
         
         ” – which are all designed as AMMs. All of them are constantly improving their mechanisms and incentive structures. As for the mechanisms, the amount of tokens types in a liquidity pool has been experimented with, as has the price curve and the algorithm. AMMs today might use liquidity pools with more than two tokens. The price finding mechanisms also vary from exchange to exchange. Existing price finding mechanisms might have been adapted within the same exchange during protocol upgrades. Decentralized exchanges also compete with other decentralized exchanges on several fronts – mostly through their fees and other incentives they offer. At some point, decentralized exchanges started to award special risk premiums in the form of governance tokens, other tokens or direct interest to generate more market traction, liquidity and market share.  
       
       
         While many exchanges claim to be “decentralized,” none of them is fully decentralized yet and all still have a long way to go. The reasons are a set of technical, economic, institutional and legal challenges: 
       
       
         Technical challenge 
         : Blockchain networks are not interoperable, which means that tokens from one blockchain network cannot be natively swapped P2P with tokens from another blockchain network. General ledger interoperability will need to be resolved to create the basic requirements for cross-chain token swaps. Otherwise, decentralized exchanges are locked into using tokens of one blockchain network only. Decentralized exchanges are experimenting with a range of solutions to enable P2P exchange of tokens, such as cross-chain atomic  
         swaps, bridges, interoperability standards like the Inter Blockchain Communication Protocol (IBC) or Polkadot. At the time of writing, no solution has clearly won out over all others. The interoperability protocol IBC and network bridges seem to be the most popular solutions and will be explained in the next subchapter. Another technical challenge is that on-chain order books are slow and expensive, at least as long as the scalability issues of public blockchain networks are unresolved. Each time an order is posted or modified, blockchain transactions become slow and expensive – every new token order or change to an order has to be added as a blockchain transaction, generating high network load, which is why liquidity pools have replaced order book systems. Decentralized exchanges also need to resolve the challenges around the trustworthiness of inbound oracles – data from the outside world that can trigger a smart contract to be executed or not. The smart contracts of decentralized exchanges constantly need to access market information from outside their system, such as token prices across various exchanges, to verify if conditions of the smart contract are met, before they can settle transactions according to the encoded rules. Currently, the oracle services that are used to provide price feeds are often centralized, and operate over unverified and unsecured channels, thereby undermining the decentralized nature of these exchanges. 
       
       
         P2P challenge 
         : In the early days of decentralized exchanges, the trades were executed completely P2P, from one user wallet to another user wallet. Most decentralized exchanges today, however, work with liquidity pools. As a result, token trades are not exchanged “user to user” anymore, but rather “user to smart contract.” The tokens are therefore in the “custody” of smart contracts governed by decentralized exchanges. When users provide liquidity on a decentralized exchange such as “Uniswap,” they interact with a Uniswap smart contract where the tokens are locked. If something unexpected were to happen to the smart contract – such as an unintended exploit that the designers of the smart contract have not provisioned for – or if decentralized exchanges would have to comply with regulatory requirements at some point (such as mandatory sanctioning by governmental institutions), users could lose their tokenized deposits.  
       
       
         Usability challenges 
         : If someone accidentally sends their tokens to the wrong blockchain address or loses the keys to their wallets, recovering the lost tokens will be impossible, unless the accidental recipient is honest and sends the tokens back. From a legal point of view, it would be possible to try and recover the funds, but the legal precedents are still sparse, and the legal process is extremely time consuming and expensive. This is why many newcomers trade tokens on centralized exchanges. Experienced traders with the necessary technical and financial skills usually prefer DEX over CEX, because of the composability of the decentralized exchanges with other DeFi protocols, and the customized financial products they can build and use around this composable financial ecosystem. 
       
       
         Inclusion challenges 
         : At the time of writing this book, decentralized exchanges mainly benefit experienced traders with technical skills who are already positioned in token markets. DEX are not necessarily suitable for newcomers to the market, as they don’t resolve the question of trading fiat currencies for tokens. Newcomers to Web3 will probably still use centralized exchanges, which offer easy crypto on-ramp services, allowing customers to easily buy tokens with any fiat currency. 
       
       
         Legal challenges 
         : It is often argued that users of decentralized exchanges might not have the same “legal protections” as users of centralized exchanges. The history of centralized exchanges paints a different picture. User funds that have been hacked and mismanaged are in most cases not returned to their owners, and if they are, it often only happens after years of litigation and insolvency procedures, regardless of the jurisdiction the exchange falls under. Many regulators worldwide are currently starting to explicitly regulate decentralized exchanges. The question under which jurisdiction a collectively maintained source code should be regulated (especially if the developers are anonymous, live in different countries, and the code is collectively executed) remains unresolved. It also remains unclear which stakeholders in a distributed network would be subject to potential regulations and sanctions. 
       
       
         In spite of all these challenges, decentralized exchanges temporarily outperformed centralized exchanges in 2021 in terms of market volume (not in terms of users), but the trend has reversed ever since. If the above-mentioned challenges can be resolved, or at least partially mitigated, decentralized exchanges could allow for a more liquid, inclusive and transparent marketplace, and might become the mainstream exchange service that even retail investors might use. Improved usability will be key for retail investors – especially those who are already registered on centralized exchanges – to be willing to move to decentralized exchanges. Furthermore, many more real-world assets (including fiat currencies) need to be tokenized to catalyze necessary network effects for a thriving decentralized financial ecosystem.  
       
       
         
         
         The Interoperability Challenge 
       
       
         Interoperability is a prerequisite for cross-chain token transfer, cross-chain smart contracts and cross-chain oracles that feed the smart contracts with data. Due to the lack of interoperability, many decentralized exchanges initially started out as “blockchain specific” (aka “blockchain centric”) services, where token swaps could only be settled between tokens of the underlying blockchain network on which the decentralized exchange was built. This was probably the reason why most decentralized exchanges at that time started to build their services on the Ethereum network, as most tokens that had market traction were issued on the Ethereum network. Offering a DEX on Ethereum meant that one could use the DEX to swap Ethereum-compatible fungible tokens. Decentralized exchanges can only be “blockchain agnostic” (aka “blockchain neutral”) if they offer decentralized cross-chain token swaps via some kind of interoperability solution such as IBC “ 
         
           Cosmos 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Polkadot 
         
         ,” “Optimistic Rollups,” “ZK Rollups,” bridging solutions, etc. Due to the technical differences of blockchain protocols, one-size-fits-all solutions that allow for interoperability between all of the hundreds of blockchain networks do not exist yet.  
       
       
         Atomic swap 
         s were one of the earliest technical solutions to allow for P2P cross-chain trading without a trusted intermediary such as a centralized exchange. They use a type of smart contract called a “hash time-locked contract” (HTLC) to secure the token transaction between separate blockchain networks, and work wallet to wallet, making sure that both parties to the token trade fulfill the requirements. Users remain in full control of their private keys and their tokens when conducting a cross-chain trade. The solution, however, requires that both networks managing the swapped tokens support HTLCs, and use the same cryptographic hash function. In order to conduct wallet-to-wallet transactions, the wallet application (aka blockchain client) used must also have atomic swap capabilities. What makes this difficult to develop are the cryptoeconomic security aspects that need to guarantee finality on both blockchain networks. If this problem can be solved, atomic swaps would be a more universal solution than other inter-blockchain protocols or bridges – which all require bilateral solutions between two blockchain networks. 
       
       
         Wrapped tokens 
          use a smart contract or another escrow solution that works like a blockchain “ 
         
           adapter 
         
         ” with a 1:1 peg. Wrapped tokens are created on a secondary blockchain network to make tokens from the primary blockchain network – which are held in escrow – usable on the secondary blockchain network. They are created and destroyed through minting and burning mechanisms. Users can lock a certain amount of a token they hold into a smart contract that holds them in escrow and mints an equivalent amount of tokens on the destination blockchain. The locked tokens can be reclaimed once the wrapped tokens on the destination network are burnt, so that the tokens from the original network can be released by the smart contract. A DeFi application on the Ethereum network could create a so-called “Wrapped Bitcoin” (WBTC) using an Ethereum token contract, which would peg their Ethereum-native WBTC 1:1 to the price of Bitcoin (BTC). The BTC tokens are then sent from a user wallet to a custodial service (or smart contract). Once the underlying BTC has been locked (aka “wrapped”) in this escrow contract, an equivalent amount of WBTC can be minted. Since blockchain networks don't communicate with each other, there are many centralization concerns with the current implementations of wrapped tokens. Wrapped tokens enable cross-chain liquidity and capital efficiency, both of which are critical issues in financial markets in general and DeFi in particular. While wrapped tokens are a fast and simple interoperability solution with low transaction fees, they also have a downside, as they can spread credit risk across protocols. 
       
       
         Inter Blockchain Protocol (IBC) 
          is one of several general interoperability protocols that seems to have gained market traction. It is a protocol for data transport, authentication, and data reliability that enables arbitrary cross-chain data transfer and computation. Even though they have their own validator sets and root chains, all blockchain networks that implement IBC share a common infrastructural underpinning. As a result, these networks can communicate with one another. Connected blockchain networks can transfer data packets on dedicated (trustless) channels using smart contracts that are then authenticated on the destination blockchain. IBC offers more than bridging services for token transfers. It is a general-purpose messaging protocol that can be used to transfer any type of data. This means that IBC can be used for creating (i) interchain accounts, (ii) interchain security, and (iii) interchain token transfers. Interchain accounts are particularly relevant for DeFi protocols, as they allow users to participate in DeFi applications running on various blockchain networks from a single account (user interface). Users can transfer, stake, or vote on one blockchain network (the host chain) from another blockchain network (the controller chain). Interchain accounts increase composability of DeFi protocols, potential market activity, and liquidity. IBC users can leverage the security of one blockchain network for another blockchain network. New blockchain networks potentially no longer need to bootstrap a new validator set and could instead use those of established networks. For the transfer of tokens, IBC specifies how data packets should be structured and how they should be interpreted by receiving chains.  
       
       
         Bridges 
          are another type of interoperability solution that can be set up in two different ways: (i) Trusted bridges are custodial solutions that rely on a trusted private institution to verify the transactions, holding the bridged assets in their custody. They are prone to systemic centralization risks and can be a single point of failure in cases of hacks, censorship or mismanagement. An example for such failure was the Ronin Bridge hack 
         
           
             
               58 
             
           
         
          in March 2022. (ii) Trustless bridges are non-custodial solutions managed and enforced using smart contracts. The issue with trustless bridges is that they could have inherent vulnerability in their code, which means that any flaws in the smart contract controlling the bridge could be exploited. This could be addressed with comprehensive audits. Compared to other interoperability workarounds, bridges seem to be a relatively simple solution that provide more access to liquidity. They solve many of the issues that atomic swaps and wrapped tokens are still struggling with, and generally require less technical knowledge. Bridges also have inherent risk factors, especially for tokenized events that happen on the other blockchain network. Bridge hacks can impact the price of the wrapped tokens on another blockchain network. Furthermore, users who hold WETH (Wrapped Ether) may not be able to convert back to ETH for lack of liquidity. Any Ethereum-based tokenized application depends entirely on Ethereum network security. However, if one would bridge tokens between another network on the Ethereum network, a  
         
           51% attack 
         
         
           
             
               59 
             
           
         
          on another blockchain network – that might be compromised and have to be rolled back – would mess with the security of the Ethereum network, as well. For example, imagine that ETH tokens are locked up on the Ethereum network, but the WETH on the compromised network is rolled back and doesn't exist anymore. In result, the larger the amount of assets that are bridged between two networks, the greater the honeypot for trying to compromise the network through low-security blockchain networks. This could ultimately be a security risk even for high-security blockchain networks. 
       
       
         
         
         NFT Marketplaces 
       
       
         The first token exchanges focused on the buying and selling of fungible tokens such as BTC and other protocol tokens, and later branched out to other fungible crypto assets. With the emergence of non-fungible tokens such as crypto-art (art NFTs) and other crypto-collectibles (collectible NFTs), a new type of exchanges and marketplaces emerged which could cater to the special needs of NFT in general, and different classes of NFTs in particular. Just like exchanges for fungible tokens, marketplaces for non-fungible tokenized assets provide market-making functionalities to bring buyers and sellers together, and provide efficient information dissemination amongst all market participants. However, the display-, negotiation- and trading aspects have different requirements: NFTs come with a lot of metadata attached that describes the special properties of the unique assets they represent. Depending on the type of NFT – whether it is virtual art, physical art, real estate, energy, or data – additional information about the tokenized assets needs to be exchanged between buyers and sellers before the sale. In addition to general discovery functionalities of the marketplace, this raises additional issues around how this metadata should be stored or displayed so that buyers can easily find what they want. The virtual or physical assets will also have to be virtually or physically transferred after the property rights to an NFT have been transferred from seller to buyer, which means that these specialized NFT marketplaces need to have technical, organizational and legal processes in place to conduct such a virtual or physical transfer.  
       
       
         Marketplaces for crypto-art and crypto-collectibles 
         : The general dynamics of marketplaces for physical art and tokenized art are pretty similar, and there is no need to reinvent the wheel for marketplaces for tokenized art. However, many art NFT marketplaces still struggle with certain technical, legal and procedural challenges that are related to globally accessible Web3 networks. Marketplaces for real-world art objects or installations, for example, come with a set of legal and organizational aspects around the management and custodianship of an art piece, especially if it is collectively owned by a set of fractional token-holders. Additional challenges are created by the techno-legal question of what one is actually buying – a URL to a website, the property rights to the actual artwork, the access rights to the actual artwork, or a copy with a unique digital signature? Attached to this question comes a set of other questions, such as how the rights might be used downstream. At the time of writing, art NFT marketplaces also often lack a legal framework to verify the real ownership of an NFT or verify copyright evidence. While there are still many challenges, the number of NFT marketplaces is growing. Examples for existing marketplaces are “ 
         
           Rarible 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           SuperRare 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           OpenSea 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           MakersPlace 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Maecenas 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           AtomicMarket 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Axie Marketplace 
         
         ” – to name just a few. These art marketplaces can be more or less specialized, either in a particular type of art or collectible class, or in a particular service. 
       
       
         Data NFT marketplaces 
         : The “ 
         
           Ocean protocol 
         
         ” offers a decentralized exchange for data publishers who can monetize their datasets via the issuance of Data NFTs and derived tokenized licenses, and potential buyers of licenses to these Data NFTs who are interested in these datasets (read more about the Ocean Protocol in the previous chapter of this book, “Asset Tokens & NFTs: Money with Attributes.” An even more in depth analysis can be found in another volume of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.”) “ 
         
           Acentrik 
         
         ” is another example of a potential data marketplace. It is a venture of Mercedes-Benz AG, who aim to create a data marketplace working with different players across an array of industries for decentralized data sharing. 
       
       
         Real estate NFT marketplaces 
          will come into existence once real estate deeds can be legally tokenized, an appropriate regulatory environment exists, and economic network effects manifest. At the time of writing this book, real estate tokenization is still under development and lacks the necessary network effects. However, specialized “tokenization-as-a-service” providers such as “ 
         
           Solidblocks 
         
         ” also offer their own marketplace, where one can buy, sell, collateralize, raise capital, and manage tokenized property.  
       
       
         General purpose NFT marketplaces 
         : “ 
         
           Opensea 
         
         ” is an example for a general purpose marketplace, and probably also one of the more seasoned NFT exchanges. While it is predominantly used for crypto art and crypto-collectibles, it started out and still is a general purpose marketplace where any kind of NFT (including domain names) can be traded. “ 
         
           Boson Protocol 
         
         ” is another example of a general purpose decentralized marketplace that aims to enable the tokenization, transfer and trade of any physical thing as a redeemable NFT.  
       
       
         The same trade-offs and questions that are relevant to centralized or decentralized exchanges of fungible tokens are also relevant to marketplaces for non-fungible tokens. NFT marketplaces come with additional technical challenges, as they need to transfer much more metadata. Currently, NFT-related metadata is often still stored off-chain on privately operated servers, which defies the decentralized nature of Web3. It seems that server architecture is still more accessible and easier to set up for many project founders than decentralized file storage systems. The NFT " 
         
           Everydays: The First 5000 Days 
         
         " that the artist Beeple sold for 69 million USD, for example, referenced the metadata accessible through an  
         
           IPFS 
         
          gateway,  
         
           but the actual image was stored using the private gateway of the service provider “MakersPlace 
         
         .” If MakersPlace would cease operations, the token holder would own an image that cannot be accessed.  
       
       As for the question of generalization versus specialization, it is quite likely that NFT marketplaces might end up more generalized and less specialized in the future – at least for certain NFTs. NFT marketplaces that cater to the needs of very unique asset classes – assets that have different market dynamics to other asset classes, or require to be designed around special regulatory issues – might remain specialized in spite of generalization trends. It is also worth noting that some decentralized exchanges that started out trading only fungible tokens eventually branched out to offering NFTs as well.  
         
         
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       
         
         Tokens of one blockchain network cannot be natively transferred to other networks, since the nodes in one network don’t have information about the state of tokens managed in another network. As a result, the buying and selling of tokens requires the intermediary services of an exchange.  ~ Exchanges and NFT marketplaces are the new financial intermediaries in Web3. They can decide whether they list a particular token or not, and have therefore become the market makers and gatekeepers in this emerging tokenized economy.  ~  Centralized exchanges perform intermediary services between buyers and sellers of tokens and also provide on-ramp and off-ramp services between the fiat world and the crypto world. Customer tokens are controlled by the operators of the exchange services through so-called “custodial wallets” or “cloud wallets.” While this might be more convenient for customers, it contradicts the idea of P2P money, where token holders are in full control of their funds, and fully responsible for private-key management at all times. ~  Centralized exchanges are owned by private companies and operate on classic client-server technology. All token swaps are brokered “off-chain” on the private order books of these exchanges, which are stored on their private server infrastructure. This means that the order book transactions of centralized exchanges are not accessible to customers or other outsiders. CEX are not subject to the same security mechanisms as blockchain networks. Their customers surrender full control over their tokens to outdated systems. ~  KYC and AML laws were designed based on the logic of a traditional banking system that operates on private ledger infrastructure, not for public ledger infrastructure. Due to the pseudonymous nature of blockchain transactions, any government institution, private security company, and really anyone with the necessary know-how and access to the identity data of the users of an exchange can run big data analysis on pseudonymous token transactions, correlate it with other data footprints of a users, and discover all blockchain addresses of that user. In the light of potential data breaches, KYC and AML laws cannot be enforced one-to-one in a Web3 environment without breaching citizens’ privacy rights.  ~ Decentralized exchanges (DEX) are applications executed by a blockchain network where smart contracts replace the brokerage functions of a centralized exchange, while granting full control over the tokenized funds to their users. Token trades are settled on-chain and therefore have an audit trail that is publicly verifiable. ~  Decentralized exchanges have increasingly become “community-governed” through the vehicle of Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAOs). This means that both the decentralized application as well as the voting processes of all DAO members are executed over blockchain networks. The smart contracts, and the protocols through which they are governed, are a new type of intermediary institution in this emerging Web3, albeit more automated and publicly verifiable. These new institutions are also created and managed by people, and therefore susceptible to similar systemic risks or human error, misjudgement and misconduct – but all token transactions are publicly verifiable almost in real time without the need for third party audits.  ~  Traditional market makers are replaced by so-called “automated market makers.” Automated Market Makers (AMMs) are alternative market making mechanisms executed by a smart contract which replace the need for traditional exchanges that work with an order book system. AMMs consist of two elements: (i) One or several pools of tokens (aka liquidity pools), and (ii) the algorithm upon which the price of a trade is determined and executed.  ~  Liquidity pools are managed by smart contracts which each hold a token trading pair in escrow. Buyers and sellers can trade their tokens against this pair when using the services of a decentralized exchange according to predefined conditions. Liquidity pools usually consist of two token types that form a trading pair. Users have to deposit both tokens of a token trading pair into the liquidity pool of that trading pair in the ratio of their exchange rate. They need to provide both sides of the market in correct proportions, as their role is to provide liquidity without impacting the current market price. ~  Users of a liquidity pool never trade with other users, but instead trade against a price-finding algorithm which is determined by a mathematical formula and encoded into the smart contract. The purpose of the algorithm is to have an automated mechanism to find the exchange rate between two assets in a pool. The price finding mechanism used by AMMs differs from traditional financial markets and follows a bonding curve. ~  A bonding curve is a mathematical formula that determines the price of an exchange rate by factors such as time, amount of lender and borrowers, and the timing of their borrowing and lending. The parameters of the curve can vary depending on the type of bonding curve. The price finding mechanisms of AMMs in their current state still have many shortcomings and need to be refined.  ~  Liquidity Providers are a new type of market participant who supplies tokens to the liquidity pool, without necessarily wanting to trade them. In theory, anyone with an internet connection and the right tokens can become a liquidity provider to a liquidity pool – independent of the amount of tokens they hold – and earn a form of “interest” or ”yield” on their deposited tokens. Liquidity providers are sometimes additionally incentivized with native protocol tokens of the decentralized exchange they are contributing to. This is quite different from centralized marketplaces, where only institutional investors or high-net-worth individuals with deep pockets have the capacity to become market makers.  ~  AMMs provide a smart-contract-enabled three sided marketplace, where the trades are conducted between a buyer, seller and liquidity providers. The market mechanism is encoded in the smart contract, which executes all transactions when the conditions to a trade are met, thereby eliminating the need for centralized exchange services. ~  The first token exchanges focused on the buying and selling of fungible tokens such as BTC and other protocol tokens, and later branched out to other fungible crypto assets. With the emergence of non-fungible tokens such as crypto-art (art NFTs) and other crypto-collectibles (collectible NFTs), a new type of exchanges and marketplaces emerged which could cater to the special needs of NFT in general, and different classes of NFTs in particular. The display-, negotiation- and trading aspects have different requirements: NFTs come with a lot of metadata attached that describes the special properties of the unique assets they represent. Depending on the type of NFT – whether it is virtual art, physical art, real estate, energy, or data – additional information about the tokenized assets needs to be exchanged between buyers and sellers before the sale.

Where possible, the links will be updated on a regular basis to prevent the issue of broken links. It is only scannable on white or light colored backgrounds and might not work on dark backgrounds. The website is also accessible under following URL:  
         
           https://bit.ly/3U6JPS7
