         Tokenized Credit & Lending 
       
       
         
         Decentralized lending services use smart contracts to create marketplaces for a tokenized credit and lending system for any type of asset. In theory, any fungible and non-fungible token that represents a currency, commodity, security, real estate deed, artwork, or SME share could be tokenized and collateralized. In combination with decentralized exchanges using  
         liquidity pools 
         , decentralized lending protocols can provide a multi-sided marketplace not only  
         between  
         lenders 
          and 
          borrowers, but also sellers and buyers of tokens. Tokenization of assets in combination with tokenized credit and lending services and liquidy pools could eventually lead to a convergence of money, financial markets and the real economy. ~ 
       
       
         Tokenized lending services offer a two-sided market, using smart contracts for credit and lending of tokens. In a fully decentralized setup, tokenized lending services only require a Web3 wallet and an Internet connection to participate. They have lower operational costs than legacy financial services, and invite more trust, since verification of the compliance to lending rules, and verification of the underlying collaterals, are executed on the fly on a publicly verifiable infrastructure. This allows for more control, security, and potentially also inclusion. Security and control refers to the fact that one can choose non-custodial services – services where one stays in control of one's private keys 
         
           
             
               60 
             
           
         
          and thus in control of one's tokens, at all times. Inclusion refers to the fact that these services, which are currently complementary to the traditional  financial system, could open doors to individuals who have so far been excluded from financial services, since a full KYC process is not required. In combination with AMM-based decentralized exchanges using liquidity pools, decentralized lending protocols have enhanced capabilities and can provide a multi-sided marketplace not only between lenders and borrowers, but also between sellers and buyers of tokens. 
       
       
         Previous chapters of this book described how any commodity, fiat currency, or security of non-fungible asset classes can be tokenized. As the tokenization of our monetary systems, the financial system, and the real economy progresses, an increasing range of asset classes that previously might not have constituted a “bankable fund” can be used as collateral for tokenized lending and other DeFi products, thereby creating a liquid lending market for these tokenized assets. The integration of such tokenized non-bankable assets with lending and borrowing schemes would allow for instant transactions, which surpasses the possibilities of the legacy systems we have today. This could change the dynamics of our global economic system. However, while tokenized lending services have much potential, they are still in their infancy. They also introduce their own, new set of challenges. Currently, tokenized lenders have to bear high risks associated with these challenges, which will be outlined in this chapter. 
       
       
         
         
         Tokenized Lending 
       
       
         In the current monetary and financial system, government institutions such as central banks in combination with other public institutions control the overall money supply of a country and regulate the national financial market. They determine who receives a banking license and control the interest rates and collateral rates applicable to various forms of credit. Depositors of money usually do not have any control over what the bank does with their money. They have no real-time control over the operational activities of their banks, such as who the bank lends their money to, or if it is keeping enough securities to pay them back their deposits when needed. In most cases, depositors cede ownership of their assets to the bank when they deposit money. They often do so unknowingly, in exchange for a bank guarantee. This is a type of insurance – usually capped to around 100.000 EUR per person per bank account. While private forms of credit also exist, they are mostly given out by friends and family – often on a trust basis – and are harder to enforce if the counterparty to the private loan fails to fulfill their side of the bargain. 
       
       
         In a DeFi world operated by blockchain networks and decentralized applications, anyone can become a creditor of tokenized assets, without the need for traditional financial institutions or analog forms of private credit. All transactions of a tokenized loan are brokered by smart contracts that are audited on a public blockchain infrastructure, instead of the private ledgers of banks or CeFi startups. Anyone can track the state of their tokens at all times. The smart contract replaces the functions of a bank which would usually broker the financial liquidity between lenders and borrowers. Tokens are locked up in an escrow contract as collateral, which automatically pays out periodic interest rates to the wallet of the lender of the borrowed tokens. Due to the publicly verifiable nature of the underlying infrastructure, these collateral assets cannot be over-leveraged through private backdoor deals, or embezzled. Two forms of tokenized lending exist: (i) “P2P lending,” and (ii) “liquidity pool lending.” While most protocols started out with P2P lending, liquidity pool lending has become the dominant form of lending in most DeFi protocols.  
       
       
         P2P lending 
         : In a P2P lending setup, any individual or institution can lend tokens to another individual or institution directly. The smart contract brokers the tokenized lending and collateral management wallet to wallet. However, in such a setup there is a “coincidence of wants” problem of matching individuals who need a certain amount of tokens with someone else who wants to lend at the same time. 
       
       
         Liquidity pools 
         : The introduction of liquidity pools resolved the coincidence-of-wants problem of P2P lending. It allows lenders to provide or to take liquidity to a lending pool – by depositing unneeded tokens – and earn interest with them. Other private individuals or institutions can now borrow this particular type of tokenized asset from the lending pool at the time when they need it. A token holder who has USDC tokens that they don’t plan to spend in the near future, could use a lending protocol such as “ 
         
           Aave 
         
         ” and deposit their USDC to a USDC liquidity pool to earn rewards – which in turn are paid by borrowers taking out USDC loans. USDC token holders who lend their tokens to the pool (aka liquidity providers or creditors), can use these deposited assets as collateral to borrow other tokenized assets. Liquidity providers, in effect, earn interest, fees, and other rewards on the tokens they provide (lend) to the liquidity pool for the duration of their loan, which they measure in terms of annual percentage yields (APY). The liquidity pool smart contract brokers the transactions against a commission or transaction fee (more on this in the previous chapter). The interest is proportionally allocated to all liquidity providers who contribute USDC to the pool. An automated market maker (AMM) defines the algorithm which determines borrowing and lending rates, while the liquidity pool provides the funds that are traded. Such a setup resolves the coincidence-of-wants problem and, at the same time, creates more liquidity for the supply and demand of a particular token class.  
       
       
         The bureaucratic efficiency of Web3-based lending protocols can significantly reduce operational costs and create more efficient credit and lending markets for each tokenized asset class. The effects could be more affordable loans, which would also be available to a much wider range of individuals and institutions than today. Any token holder, for example from Africa, could earn passive income on their token holdings through interest paid by a borrower in South America, and vice versa. 
       
       
         
         
         Tokenized Borrowing  
       
       
         In the current financial system, when a private individual or institution needs to lend money to buy a house, or leverage capital to create a business, they usually need to provide some form of collateral to the bank. This collateral serves as a guarantee for the bank, and can be seized by the bank if the borrower fails to pay back their debt. The percentage of the required collateral depends on what the investment is used for, on the creditworthiness of the lender, and also on the regulatory framework of the respective jurisdictions the borrower is subject to. The creditworthiness of borrowers, in turn, depends on what assets they own – such as real estate holdings or company shares – which could potentially be seized by the bank. Expected future income could also be a variable when determining the collateral ratio, which is why banks often ask for income statements and employment contracts. The alternative to bank loans would be private loans given out by so-called “loan sharks” who very often charge exorbitant interest rates in the absence of collateral or sufficient collateral. Lending from family and friends only happens on a trust basis and often doesn't require collateral.  
       
       
         In the case of DeFi-based tokenized borrowing, loans are only given out to those who can provide a tokenized collateral in exchange. This collateral serves as a guarantee that the lenders will be repaid, in the absence of personal identification and reputation systems. As opposed to the traditional financial system that requires credit checks and other paper-based formalities regarding collateral management, DeFi protocols don't use such credit checks – at least at the time of writing this book. They require full-collateralization, or in many cases even “over-collateralization.” Over-collateralization refers to the fact that – since many tokens have highly volatile prices – one has to lock up tokenized assets worth much more than the tokenized assets one wants to borrow. Borrowers can lock accepted crypto assets as collateral in a smart contract to take out a tokenized loan. If the borrower fails to repay their tokenized debts on time, the borrower’s collateral tokens are transferred to the lender's wallet by the smart contract after automated procedural checks. Equivalent to lending, there are two forms of tokenized borrowing: (i) “P2P borrowing,” and (ii) “liquidity pool borrowing.”  
       
       
         To maintain full-collateralization at all times, lending protocols have inbuilt mechanisms which can be triggered if the market price of the collateral begins to drop significantly. When a user falls below their collateralization ratio, the smart contract is designed such that collateral tokens can be sold at a pre-defined spot price or a market auction by other market participants who discover the under-collateralization and can make money by detecting and triggering these actions. This mechanism intends to mitigate counterparty risk of the lender, in case the price of the collateral tokens drops too fast.  
       
       
         Once the tokenization of the real economy progresses, potential borrowers could use a range of tokenized real-world assets as collateral, which are currently not accepted by banks as a form of collateral. Previously non-bankable assets, such as household goods or art, could be tokenized and used as collateral for receiving a loan. However, because of the need for full-collateralization, decentralized borrowing services are at this time not a practical solution for people who are underfunded and actually in need of a loan, which excludes large parts of the population. Decentralized borrowing protocols are mainly used by professional traders for the sake of obtaining so called “ 
         
           margin 
         
         ” 
         
           
             
               61 
             
           
         
          for “leverage trading.” Tokenized lending is currently also used by private holders of crypto assets who want to stay in crypto, and take out loans against their current holding instead of selling their crypto assets against fiat money. This often happens for tax reasons, or because they don’t want to move out of crypto but use their assets as collaterals for other investments, which conventional banks would otherwise not accept.  
       
       
         
         
         Over-Collateralization 
       
       
         The practice of full-collateralization replaces lender protection mechanisms typical to traditional finance, where banks and other financial institutions personally identify their clients, and use the services of credit rating agencies that inform them about the creditworthiness of a borrower to reduce the need for collaterals. The purpose is to reduce the credit risk in the face of pseudonymous lending. Given the considerable price fluctuation of many crypto assets, DeFi applications had to price in additional volatility buffers when determining the collateralization ratio, which eventually led to the practice of over-collateralization. A minimum collateralization ratio is typically 101-105% (or less), but only for "trusted" stable tokens (such as USDC), with almost no volatility. The minimum for tokens with more volatility would be around 110-150%, which means that one can only take out a loan at the value of 60-90% of the collateral deposited into the smart contract. However, this is the minimum, and more of a historic number from the early years of DeFi. At the time of writing this book, the typical collateralization ratio for some collateral types can be up to 350%, which results in even lower loan-to-collateral value ratios (as low as 25%). Lending protocols have started to apply more differentiated collateralization methods, where ratios vary depending on the volatility risk associated with the asset used as collateral.  
       
       
         In spite of over-collateralization, DeFi loans can be interesting to some investors in bull markets when prices rise and market throughput is high. An investor can lock up 1000 EUR worth of ETH to borrow 500 EUR worth of DAI in exchange, which can be used to buy 500 EUR more ETH. This could be interesting for leveraging existing assets in a bull market without creating a taxable event by taking a crypto loan out of a crypto asset. Such a strategy is only relevant in jurisdictions where investors are not taxed as long as they don’t leave the crypto assets in exchange for fiat.  
       
       
         With more reliable identity management systems that can hold borrowers and lenders accountable, collateralization levels will eventually fall. After all, over-collateralization is an inefficient use of capital and more importantly, a significant barrier of entry for those who are most in need of a loan. It excludes people who don’t have the luxury of owning many assets and who are in need of credit to make ends meet. It favors the rich getting richer, and is only a possible strategy in bull markets. However, under-collateralization will probably only be possible for taking out tokenized loans from a DeFi protocol once more nuanced credit-risk management mechanisms for lender protection are introduced. Such mechanisms would include (i) decentralized reputation mechanisms, (ii) decentralized arbitration systems, or (iii) decentralized payback enforcement mechanisms. “ 
         
           Maple Finance 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           TrueFi 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           Gearbox 
         
         ” have started working on some form of chain credit score solution. As regulation becomes more explicit towards DeFi, the KYC loophole of DeFi protocols will probably also close. Under-collateralization would be necessary to make lending or borrowing more accessible and inclusive. It is quite likely that DeFi protocols will start implementing certain KYC practices, as soon as AML (anti money laundering) regulation starts to target DeFi protocol participants.  
       
       
         As long as pseudonymity is standard practice in DeFi protocols, and decentralized credit risk solutions don’t exist, strong risk management practices will be necessary to protect not only the lenders, but also to avoid market collapses and system fallouts. As recent market failures in the wake of the Terra(LUNA) collapse in May 2022 have shown, DeFi lending protocols were able to cope with the sudden price crash in the crypto market, since they were sufficiently over-collateralized. Many CeFi startups that were mistaken for DeFi protocols, however, did not have such a collateralization discipline, and instead conducted under-collateralized lending to individuals and institutions they thought were “good borrowers.” It was exactly those CeFi startups that went bankrupt in the wake of the Terra(LUNA) collapse. While it has been suggested by some players in DeFi to deviate from strict over-collateralization, and to lend out locked-in collateral tokens, this would create exactly the same systemic market risks of the traditional financial systems which ultimately led to the global financial crisis of 2007-2009; a scenario that first Bitcoin and later DeFi wanted to avoid in the first place.  
       
       
         
         
         History of P2P Lending Protocols 
       
       
         The first lending protocols in the early days of decentralized lending resolved many issues of “dormant capital.” Back then, most investors bought cryptocurrencies, crypto assets and other tokens for long-term investment only. Unless the tokens were spent for day-to-day payments, tokens often stayed in a hardware wallet, software wallet, or paper wallet, as token holders expected their value to grow over time. Tokenized lending protocols converted this dormant tokenized capital into so-called “working capital.” While the first protocols were designed to be P2P, they did not resolve the coincidence-of-wants problems to match creditors and lenders. Liquidity pools combined with AMMs resolved this issue. Lenders could lock their tokenized assets in liquidity pools, which borrowers could use to borrow tokens from.  
       
       
         In the bull run that was triggered by the 2020 “DeFi summer,” investors were motivated by the high yields they were promised by many of the emerging lending protocols. The promised yields were often unsustainable, at least in the medium to long run, and designed in an attempt to bootstrap a minimum viable economy for their lending protocol to create enough liquidity and outperform other protocols. These high yields were either unsustainable in the first place, or only possible based on leveraging the collateral tokens multiple times against other protocols or CeFi services, basically running a fractional-reserve scheme that was hard to understand for the average investor. Various fancy new and somewhat interchangeable terms popped up – “yield farming,” “liquidity mining,” “liquidity harvesting” – that all describe the act of lending existing token holdings to a liquidity pool against the possibility of earning interest and potentially also additional protocol rewards. While in the early years of DeFi, only specialized liquidity protocols existed that only allowed credit and lending, token exchanges now also started to offer liquidity pool services, as they are an integral part of providing enough liquidity to a decentralized exchange. Given the fact that exchanges are the new banks in this tokenized economy, it makes sense that they also want to broker credits and loans, just as banks do in the traditional financial system. The following protocols have played an integral part in developing decentralized lending protocols: 
       
       
         
           MakerDAO 
         
           
         is one of the most seasoned DeFi projects that was launched in 2017 with the aim to create a stable token system. It was one of the first DeFi protocols that gained significant traction and is still a market leader in the stable token market. Their stable token DAI comes with an inbuilt decentralized lending aspect, as it can only be issued against depositing (aka lending) a tokenized collateral into a smart contract. MarkerDAO was probably the first use case where token holders could borrow money on a large scale without having to go through a centralized financial institution. This made them a first mover in the decentralized lending game. While they started out with ETH only, today a range of tokens are accepted as collateral for taking out a DAI loan. At the time of writing this book, MakerDAO is one of the largest DeFi applications on the Ethereum network. It seems to be the first DeFi protocol that transitioned from centralized governance via a foundation, to a form of token-based governance through the vehicle of a decentralized organization which is collectively governed by all MKR token holders. Over 400 applications use their stable token (DAI) and other services. (Read more about the MakerDAO use case in a previous chapter on “Stable Tokens” in this book. A more extensive analysis of DAI & MakerDAO is available in another volume of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.”) 
       
       
         
           Uniswap 
         
          is a decentralized exchange that was launched on the Ethereum mainnet in 2018. As opposed to other early decentralized exchanges such as EtherDelta, Uniswap adopted the concept of liquidity pools and automated market makers instead of working with an order book. They were the first project to implement liquidity pools to bootstrap liquidity for decentralized exchanges. This created a completely new multi-sided marketplace for tokenized credit and lending which allowed for more liquid buying and selling of tokens without considerable slippage. Uniswap set up a system where each token type has a global borrow and lend pool that represents a market for that token’s borrow and lend positions. Any token holder can contribute their tokens to the liquidity pool and earn interest on their token holdings. The 2020 upgrade of the Uniswap protocol introduced direct token-to-token swaps, instead of relying on asset pairs with ETH as a fixed base token. The protocol upgrade also introduced “flash swaps,” a flash loan function that allows users to withdraw tokens for instant on-chain trades, and return them by the end of the transaction. The upgrade was designed to be more resistant to potential attacks and manipulation, such as the “flash attacks'' of February 2020, which will be described later in this chapter. The first version of Uniswap was built with funding from the Ethereum Foundation. At the time of writing this book, Uniswap is a blockchain-centric exchange that lets users swap only Ethereum-based tokens. Over  
         
           650 tokens can be traded over 1500 trading pairs 
         
         . Uniswap is also notable for being the first DeFi protocol to reward their users with a governance token (UNI) when they launched the Uniswap token (UNI) and rewarding all the previous users and liquidity providers of Uniswap with a retrospective airdrop which was an added incentive to becoming a liquidity provider. This contributed to what has become known as DeFi Summer. In its v3 protocol upgrade, Uniswap introduced the concept of “concentrated liquidity,” which allows liquidity providers to select a price range within which they are willing to deposit their tokens. This means that liquidity providers can earn fees only for trades within their selected range, and will need to reset their price ranges in a volatile market to optimize returns – but it also mitigates the risk of “ 
         
           impermanent loss 
         
         .” 
         
           
             
               62 
             
           
         
       
       
         
           Compound 
         
          was launched in 2018 as a pure decentralized lending protocol using liquidity pools. Lenders could deposit their tokens into lending pools to earn interest. The interest rate of each token lent was algorithmically defined, based on supply and demand of tokens in each pool, and thus variable. One could receive one type of token in exchange for depositing another type of token (e.g. cDAI for DAI.) The loans had no fixed durations, which meant that lenders could withdraw their funds at any time, and the collateralization ratio was asset-dependent. In summer 2020, Compound deployed a new mechanism on top of their liquidity pools which they called “liquidity mining.” They introduced a governance token (COMP) to give their users additional incentives to provide liquidity to their protocol. This resulted in a dramatic increase in the annual percentage yield (APY) for the supply and borrowing of tokens. It also enabled the development of so-called “yield farming,” where users were incentivised to keep switching between borrowing and lending different tokens to achieve the best yield possible. This inspired other DeFi protocols to issue and distribute protocol tokens via liquidity mining, thereby creating more and more yield farming opportunities. The distribution of the COMP token also made the governance of the protocol more inclusive, as more users of the protocol started to receive voting rights in the system. Similar to MakerDAO and other DeFi protocols, Compound changed its governance structure and is now collectively  
         
           governed by all COMP token holders 
         
         . The voting rights tied to COMP tokens can be delegated to other users. How many of the token holders participate or delegate their tokens is an important question which needs to be analyzed in order to better understand the power and participation structures in the protocol. Compound’s governance model was adopted by other DeFi projects; and in response to other protocols mimicking this incentive system, an avalanche of new users flocked into decentralized lending protocols, contributing to a DeFi bull that kicked off DeFi summer. 
       
       
         
           Curve 
         
          is a blockchain-centric decentralized exchange, similar to Uniswap, that operates on the Ethereum network. However, they only focus on stable tokens, not other types of tokens. Curve protocol introduced the concept of so-called “veTokens,” to mitigate the challenges of liquidity pools by using lock-up periods, with the aim to create a sustainable economic system. The mechanism design of veTokens changed the incentive system for liquidity mining. 
           
         The purpose of veTokens was to give token holders “skin in the game” so they have an interest in the long-term success of the DeFi protocol, rather than being incentivized to sell their tokens fast to generate short-term profit. The expectation was that this would increase the total value locked (TLV) without over-inflating the supply of protocol tokens (CRV). The voting mechanisms tied to the tokens are also more sophisticated than previous governance tokens of DeFi protocols, with the result that veToken holders are more motivated to participate in protocol governance. VeTokenomics is not a one-size fits all solution, and also has its downsides. But the long-term perspective in their economics design motivated many other lending protocols to adapt the veToken model. Some have adapted the mechanism design to the purpose of their own system. Examples for these early adopters are “ 
         
           Yearn 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Synthetix 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Pancakeswap 
         
         ” or “ 
         
           Perpetual 
         
         .” It is also worth pointing out that Curve gained a lot of user traction because the protocol was designed to integrate with the Compound and Yearn protocols, which made it much more useful to the stakeholders. 
       
       
         
           Yearn 
         
          is a yield-optimizing protocol that was developed in 2020 by Andre Cronje with the purpose of maximizing DeFi capabilities by allowing users to automatically switch between different lending protocols. “Yearn executes automated trading strategies across Ethereum-based DeFi platforms using money robots. A Yearn money robot, for example, can earn trading fees on the Curve protocol, borrow money via Aave’s flash loans, and lend money to other Yearn money robots.” 
         
           
             
               63 
             
           
         
          The founders decided to distribute a governance token (YFI) to the Yearn community, so that the protocol could be collectively governed by all YFI token holders. YFI tokens were not manually distributed to arbitrary participants, but distributed via liquidity mining as an incentive to participate in their network. This motivated many users to become liquidity providers and stood in stark contrast to the practices of other DeFi protocols at the time, which distributed governance tokens to VCs, founders and the developers of the protocols. 
       
       
         
           Centrifuge 
         
          was founded with the purpose of providing a lending market for tokenized real-world assets. Their value proposition was that many real-world assets – such as art, invoices, mortgages, or streaming royalties – have less price volatility than native crypto assets, providing more stable collateral types. On the flip side, by accepting RWA as collateral, Centrifuge could provide a liquid market for real-world assets that had previously been illiquid. The protocol only accepted assets with a stable value or with a consistent payment stream as collateral. Centrifuge created a legal security framework for each asset pool to ensure that lenders could recourse to the real-world assets, and that the property rights would be transferred. Each asset pool was designed to be an associated legal entity (Special Purpose Vehicle). They developed a set of legal contracts that delegate responsibilities to on-chain smart contracts, where all tokenized real-world assets are valued according to a fair-value DCF model. 
       
       
         
           Aave 
         
          is a decentralized non-custodial liquidity protocol that was founded in 2017 and originally called ETHLend. It is worth mentioning because it has liquidity pools for a range of assets including real-world assets, where it partners with Centrifuge to offer pools for real-world assets (RWAs) including real estate, freight, shipping bills, and payment advances. Just like other liquidity protocols, depositors provide liquidity to the market in return for passive income. Another thing that made Aave stand out was the introduction of “flash loans,” which allow for undercollateralized loans if the loan is settled within one block.  
       
       
         
         
         Flash Loans & Flash Attacks 
       
       
         Flash loans are a specific type of instantaneous and collateral-free loan that are only valid within one blockchain transaction and must be repaid by the end of that transaction. The concept was first introduced in 2018 by “ 
         
           Marble Protocol 
         
         ,” but was popularized by “ 
         
           Aave 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           dYdX 
         
         .” Like many other early DeFi protocols, Marble contributed to important innovation, but didn’t survive its first years of existence. In a flash loan setup, a borrower can get any amount of tokens from a liquidity pool without the need to deposit a collateral, provided that the borrower can return all tokens, including borrowing fees, within the same blockchain transaction. The default and illiquidity risk that a lender (in this case the liquidity pool) usually bears when loaning funds is reduced to zero, because the borrower has to repay the borrowed tokens within the same blockchain transaction – otherwise the smart contract will not execute the loan.  
       
       
         In a flash loan, there are several operations: (i) an operation that reflects the loan itself, (ii) a operation that uses the funds loaned for a specific purpose and (iii) an operation that repays the loan, including interest, within the same block of transactions executed by the blockchain network (also referred to as “epoch”). A blockchain transaction can include an arbitrary amount of computation (usually limited by block size), and perform various operations. This means that one blockchain transaction could include all of the operations mentioned above. Only if this blockchain transaction returns the loan within this same transaction, will the loan actually be executed by the network. The loans are taken from a public, smart-contract-governed liquidity pool. This means that anyone could borrow the entire amount of tokens available in the pool at any point in time.  
       
       
         Due to the atomic nature 
         
           
             
               64 
             
           
         
          of blockchain networks, transactions can be reverted during execution if the condition of a repayment is not satisfied. This means that a flash loan transaction will fail in the case of (i) insufficient transaction fees, (ii) conflicting transactions, or (iii) if another condition within the transaction cannot be met. The combination of DeFi components that are relevant in the context of a flash loan are the services of decentralized exchanges, decentralized margin trading, or credit/lending services. At the time of writing this book, flash loans have been predominantly used by professional traders for arbitrage opportunities or the liquidation 
         
           
             
               65 
             
           
         
          and refinancing of crypto loans. 
         
           
             
               66 
             
           
         
           
       
       
         Flash loans also have attack vectors – so-called “flash attacks” – which refer to capital-intensive attack vectors on a DeFi protocol. The first flash attacks occurred in 2020 on “ 
         
           bZx 
         
         ,” a decentralized lending service. An anonymous person or group of people without any funds instantaneously borrowed hundreds of thousands of USD with ETH, exploiting a series of vulnerable on-chain protocols that had not been stress tested before, within a single Ethereum transaction. This happened in spite of multiple prior warnings from within the crypto community. Months before, an anonymous hacker, SamCZSun, 
         
           
             
               67 
             
           
         
          exposed the possibility that flash loans could be used to manipulate data feeds about asset prices (aka oracles). Taylor Monahan, the founder of Mycrypto.com, had also pointed out vulnerabilities in public tweets. 
         
           
             
               68 
             
           
         
          Even though bZx claimed to have fixed the problem, flash loans were used to drain around 954,000 USD total in two attacks within four days: once on February 14, 2020, and the second time during a copycat attack with a few modifications on February 18, 2020. The attacker(s) took advantage of these oracle vulnerabilities and exploited a bug within the bZx protocol’s code to secure the payout.  
       
       
         These attacks were possible because of the price spreads for the same token across various exchanges. In a DeFi setup, smart contracts must have information about the value of the collateral token at all times. This data is collected from outside oracles, e.g. provided by token exchanges. However, as opposed to traditional financial markets, where stocks are often traded on one particular stock exchange only, and there is one reliable source for a stock’s price, tokens can be traded on various exchanges, and very often have highly volatile price spreads across and within exchanges. This spread across different token exchanges creates arbitrage opportunities. One could, therefore, profit by borrowing tokens at a low price, then selling at a higher price before repaying the loan. This whole process can be performed within the same transaction, on-chain, since most DeFi services (including many decentralized exchanges) run on the Ethereum network. Flash loans can be used by borrowers to leverage almost unlimited amounts of funds to profit from such arbitrage possibilities.  
       
       
         These exploits show how markets with low liquidity and smart contracts are prone to attacks, and that flash loans in combination with smart contracts that have unintentional loopholes and/or unreliable data feeds can be exploited. The attacker(s) used the borrowed tokens of the flash loan to manipulate the market price of an token backed by BTC on a decentralized exchange with little market depth, pumping the price from 38 to 109.8, using a chain of transactions and also exploiting other loopholes in the code, and repaying the flash loan at a profit of 350,000 USD (600,000 USD in the second attack). In the current financial industry, such market manipulation can only be conducted by individuals or institutions with deep pockets. While some would argue that flash loans democratize market manipulation, it is important to point out that only people with enough industry knowledge can profit from such arbitrage opportunities – not the average investor. 
       
       
         In the wake of these incidents, it was discussed whether to refer to these incidents as “attacks,” “hacks,” or “exploits.” The discourse was reminiscent of the discussions around TheDAO incident in 2016. The attacks demonstrate that the DeFi community needs to develop attack-resistant mechanisms for a sustainable DeFi architecture. It has become more obvious that smart contract code needs to be sufficiently audited for potential economic exploits that might be to the detriment of the majority of investors. Reliable data feeds and the liquidity issues of certain exchanges were well known, so the bZx flash attack could have been prevented with enough due diligence on the side of the smart contract developers.  
       
       
         
         
         Staking 
       
       
         The term “staking” in the context of many DeFi protocols has become somewhat synonymous with lending tokens and locking them up for some kind of tokenized yield, often in the form of governance tokens issued by a DeFi protocol. The term has been misused by some DeFi protocols who issued their own tokens in the bull years of DeFi. This was probably done to market their own protocol tokens to potential buyers and motivate them to then lock their newly purchased protocol tokens against the issuance of tokenized rewards. The original term “staking” is used in relation to a security mechanism (“ 
         
           Proof-of-Stake 
         
         ”) in blockchain networks. While it has certain similarities to lending, it has very different game theoretic origins, and also a significantly different market design than locking up one’s tokens for a lending protocol. The game theoretic mechanisms of simple lending services are less complex than that of Proof-of-Stake staking. 
       
       
         Staking in the context of blockchain protocols is a market mechanism for providing one's tokens to a blockchain protocol directly, or via third party institutions, who need the tokens for collateral to run a staking service. The purpose of staking tokens is to collectively maintain a ledger of transactions by participating in the consensus mechanism  
         
           Proof-of-Stake 
         
          (PoS) or a variation of the mechanism, such as Delegated-Proof-of-Stake (DPoS). PoS is an alternative consensus mechanism for public and permissionless blockchain networks. Blockchain networks such as Ethereum, Polkadot, Cosmos, Avalanche, Cardano, or BNB Smart Chain use this mechanism. Token holders of these blockchain networks contribute to the security of the network by running a validator node, and lock their network tokens as a collateral. These tokens can be “slashed” (destroyed) if the validator node does not conform to the network validation rules. Unlike Proof-of-Work (PoW), which uses computational work to contribute to the security mechanism of a blockchain network, Proof-of-Stake relies on an economic collateral mechanism to ensure that participants validate blocks according to the rules. Instead of investing in hardware and energy costs, a validator in PoS needs to download a client that follows the consensus protocol on their computer, and stake a minimum collateral of network tokens. In addition to the “block rewards,” they can earn transaction fees by running a validator node. The crypto-economic security mechanisms of different Proof-of-Stake systems vary. In general, it can be said that the protocol defines the minimum stake that needs to be provided as collateral, and randomizes who gets to build the next block and collect fees. Validators are selected randomly on a block-to-block basis, to provide the next block by confirming and bundling transactions. By staking tokens, token holders can generate additional income by earning some form of interest on their staked tokens, instead of passively managing their tokens from their wallets. While more validators improve overall network security, the rewards also get divided by a larger number of validators. (More on different consensus mechanisms in another book of the Token Economy Series titled “Web3 Infrastructure.”) 
       
       
         Instead of downloading the protocol to their own hardware and operating a staking node themselves, users could also deposit tokens in a staking pool. This pool is operated by a service provider who will operate the staking node and share profits with the token holders providing the stake. The advantage of using a staking service is that one does not need a minimum stake, but can contribute with a fraction of the minimum stake, and also outsource the operational overhead of operating a validator node. Depending on the service provider, this fraction can differ. This act of outsourcing is sometimes referred to as "liquid staking." Staking service providers can be operated by a DeFi protocol (such as Lido or Rocketpool, who offer staking services for the Ethereum network), or a CeFi service such as a centralized exchange. Staking with third party providers often requires a specific lock-up or “vesting” period. Users cannot withdraw or transfer their tokens, even when market conditions change. The level of services and the interest rates offered vary, depending on the provider. While the act of depositing tokens with a staking service has similarities to lending, the market risk and benefits associated are different from those associated with lending to a bank or DeFi protocol. 
       
       
         
         
         Challenges & Outlook 
       
       
         While tokenized lending creates exciting new possibilities, the scene is still nascent. At the time of writing this book, decentralized lending services cannot compete with legacy financial systems because of usability issues, attack vectors and inclusion issues: Tokenized lenders using DeFi protocols have to bear high risks such as impermanent loss and as a result of potential vulnerabilities or bugs in the smart contracts. They face liquidation risk, suffer from lack of inclusion, as well as from the absence of a decentralized “lender of last resort.” 
       
       
         Impermanent loss 
          refers to the fact that the value of their deposited tokens can fall, compared to when it was first deposited.  
       
       
         Vulnerabilities or bugs in the smart contracts 
         : Since decentralized lending contracts are automatically enforced, they are also prone to programming vulnerabilities that don't reflect the intended use of the smart contract and can be exploited by savvy attackers.  
       
       
         Liquidation risk 
          refers to the fact that – in the absence of personal identification and reputation systems – if and when the price of the collateral drops below the price of the loan, the loan is liquidated automatically, which means the depositor bears a loss. 
       
       
         Inclusion 
         : At the time of writing this book, the lack of personal identification requires over-collateralization, which excludes people who are in need of a loan for daily survival, because it is exactly those people who don’t have enough tokenized collateral assets for a loan. Furthermore, many decentralized lending services are not user-friendly enough yet. This means that only tech and finance savvy people can use many of the decentralized lending protocols, which practically excludes most people from participating in decentralized lending. To fill this gap, many CeFi services have moved into the DeFi space – they offer custodial services for tokenized lending, but are not really P2P in nature. While CeFi services offer better usability for potential lenders, they also require a full KYC process, which again excludes billions of underbanked or undocumented people worldwide that were supposed to be included by the emergence of P2P financial services in the first place.  
       
       
         Lender of last resort 
         : In a nation state setup, it is the role of the government to apply fiscal and monetary politics where they adapt the interest rate to stimulate or contract local economic interactions. Central banks and other (semi-)governmental institutions act as lenders of last resort to mitigate financial shocks or cover failures of institutions that are considered “too big to fail.” In a globalized cryptocurrency world, it is still unclear how and by whom this role will be fulfilled – especially when it comes to the “lender of last resort.” How can the function of a central bank as lender of last resort be meaningfully decentralized? 
       
         
         
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       
         
         Tokenized lending services offer a two-sided market, using smart contracts for credit and lending of tokens. In a fully decentralized setup, tokenized lending services only require a Web3 wallet and an Internet connection to participate. The smart contract replaces the functions of a bank which would usually broker the financial liquidity between lenders and borrowers. Tokens are locked up in an escrow contract as collateral, which automatically pays out periodic interest rates to the wallet of the lender of the borrowed tokens. Due to the publicly verifiable nature of the underlying infrastructure, these collateral assets cannot be over-leveraged through private backdoor deals, or embezzled. This allows for more control, security, and potentially also inclusion.  ~ Two forms of tokenized lending exist: (i) “P2P lending” and (ii) “liquidity pool lending.” While most protocols started out with P2P lending, liquidity pool lending has become the dominant form of lending in most DeFi protocols.  ~ In a P2P lending setup, any individual or institution can lend tokens to another individual or institution directly. The smart contract brokers the tokenized lending and collateral management wallet to wallet. However, in such a setup there is a “coincidence of wants” problem of matching individuals who need a certain amount of tokens with someone else who wants to lend at the same time. ~ The introduction of liquidity pools resolved the coincidence-of-wants problem of P2P lending. It allows lenders to provide or to take liquidity to a lending pool – by depositing unneeded tokens – and earn interest with them. Other private individuals or institutions can now borrow this particular type of tokenized asset from the lending pool at the time when they need it.  ~ The practice of full-collateralization replaces lender protection mechanisms typical to traditional finance, where banks and other financial institutions personally identify their clients, and use the services of credit rating agencies that inform them about the creditworthiness of a borrower to reduce the need for collaterals. The purpose is to reduce the credit risk in the face of pseudonymous lending. Given the considerable price fluctuation of many crypto assets, DeFi applications had to price in additional volatility buffers when determining the collateralization ratio, which eventually led to the practice of over-collateralization. ~ To maintain full-collateralization at all times, lending protocols have inbuilt mechanisms which can be triggered if the market price of the collateral begins to drop significantly. When a user falls below their collateralization ratio, the smart contract is designed such that collateral tokens can be sold at a pre-defined spot price or a market auction by other market participants who discover the under-collateralization and can make money by detecting and triggering these actions. This mechanism intends to mitigate counterparty risk of the lender, in case the price of the collateral tokens drops too fast.  ~ Flash loans are a specific type of instantaneous and collateral-free loan that are only valid within one blockchain transaction and must be repaid by the end of that transaction. In a flash loan setup, a borrower can get any amount of tokens from a liquidity pool without the need to deposit a collateral, provided that the borrower can return all tokens, including borrowing fees, within the same blockchain transaction. The default and illiquidity risk that a lender (in this case the liquidity pool) usually bears when loaning funds is reduced to zero, because the borrower has to repay the borrowed tokens within the same blockchain transaction – otherwise the smart contract will not execute the loan.  ~ In a flash loan, there are several operations: (i) an operation that reflects the loan itself, (ii) an operation that uses the funds loaned for a specific purpose, and (iii) an operation that repays the loan, including interest, within the same block of transactions executed by the blockchain network (also referred to as “epoch”). The loans are taken from a public, smart-contract-governed liquidity pool. This means that anyone could borrow the entire amount of tokens available in the pool at any point in time. A flash loan transaction will fail in the case of (i) insufficient transaction fees, (ii) conflicting transactions, or (iii) if another condition within the transaction cannot be met. Flash loans also have attack vectors – so-called “flash attacks” – which refer to capital-intensive attack vectors on a DeFi protocol. ~ While tokenized lending creates exciting new possibilities, the scene is still nascent. At the time of writing this book, decentralized lending services cannot compete with legacy financial systems because of usability issues, attack vectors and inclusion issues: (i) Tokenized lenders using DeFi protocols have to bear high risks such as impermanent loss and (ii) as a result of potential vulnerabilities or bugs in the smart contracts. They face (iii) liquidation risk, suffer from (iv) lack of inclusion, and (v) the absence of a decentralized “lender of last resort.”

Where possible, the links will be updated on a regular basis to prevent the issue of broken links. It is only scannable on white or light colored backgrounds and might not work on dark backgrounds.
