         Tokenized Derivatives, Synthetics & Insurances 
       
       
         
         Derivatives are financial instruments that allow investors to get exposure to the price movement of an underlying asset without owning the asset. Derivatives have also become an important financial instrument in an increasingly tokenized economy.  
         Synthetics are a special type of derivative that has gained importance in traditional finance, and has become one of the most important use cases for tokenization. They simulate the risk and reward profile of other financial instruments, usually by combining a set of other financial instruments, such as one or more derivatives, which result in the same risk profile as investing in an underlying security. This chapter will explain why m 
         any token use cases presented  
         in 
          this book are derivatives by nature, and why it is important to understand the core concepts of financial derivatives before one can understand  
         potential implications of 
          tokenization 
          on money, finance and the 
          real economy. 
         ~ 
       
       
         Traditional finance distinguishes two main types of financial instruments (i) securities and (ii) financial derivatives. Securities encompass equity instruments (such as shares of a company), debt instruments (such as mortgages or bonds), or hybrid forms (such as convertible bonds). Derivatives are different from securities, since they detach the market risk of the underlying asset or obligation from the ownership of that asset via a contractual agreement, which can be traded separately from the underlying asset. The underlying asset does not have to be acquired by the person buying a derivative, as derivatives separate the ownership from the volatility risk of the underlying asset. They do not have an inherent value, but rely on the value of the underlying asset, thereby allowing investors to get exposure to the price movement of the underlying asset without owning the asset. Their value is “derived” from the performance of the underlying asset or from an economic indicator – such as interest rates or an index – that they might be tied to. The underlying asset can be equity- or debt-based. The underlying asset can also be another derivative, or a derivative of a derivative, which adds more complexity to the valuation of the derivative.  
       
       
         Examples of derivatives are (i) forward commitments such as  
         
           futures 
         
         ,  
         
           forwards 
         
         , and  
         
           swaps 
         
          or (ii) contingent claims such as  
         
           options 
         
         ,  
         
           credit derivatives 
         
          such as credit default swaps, and  
         
           asset-backed securities 
         
         . 
         
           
             
               69 
             
           
         
          Traditionally, derivatives were not traded on a stock exchange, but off-exchange (aka “over the counter”). Exchange-traded derivatives are a relatively new product class in traditional financial markets, and have increasingly been traded over specialized derivatives exchanges or traditional stock exchanges since the early 2000s. Derivatives have also become an important financial instrument in an increasingly tokenized economy.  
       
       
         Smart contracts can be programmed to contain all the terms and margin requirements of the bilateral agreement between the counterparties relevant to a derivative contract. They can be designed to be triggered by information from the outside world, usually via price feed oracles that give information about the reference asset underlying the derivatives contract. Outside events can also be triggered by automated market makers. As everyone has their own opinion of what the current rate for an interest rate swap should be, one can create an automated market maker that reflects that sentiment and lets people trade on interest rates rather than on asset prices. 
       
       
          The first crypto futures were Bitcoin futures. They emerged in 2011 on an online exchange called “ 
         
           ICBIT 
         
         .” They were often individually negotiated and issued over online chat channels such as “ 
         
           IRC 
         
         ,” but the demand for these futures was not high, since adoption of Bitcoin tokens as the underlying asset was quite low. As more and more mainstream investors – both retail and institutional – flocked to the crypto space, the demand for derivatives that relied on these underlying asset classes also grew. As a result, an increasing array of derivative products were designed for this new market. In 2014, “ 
         
           BitMEX 
         
         ” introduced a range of derivative products, and the concept of “perpetual swaps.” Perpetual swaps had been around since the 1990s, but were never really used in financial markets until BitMEX adapted the concept for crypto. Perpetual swaps have since become a dominant crypto derivative class that is traded on many exchanges today. In 2017, “ 
         
           Deribit 
         
         ” offered the first BTC options on the market, and that same year, “ 
         
           LedgerX 
         
         ” became the first regulated clearing house and swap execution facility (SEF) that listed and cleared fully-collateralized, physically-settled digital currency swaps and options. In 2019, the Chicago Board Options Exchange (CBOE) as well as the Chicago Mercantile Exchange & Chicago Board of Trade (CME) started to offer Bitcoin futures. This was quite a remarkable turn of events, as it provided investors with a regulated marketplace on which they could trade these derivatives. It gave the whole asset class a new sense of legitimacy, which drove many more investors into the crypto market and created another bull run on the underlying asset and its derivatives. Throughout 2019, a range of specialized crypto derivatives exchanges such as “ 
         
           Quedex 
         
         ” or “ 
         
           Bakkt 
         
         ” were launched. The demand for crypto futures was additionally spurred by the COVID-19 pandemic of 2020, and a rapidly evolving DeFi ecosystem that was being built on top of the Ethereum network. Both events drove even more investors into crypto markets in general. As a result, BTC, ETH and other crypto assets soared to new all-time highs, which further drove investors’ demand for derivatives.  
       
       
         Eventually, even centralized exchanges who had predominantly focused on providing a market for instant trading at the current market price (also referred to as “spot trading”) started to offer derivative products – such as “ 
         
           Binance 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           OKEx 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Huobi 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Bit.com 
         
         ,” and “ 
         
           FTX 
         
         .” “ 
         
           Crypto.com 
         
         ” – one of the biggest and longest standing spot exchanges – even launched their own derivatives exchange in 2021. This started to attract more traditional financial players into the tokenized derivatives market. By early 2022, the trading activity of the crypto derivatives market outperformed the spot or cash market for the first time. When regulators in the United States and Europe started to introduce more specific regulation for token exchanges, many exchanges that specialized in derivative trading relocated to offshore jurisdictions. Asia, and in particular Singapore, became a new hub for crypto derivatives and the – predominantly centralized – service providers who offered these derivatives. These offshore derivatives exchanges drove most of the product innovation around tokenized derivatives, because they were less explicitly regulated or not regulated at all. They offered faster and easier account creation and more trading leverage options. While “offshore” derivatives exchanges drove a lot of product innovation in this emerging DeFi space, they also provided higher counterparty risks for institutional investors and private investors alike. Among many serious players who were active in the space, more corrupt players took advantage of the loose regulatory oversight in some of these offshore jurisdictions – among others, the now infamous derivatives exchange “ 
         
           FTX 
         
         ” which, at the time of writing, had to file for bankruptcy and is alleged to have intentionally misappropriated customer funds.  
       
       
         
         
         Types of Derivatives & Perpetual Contracts 
       
       
         Derivatives are versatile financial instruments which can be designed to lock the buyer in a contractual relationship at some future date, as is the case with traditional derivatives, including futures, forwards and swaps. Alternatively, derivatives can be designed to offer the right to enter the contract under pre-specified terms, without an obligation to do so, as is the case with options. The terms and conditions of a derivative contract can be adapted to the specific needs of different buyers and sellers of the contract. The contracts can be designed to be more risky or less risky. They may also be designed with stronger or weaker leverage effects in mind. Derivatives can be used for speculation purposes, or to hedge against a risky position – i.e. to offset the financial risk attached to a potential future event. When derivatives are used for hedging, they enable a personalized insurance position.  
       
       
         Tokenized futures 
         : Futures are purchasing agreements between sellers and buyers of an underlying asset, set at a specific future date (the "expiry date”) with a predetermined price (the “strike price”). This means that when the contract expires, the buyer is contractually obligated to buy the underlying asset, and the seller is contractually obligated to deliver the asset, once the price has been paid. The contract duration can last anything from a week, a month, a quarter and potentially longer periods. Betting on increasing prices is referred to as “going long;” betting on decreasing prices is referred to as “going short.” Investors who go long make a profit if the market price of the underlying asset is higher than the defined price at the expiry date, but take a loss when the opposite is true – and vice versa. As an example, assume that the current price of Bitcoin is at 25,000 EUR, and futures are sold for ending in one month with a 28,000 EUR strike price. A futures buyer can now speculate that the actual BTC price in one month will be higher than 28,000. In the same way, they could sell the futures contract if they expect the BTC price to be lower than 28,000 EUR. If their forecast is correct, they will make a profit of the difference from the strike price to the actual price (minus fees). Futures markets usually have more trading activity and are therefore more liquid than the corresponding spot market of their underlying assets. As a result, the futures market often fulfills a price discovery function for the underlying assets. Because of this property, it is expected that crypto derivatives in general, and crypto futures in particular, may have a stabilizing effect on the volatility of crypto asset prices. Tokenized futures are often traded “on margin,” which means that the buyer does not have to pay for the full amount of the trade themselves. A portion of the buying price can be borrowed from the exchange or trading platform, which allows for so-called “leverage.” This can amplify both gains and losses. 
         
           
             
               70 
             
           
         
           
       
       
         Perpetual futures 
          (aka “perpetual contracts” or “perpetual swaps”) are a special type of futures contract. They were first used by “ 
         
           BitMEX 
         
         ” in 2016, and have since become a popular instrument in DeFi. Perpetual futures do not have an expiry date. Investors can hold a perpetual futures contract for as long as they wish, until they close their future position or are forced into liquidation due to “margin issues.” 
         
           
             
               71 
             
           
         
          The potential gains or losses of the contract holder are determined by the difference between the contract price and spot price of the underlying token. This difference is also referred to as the “funding rate.” The term “perpetual” alludes to the fact that perpetual contracts do not expire as long as the investor pays the holding fee (aka funding rate) and holds a mandatory minimum of tokens (aka the margin). Perpetual contracts are similar to “ 
         
           contracts for difference 
         
         ” in traditional finance. Decentralized solutions for perpetual contracts have been developed by “ 
         
           GMX 
         
         ” and “ 
         
           dYdX 
         
         .” 
       
       
         Tokenized options 
         : Similar to futures, options are a type of derivative contract that allows an investor to buy or sell assets at a future date. However, option contracts do not contractually oblige, but only give a contractual opportunity to buy or sell an underlying asset at a future date. Option holders have to pay a fee (aka “premium”) to acquire the option, on top of the price the asset might cost. This fee will have to be paid regardless of the outcome of the trade. If option holders decide not to exercise the contract upon expiry of the contract, they lose the premium they paid to acquire the option. Options have also become a popular derivative instrument for crypto assets. At the time of writing this book, the most important underlying asset classes are BTC and ETH. Their options can either be pegged to real Bitcoin or Ethereum tokens. Alternatively, crypto options are often also pegged to underlying crypto futures such as BTC futures or ETH futures. In general, there are various types of options: options to buy (Call Option), options to sell (Put Options), options to buy or sell before the contract’s expiry date (American Options) or options to buy or sell on the exact agreed date (European Options). Depending on where the current market price of the underlying asset stands in relation to the strike price of the option, options are referred to as at-the-money, in-the-money, or out-of-the-money. 
       
       
         Swaps 
          are agreements which are mostly used by companies or financial institutions. They can be used to speculate on changes in the expected direction of underlying asset prices or on the interest rate one has to pay, and can also be used to hedge certain market risks, such as future changes to interest rates. For example, mortgage holders who are unhappy with the terms of their mortgage can enter swap agreements to change the interest rate or terms paid on their mortgage without having to renegotiate the terms with their mortgage lenders. If one party has a fixed interest rate, and the other party has a floating interest rate, and both parties would prefer the terms of the counterparty, they could set up a customized swap contract. The term “swap” in finance should not be confused with the technical term of “swap” or “swapping” in and between blockchain protocols, which refers to the technical exchange of tokens, wallet to wallet, without a third party intermediary, only using blockchain protocols and automated contracts to enforce the trade. Financial swaps were used for the first time by IBM and the World Bank in 1981 and have become one of the most traded instruments in traditional financial markets in recent decades. Interest rates and currency swaps were the first type of swaps to be traded, initially only “over the counter” and not on standardized exchanges, since they were tailor-made for the exact needs of the counterparties to a particular swap contract. Over the years, swaps have become much more regulated and standardized and are subject to regulatory reporting. Currency swaps and interest rate swaps were the most dominant type of swaps. In the early 1990s, new types of swap instruments appeared, such as the “credit default swap (CDS),” which became popular after the year 2000. The seller of a CDS compensates the buyer of the swap (the creditor of the reference loan) in the event of a loan default by the original debtor. The buyer of a CDS pays a fee to the seller in a series of cash payments, in exchange for a future payoff in a “credit event” – if the lender fails to repay the loan, or for other restructuring reasons. CDS were not traded on exchanges and did not require financial reporting. This lack of transparency coupled with high systemic risk of the instrument led to a series of financial crises from  
         
           2007–2010 
         
         . Crypto-swaps are harder to construct for various reasons. They are usually specific agreements between counterparties, and thus not easy to standardize. Many of the potential underlying agreements – such as default swaps, interest swaps, and cash flow swaps – are not yet fully developed in crypto  (maybe with the exception of interest rate swaps). Furthermore, contractual agreements need to be tradable positions to use them in crypto, since one would need to lock up both counterparty tokens to a swap. Financial swaps are mostly conducted on margin. That means one doesn’t provide the entire value, but only margin up to what is at risk, which is also a challenge due to the nature of blockchain networks and the dynamics of DeFi markets. 
       
       
         Blockchain networks themselves provide new asset classes that can be used to create derivative products. Dmitriy Berenzon argues  
         
           in his blog post from 2019 
         
         
           
             
               72 
             
           
         
          that services such as “ 
         
           BitOoda 
         
         ” have been working on structuring derivative products: “Bitcoin Difficulty Swaps,” and “Hash-Power Swaps” that are settled via “Hash-Power Weekly Extendable Contracts.” Other interesting derivatives aim to hedge the energy risk of miners, such as “Electricity Futures,” or are  tailored towards DeFi products, such as “ Staking Yield Swaps,” “Slashing Penalty Swaps,” “Stability Fee Swaps,” “Airdrop Options,” or “Lockdrop Forwards.” 
       
       
         Tokenized insurances 
         : Insurance contracts can also be seen as a type of financial derivative. However, the provision of insurance contracts has developed into a separate and highly specialized financial service industry that offers a range of standardized hedging positions for individuals and institutions alike. Unlike derivatives – which are often negotiated over the counter and tailored to one's personal risk profile – insurance products can be bought “off the shelf” from a range of service providers. These providers usually offer standardized products meeting different risk profiles, from which individuals and institutions can select. In a growing DeFi ecosystem, tokenized insurances seem to be a logical next step of tokenization which could potentially (i) reduce the operating costs and associated management fees due to immediate settlement and automatic yield distribution, (ii) provide much more transparency and accountability between all stakeholders in the industry, which might result in fairer pricing structures for insurance consumers, (iii) provide more liquidity for all industry stakeholders, and (iv) allow for more customizable insurance products, which would not be feasible within the current legacy systems the insurance industry operates on. However, tokenized insurance services also require considerable network effects to fall into place. A critical mass of consumer products and institutions first need to transition to Web3-based infrastructure. I assume that tokenized insurance will only become mainstream once most households and institutions use Web3 wallets on a regular basis, holding and swapping tokenized crypto assets, which – at the time of writing this book – is far from being the case. 
       
       
         
         
         Purpose of Derivatives 
       
       
         Derivatives can be used for a number of purposes, from generating liquidity in a market or getting access to otherwise hard-to-trade assets or markets, to insuring against price movements (hedging), to increasing exposure to price movements for speculative reasons. 
       
       
         Liquidity 
         : Just like in traditional finance, tokenized derivatives are an important tool for increasing liquidity. They often increase the supply and demand for underlying securities, thereby making them more easily available for buyers and sellers – and less susceptible to price spikes, as they increase market depth for the underlying security. Liquidity is also an issue in DeFi, and was a particularly critical issue in the early days of crypto assets. A liquid market contributes to lower transaction costs, lower slippage, and creates more favorable market conditions that attract more investors.  
       
       
         Diversification 
         : Investors can diversify their investment portfolios and access asset classes that were previously hard to access because of market dynamics and potential market barriers. Due to the nature of blockchain networks, tokenization can lessen smallhold investors’ barriers to entry for diversifying their investment portfolio. 
       
       
         Hedging 
         : Derivatives can be used to protect from unexpected market risk, which is generally referred to as “hedging.” It allows investors to open positions that negatively correlate with existing positions in one's portfolio in an attempt to mitigate potential losses. In the light of the high volatility of certain crypto asset prices, derivatives seem to be an important instrument. Hedging is considered a more proactive strategy in a crypto bear market, as opposed to waiting for the price to recover, or worse, being forced to liquidate holdings at an unfavorable price. 
       
       
         Speculation 
         : Derivatives can also be used for betting on the asset price, such as speculating about a future price of a crypto currency or other crypto asset. An investor can speculate that a price will increase or decrease, and open respective positions by entering into either a long or short trade. While the rewards can be high, the risks can be just as high, which is why these instruments are rarely used by retail investors who do not understand the complexities of financial markets. Speculation in a DeFi environment can amplify potential risks and rewards, since crypto options and crypto futures often use leverage. 
       
       
         Leverage 
          is an important aspect in the context of derivatives in general, and crypto derivatives in particular. The fact that investors can leverage a trade makes derivatives particularly attractive to many investors – leverage gives exposure to an underlying asset at a fraction of the cost one would have to pay without leverage. Crypto derivatives exchanges have allowed investors particularly high levels of leverage, up to as much as 125 times the initial margin. As a result, even small price increases in the underlying asset can result in large returns on one's investment – but large losses are also possible. 
         
           
             
               73 
             
           
         
       
       
         Price discovery 
         : One important function of a marketplace is price discovery, which refers to the process of being able to determine the current price of any asset. The reliability of price prediction usually depends on a range of factors such as market structure, liquidity and the accessibility of reliable information, resulting in a more efficient market. In traditional finance, a strong derivatives market is thought to help predict market risk, and to allow educated investors to take the appropriate positions, such as buying options to protect their investment portfolios. This creates smoother market adjustments that are less susceptible to single events such as a single buy or sell offer. The same is true for crypto derivatives.  
         
           
             
               74 
             
           
         
       
       
         
         
         Synthetics 
       
       
         Synthetics are a special type of derivative that simulates the risk and reward profile of other financial instruments, usually by combining a set of other financial instruments such as one or more derivatives, which – in combination – result in the same expected profit as investing in the underlying security or derivative. While simple financial derivatives allow investors to create various different positions without needing to use as much capital, financial synthetics are a way to mimic a financial position with a different strategy. They are reverse engineered to recreate the cash flow of any type of security or other derivative, by simulating other instruments while altering key characteristics, like duration and cash flow. Combining a long call and a short put can be synthetically the same thing as being long on a stock, with the advantage of requiring less capital than holding the stock and potentially creating more leverage. Synthetics are often used to create liquidity for an asset, or to provide easier access to asset classes that one would not otherwise have easy access to. Buying a synthetic product over buying or shorting the underlying security has advantages: one does not need to borrow a security if one wants to sell it short, or worry about potential dividend payments on the shorted security. The development of synthetic products is mostly only possible if there is a critical mass of liquidity in the underlying asset. If the underlying asset is too illiquid, the potential economic benefits of a synthetic product built on top of it are reduced. 
         
           
             
               75 
             
           
         
           
       
       
         Many token use cases presented in this book are derivatives by nature. Derivatives that are issued based on staked tokens, such as Staked Ether (sETH), are one example for crypto-native derivatives that have emerged in Web3. Asset-backed tokens that don’t constitute securities or currencies can also be considered a financial derivative, similar to an "asset-backed security" (ABS) in traditional finance (such as consumer-backed products like consumer loans, credit cards or car loans). With tokenized real-world assets and obligations such as tokenized mortgages, the token representing a real-world asset or obligation can be traded, while the underlying assets and obligations are held in escrow by a smart contract or in the custody of a third party service provider (as is the case for stable tokens, tokenized art and tokenized real estate). The collateralized debt positions of stable tokens such as DAI can be considered a form of  
         
           collateralized debt obligations 
         
          (CDOs), while tokenized mortgages could be seen as a form of  
         
           collateralized mortgage obligations 
         
          (CMO).  
       
       
         Expanding the asset base 
         : As more crypto-native assets emerge, so will the synthetic products that build on top of them. Smart contracts can be used to create publicly verifiable tokenized debt positions for any type of underlying real-world asset (RWA) in a trustless manner. In fact, tokenization provides a Web3-based synthetics mechanism for real-world assets, where the tokenized synthetics can be traded and settled over a public and permissionless blockchain network. A whole range of asset classes beyond currencies, commodities, stocks or bonds can be tokenized that so far have been hard to access and trade, and more sophisticated tokenized synthetic positions can be created on top of these real-world assets. One could use tokenization to either create a simple fiat-collateralized stable token such as Tether, or commodity-collateralized token such as gold tokens. A USD stable token is a product engineered to mimic the U.S. Dollar. It might deviate from the underlying product if it is not engineered perfectly, but in essence, one is trading a synthetic version of the U.S. Dollar. Alternatively, smart contracts could be used to create a synthetic price exposure to a fiat currency such as USD without having to hold the actual asset in custody with a centralized counterparty. DAI is a good example for such synthetic price exposure. For many investors and users of stable tokens, reliable and sustainable price exposure is good enough, no matter the collateralization mechanism. The use case of DAI is analyzed in detail in an earlier chapter of this book (“Stable Tokens”) and in even more detail in another book of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.”  
       
       
         Interoperability solutions 
         : Some synthetic products such as “wrapped ETH” are synthetic financial instruments that were initially created to resolve a major technical challenge of Web3 networks: transaction interoperability between blockchain networks in the absence of intermediary institutions. As long as secure cross-chain communication is not fully resolved, the availability of tokens will be limited to tokens that are compatible with the blockchain network on which the DeFi application operates. This creates lock-in effects, and centralization effects. So called “wrapped tokens” provide synthetic price exposure to tokens of another blockchain infrastructure, without forcing investors to hold the token directly on the network of the DeFi application used. 
       
       
         Liquidity solutions 
         : The combination of tokenized synthetics of real-world assets with DeFi protocols can create a more liquid market for these assets than was possible in the off-chain world. Examples were discussed in an earlier chapter, “Money with Attributes: Asset Tokens and NFTs.” Simple fractionalization of the property rights to these tokenized asset classes can already provide more market liquidity for the assets themselves. More sophisticated derivative products will do the rest. But liquidity is somewhat of a catch-22. While the underlying asset needs a minimum viable market size and liquidity to make derivative products in general and synthetics in particular attractive, synthetic tokens can create more liquidity for the underlying asset class. An increasing number of synthetic protocols are emerging, with the main promise to provide “infinite liquidity” for their many tokenization use cases and the DeFi ecosystem as a whole. 
       
       
         Inclusion 
         : In the traditional financial industry, synthetics have only been available to institutional investors and sophisticated investors. DeFi protocols specializing in synthetic products potentially allow retail investors to access the benefits of holding synthetic financial products. However, inclusion also requires a minimum level of financial literacy, so investors are able to make educated investments decisions, instead of using tokenized synthetics to participate in some form of roulette-like casino capitalism, and suffer the collateral damage of losing all their funds because they had problems appropriately assessing the associated risks. 
       
       
         The history of crypto synthetics started around 2014 and was pioneered by “ 
         
           Abra 
         
         .” Investors could deposit funds into their wallet, which would immediately convert to BTC, but would be displayed in their USD value in their wallet application. The protocol provided a form of crypto-collateralized stable tokens by maintaining the BTC/USD peg to guarantee that inventors have the right to redeem their deposited funds, regardless of the price fluctuations of either BTC or USD. Around the same time, the “DAI” stable token emerged, which is probably the most widely known and used crypto synthetic to date. As described in a previous chapter titled “Stable Tokens,” in order to create DAI, users need to lock ETH as collateral in a smart contract. These smart contracts were initially referred to as a “collateralized debt position” and are now referred to as a “vaults.” Once the collateral assets are deposited in the smart contract, newly minted synthetic assets – DAI stable tokens – can be withdrawn. The mechanism is designed to maintain a soft peg to USD, which means that DAI token holders receive synthetic price exposure to the U.S. Dollar without actually holding USD. Recovering collateralized ETH or other collateral tokens required the DAI holders to repay the DAI loan and the interest for taking out the DAI. The “ 
         
           MARKET Protocol 
         
         ” was another early project that allowed users to create synthetic assets which they referred to as “Position Tokens,” and that could track the price of any reference asset via oracles. These position tokens could provide bounded long and short exposure to the underlying assets and offer a payoff structure similar to that of a “bull call spread” in traditional finance. Another interesting early derivative project was “ 
         
           UMA 
         
         ,” which focused on issuing “Total Return Swaps” which could provide synthetic exposure to a wide variety of asset types, with market mechanisms they referred to as “provably honest oracle.” Any two parties could establish derivative token contracts based on their specific needs using the UMA protocol. “ 
         
           Synthetix 
         
         ” is another issuance protocol and exchange for a range of synthetic assets. The “ 
         
           Rainbow Network 
         
         ” is a DeFi protocol that works with state channels and allows nesting synthetics along with other assets within a payment channel. It also allows tokenized versions of “contracts for difference” and “total return swaps.” At the time of writing, some other interesting new players in this space are ” 
         
           Alchemix 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           dYdX 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           FRIKTION 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Injective 
         
         ,” “ 
         
           Kepp3r 
         
         ,” or “ 
         
           Ribbon Finance 
         
         .” 
       
       
         
         
         Challenges & Outlook 
       
       
         Derivatives in general, and synthetics in particular, allow investors to trade virtually any type of asset beyond currencies, commodities, stocks or bonds. They play an important role in traditional financial markets and are becoming a key component of DeFi. Tokenization in general allows the frictional transfer of any type of asset that can be technically swapped  
         – 
          wallet 
         - 
         to 
         - 
         wallet. Tokenized derivatives will create a fertile breeding ground for the tokenization of more and more  
         real-world 
          assets and  
         real-world 
          liabilities. As a result, any retail investor will be able to frictionlessly move between different types of currencies, commodities, stocks, art or real estate 
         , 
          without having to hold the underlying asset.  
         Crypto synthetics have the power to convert any real-world asset into a tokenized asset with higher availability and liquidity that can easily be converted into bankable funds, aka money. While the possibilities of tokenization in general and tokenized derivatives in particular are large, so are the related challenges: 
       
       
         Counterparty risk 
         : In the world of traditional finance it is the role of clearing firms to manage all post-transaction management of derivative products and mitigate counterparty risk by ensuring that transactions on an exchange will settle. In smart-contract-based systems, and in the absence of human-managed clearing institutions, potential counterparty risk needs to be mitigated through preventive mechanisms. Such mechanisms needed to be developed for the use case of derivatives that are executed via smart contracts. They are generally referred to as “auto-liquidations.” Auto-liquidations are usually triggered when the price of the underlying assets meets certain predefined critical targets. These critical targets are defined in the protocol to prevent investors from having insufficient funds to keep the trade open because of a sudden drop in price of the underlying asset. However, the design of resilient auto-liquidation mechanisms needs to be refined and is one of the biggest design challenges for various derivative products such as stable tokens. MakerDAO , for example, is a stable token that uses auto-liquidations – DAI vaults are liquidated before they turn under-collateralized. If the price of the collateral assets held in the vaults drops under a critical threshold, market players (so-called “Keepers”) – who are incentivized with potential arbitrage opportunities to monitor the collateralization ratio of all vaults – can trigger a liquidation of a DAI vault if the DAI debt is not sufficiently collateralized anymore due to exchange rate drops. (An in-depth analysis of DAI and MakerDAO can be found in a dedicated chapter in another volume of the Token Economy Series titled “DAOs & Purpose-Driven Tokens.”)  
       
       
         Regulatory aspects 
         : Derivatives and synthetics are financial instruments that are differently regulated depending on the jurisdiction and which might be illegal in certain jurisdictions. This needs to be considered when offering such products over a globally accessible infrastructure. While tokenized derivatives that are issued and settled on public and permissionless infrastructure resolve these transparency issues, complexities related to these financial products can create an environment of quasi-intransparency in an ocean of available DeFi trading data. 
       
       
         Financial education & financial discipline 
         : In the history of traditional finance, the recent rise of derivatives, and especially synthetics, has created elevated money flows into financial markets that have surpassed the market activity of the real economy by orders of magnitude. Events such as the financial crisis of 2007-2009 suggest that the issuers and buyers of derivatives in general, and synthetics in particular, are not as well-informed as one would expect from financial market experts and professional financial market institutions. If even professional and institutional investors cannot manage to see through the complexities of entangled and interrelated financial products, how should retail investors break even when they invest part-time and do not have the level of market and industry know-how of professional investors? To understand the potentials and risks of tokenized derivatives, it is important to have a general understanding of how financial markets work. This would not only create a healthier environment for making sound investment decisions, but would also generate a more sustainable development environment for tokenized use cases.  
       
       
         Market failures 
         : Just as in traditional financial markets, crypto derivatives are complex financial instruments typically used by advanced traders that require financial education, market knowledge, skill, and considerable financial resources. Blindly investing in complex interconnected financial products in a bull market can result in market crashes and bank runs. The financial crisis of 2009 and a series of crypto-market crashes in 2022 are a testament to this.  
       
       
         Trading bots, high frequency trading & systemic risks 
         : While tokenization allows to create a derivative from almost all financial and economic activity, return, and position – including derivatives of derivatives and even more complex synthetic products – the levels of attraction are magnified in comparison to today’s financial markets. It is safe to say that the higher the attraction level of a financial product, and the more derivative products build on top of that financial product, the higher the interdependencies and systemic risk of the whole market interacting with that financial product.  
       
       The access to AI and machine learning tools has helped institutional investors to somewhat manage these complexities, which is why trading bots have become a new player in this derivatized financial market. Private investors who don’t have their own trading bot won't be able to engage in high frequency trading – so far this is only accessible to financial institutions and tech- or finance-savvy individuals. If and when open source AI tools are available, they could provide personalized trading services to investors, and make financial markets more democratic so that retail investors also have a chance to assess the systemic risks involved in trading a tokenized derivative. Until then, the claims of decentralization and inclusion in DeFi should be taken with a grain of salt. 
       
         
         
         Chapter Summary 
       
       
         
         Derivatives are versatile financial instruments which can be designed to lock the buyer in a contractual relationship at some future date, as is the case with traditional derivatives, including futures, forwards and swaps. Alternatively, derivatives can be designed to offer the right to enter the contract under pre-specified terms, without an obligation to do so, as is the case with options. The terms and conditions of a derivative contract can be adapted to the specific needs of different buyers and sellers of the contract. The contracts can be designed to be more risky or less risky. They may also be designed with stronger or weaker leverage effects in mind. ~ Derivatives can be used for a number of purposes, from generating liquidity in a market or getting access to otherwise hard-to-trade assets or markets, to insuring against price movements (hedging), to increasing exposure to price movements for speculative reasons. ~ Derivatives have also become an important financial instrument in an increasingly tokenized economy. Smart contracts can be programmed to contain all the terms and margin requirements of the bilateral agreement between the counterparties relevant to a derivative contract. They can be designed to be triggered by information from the outside world, usually via price feed oracles that give information about the reference asset underlying the derivatives contract. Outside events can also be triggered by automated market makers.  ~ Just like in traditional finance, tokenized derivatives are an important tool for increasing liquidity. They often increase the supply and demand for underlying securities, thereby making them more easily available for buyers and sellers – and less susceptible to price spikes, as they increase market depth for the underlying security. Liquidity is also an issue in DeFi, and was a particularly critical issue in the early days of crypto assets. A liquid market contributes to lower transaction costs, lower slippage, and creates more favorable market conditions that attract more investors.  ~ Investors can use derivatives to diversify their investment portfolios and access asset classes that were previously hard to access because of market dynamics and potential market barriers. Due to the nature of blockchain networks, tokenization can lessen smallhold investors’ barriers to entry for diversifying their investment portfolio. ~ Derivatives can be used to protect from unexpected market risk, which is generally referred to as “hedging.” It allows investors to open positions that negatively correlate with existing positions in one's portfolio in an attempt to mitigate potential losses. In the light of the high volatility of certain crypto asset prices, derivatives seem to be an important instrument. ~ Derivatives can also be used for betting on the asset price, such as speculating about a future price of a crypto currency or other crypto asset. An investor can speculate that a price will increase or decrease, and open respective positions by entering into either a long or short trade. While the rewards can be high, the risks can be just as high, which is why these instruments are rarely used by retail investors who do not understand the complexities of financial markets. Speculation in a DeFi environment can amplify potential risks and rewards, since crypto options and crypto futures often use leverage. ~ Leverage is an important aspect in the context of derivatives in general, and crypto derivatives in particular. The fact that investors can leverage a trade makes derivatives particularly attractive to many investors – leverage gives exposure to an underlying asset at a fraction of the cost one would have to pay without leverage. Crypto derivatives exchanges have allowed investors particularly high levels of leverage, up to as much as 125 times the initial margin. As a result, even small price increases in the underlying asset can result in large returns on one's investment – but large losses are also possible. ~ In traditional finance, a strong derivatives market is thought to help predict market risk, and to allow educated investors to take the appropriate positions, such as buying options to protect their investment portfolios. This creates smoother market adjustments that are less susceptible to single events such as a single buy or sell offer. The same is true for crypto derivatives. ~ Synthetics are a special type of derivative that simulates the risk and reward profile of other financial instruments, usually by combining a set of other financial instruments such as one or more derivatives, which – in combination – result in the same expected profit as investing in the underlying security or derivative. While simple financial derivatives allow investors to create various different positions without needing to use as much capital, financial synthetics are a way to mimic a financial position with a different strategy. They are reverse engineered to recreate the cash flow of any type of security or other derivative, by simulating other instruments while altering key characteristics, like duration and cash flow. Many token use cases presented in this book are derivatives by nature.  ~ Tokenization has the potential to expand the asset base for financial derivatives, overcome interoperability issues of blockchain networks, provide more inclusive financial products, and create more liquid markets for underlying assets and financial products. 
       
       Private investors who don’t have their own trading bot won‘t be able to engage in high frequency trading – so far this is only accessible to financial institutions and tech- or finance-savvy individu- als. If and when open source AI tools are available, they could provide personalized trading services to investors, and make financial markets more democratic so that retail investors also have a chance to assess the systemic risks involved in trading a tokenized derivative. Until then, the claims of decentralization and inclusion in DeFi should be taken with a grain of salt.
